# Front Cover

The image displays the exterior of the manuscript. The volume is bound in decorative **marbled paper** featuring a "stone marble" pattern. This artistic style was created by floating oil-based pigments—in this case, pale blues, light greys, and reddish-brown sienna—on a liquid surface and then dipping the paper to capture the swirling, organic design. 

On the left edge, near the spine, there are remnants of a small, worn paper label containing fragmentary text:

### The
# Drawn Curtain
<note>original: "Aufgezogne Vorhang." In the context of 18th-century literature, a "drawn curtain" or "unveiled" title signified a work intended to reveal secrets or hidden knowledge to the public.</note>
### of
# Freemasonry
<term>Freemasonry: a fraternal organization that evolved from the guilds of stonemasons and became a central focus of Enlightenment-era social and philosophical debate.</term>
## by means of the only true
## history thereof

<image-desc>A detailed engraving of a masonic building site. On the left, under a rustic wooden shed, two stonemasons work on large rectangular blocks; one uses a mallet and chisel. In the center, several men in 18th-century coats and hats are gathered. On the ground in the foreground lies a dark rectangular slab or tombstone featuring a skull and crossbones and the Roman numeral "XI". In the background, there is a circular domed structure (monopteros) surmounted by a shining star. The scene is signed "Berndt made this in Frankfurt" <note>original Latin: "Berndt fecit Francof:"</note> in the lower right corner of the frame.</image-desc>

## Frankfurt am Main
## published by Gebhard and Körber 1790.

<summary>This is the title page of a 1790 German book claiming to provide the "only true history" of Freemasonry, a common theme in the era's literature of disclosure. The accompanying engraving depicts both the literal craft of stonemasonry and the symbolic architecture associated with the Masonic order.</summary>

# Preface.

If various reviewers, following the <term>laudable traditional fashion</term> <note>The author uses "laudable" (löblich) sarcastically here to mock the predictable habits of contemporary literary critics.</note>, would like to review my book—and, what is nowadays almost one and the same, decry it—without taking the trouble to read it themselves: then, after making a start with a few <term>commonplaces</term> <note>Clichés or predictable arguments.</note> (for example, that much useless material has already been written about <term>Masonry</term> <note>original: Maurerey; refers to Freemasonry.</note>), they need only humbly add: The author has cobbled his material together from wretched books, and even where he has happened upon a good book, he understands everything incorrectly. He falsely claims to be a <term>Freemason</term>, but is probably a secret <term>Jesuit</term> <note>A common 18th-century conspiracy theory suggested that Jesuits had secretly infiltrated Freemasonry to further the interests of the Catholic Church.</note>, or at least a <term>dry staff</term> <note>An idiomatic expression for a lifeless tool or a pawn being used by others.</note> in the hands of the Jesuits, from which one can easily draw further conclusions.

<summary>In this satirical opening, the author anticipates and mocks the knee-jerk reactions of critics who might dismiss his work on Freemasonry as unoriginal or as secret Jesuit propaganda without reading it.</summary>
<keywords>Freemasonry, Jesuits, literary criticism, 18th-century satire, conspiracy theories, book reviewers</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the preface's opening where the author mocks lazy critics, this page addresses those who review books based on professional or institutional biases rather than the text itself. Catchword: Noch</meta>

Those gentlemen who undertake the laborious task of leafing through my book will perhaps encounter passages that do not conform to the principles which they, as contributors to certain scholarly periodicals, are obliged to follow in their <term>reviews</term> <note>original: Recensionen; an archaic spelling of Rezensionen. In the 18th century, "learned journals" often had specific ideological or theological agendas.</note>. To extract themselves from any such embarrassment, the safest course of action is for them not to announce my book at all, so that at least a large portion of the <term>public</term> <note>original: Publikum.</note> remains unaware of its existence. Silence in this case is better than ripping passages out of context or twisting them. For even that might entice some people to examine the book for themselves.

To those, however, who wish to read it in its entirety and subject it to a rigorous and honest <term>examination</term> <note>original: Prüfung.</note>, I have nothing further to say except that they place me under an obligation; and however their <term>judgment</term> <note>original: Urtheil; an archaic spelling of Urteil.</note> may turn out, they will certainly be of service to the <term>truth</term> <note>original: Wahrheit.</note>.

<summary>The author challenges biased reviewers to either ignore his work entirely or engage with it honestly, asserting that a thorough and fair critique—regardless of its verdict—serves the ultimate goal of truth.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, scholarly journals, intellectual honesty, book reviews, 18th-century publishing, pursuit of truth</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page follows the author's preface where he challenged critics and reviewers. The catchword from the previous page was "Still" (German: "Noch").</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut horizontal divider consisting of symmetrical floral vines and flower blossoms.</image-desc>

---

Still today, not only the non-Masonic public but also the Masonic one gropes around in the dark. Men who have passed through all degrees and are familiar with every branch of Masonry still do not know what purpose Masonry has, or what its <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>original: Hieroglyphen; in Masonic tradition, this refers to the symbolic emblems and visual allegories used in rituals, such as the square and compass.</note> are supposed to mean.

The reason for this lies in an unfamiliarity with history, primarily that of the origin of Masonry. That which should have been remembered above all else was forgotten; and thus, even in the most recent times, one was forced to raise questions through which one made oneself appear ridiculous to the <term>profane</term> <note>original: Profanen; the standard Masonic term for the uninitiated or the general public.</note>: From whence did we come? And what is our intention?

The fact that this history was not sufficiently pursued was due to the delusion that everything in Masonry must be a <term>secret</term> <note>original: Geheimniß.</note>; and that the study of it was, in part, not recommended,

<sig>A</sig>
<meta>catchword: partly</meta>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's preface. However, this section shifts focus to address the author's critics and the scholarly reviewers of his time.</meta>

Those gentlemen who undertake the laborious task of **leafing through** my book will perhaps encounter passages that are not in accordance with the principles which they, as contributors to certain <term>scholarly journals</term> <note>original: gelehrten periodischen Schriften; these were the academic and literary periodicals of the Enlightenment era where new books were formally reviewed and debated.</note>, must follow in their <term>reviews</term> <note>original: Recensionen; formal critical evaluations.</note>. To extricate themselves from all embarrassment, the safest course is for them not to announce my book at all, so that at least a large portion of the public learns nothing of its existence. Silence here is better than tearing passages out of context or twisting them. For even this might entice some people to check the book for themselves.

To those, however, who wish to read the work in its entirety and subject it to a careful and honest examination, I have nothing further to say than that they place me under an obligation <note>original: verbinden; a formal way of saying they do the author a great favor or make him beholden to them through their serious attention.</note> by doing so; and however their judgment may turn out, they will certainly do a service to the truth.

<summary>The author addresses potential critics, suggesting that biased reviewers should remain silent rather than misrepresent his work, while expressing gratitude to those who read and critique it honestly.</summary>
<keywords>literary criticism, scholarly journals, book reviews, intellectual honesty, public reception, pursuit of truth</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>Continuity: While the previous page addressed the author's critics and scholarly reviewers, this page shifts focus to the core problem the book aims to address: the widespread ignorance regarding the history and purpose of Freemasonry, even among its own members. The catchword "partly" at the end suggests the author is beginning a list of reasons for this lack of study.</meta>

<image-desc>A decorative horizontal woodcut banner featuring a central rose flanked by symmetrical floral scrollwork and smaller blossoms.</image-desc>

Not only the non-Masonic public, but even the Masonic community itself, continues to grope about in the dark. Men who have attained every degree and are familiar with all branches of Masonry still do not know what purpose Masonry serves, nor what its <term>hieroglyphs</term> <note>original: Hieroglyphen; in early Masonic literature, this term referred to the symbols, emblems, and visual allegories used in the lodge rituals, such as the square and compass.</note> are meant to signify.

The reason for this lies in a lack of familiarity with history—especially concerning the origins of Masonry. People have forgotten exactly what they should have remembered above all else; as a result, even in recent times, they have felt compelled to raise questions that make them look ridiculous to the <term>profane</term> <note>original: Profanen; a standard Masonic term for those who are not initiated into the fraternity—the "uninitiated" public.</note>: "Where do we come from?" and "What is our aim?"

This failure to sufficiently study history was rooted in the delusion that everything in Masonry must be a secret; and because the study of it was, in part, not encouraged,

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument from page 1 regarding the neglect of Masonic history. It explains that the study of origins was suppressed for two main reasons: a fear that the truth might make certain rituals look silly, and the desire of "cunning" leaders to maintain power over their members using invented myths.</meta>

partly did not properly direct <note>This continues the sentence from the previous page: "...because the study of [history] was, in part, not encouraged..."</note> their focus, the blame lay with the fear that by uncovering the true origin of the <term>Order</term> <note>original: Ordens; referring to the Masonic fraternity.</note>, certain adopted customs might appear ridiculous, and thus the Order might be disadvantaged. Furthermore, there were also cunning minds who likely knew the true nature of the matter but intentionally obscured it. They did this to achieve ulterior motives by means of their own invented hypotheses—or rather, pretenses—and to govern less-informed <term>Brethren</term> <note>original: Brüder; the standard term for members of the Masonic fraternity.</note> as they pleased.

It was established as a kind of axiom that the history of the origin of the Order was and must remain a secret. One might have asserted this at most against the <term>profane</term> <note>original: Profanen; those not initiated into Freemasonry.</note>, even if one could not thereby prevent them from conducting their own research. But one should have stopped there, instead of sending all kinds of fabrications into the world—fictions that agreed neither with one another nor with other historical truths, and which bore the mark of falsehood upon their very foreheads. Admittedly, the intent was to mislead the profane so that they might not get to the bottom of things. However, regardless of whether this approach was commendable or useful, it was not considered that one would necessarily be exposed to mockery as a result.

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's critique of the Masonic leadership. Having previously argued that leaders intentionally obscured the Order's history to maintain power or avoid appearing ridiculous, the author now questions why even high-ranking members (Masters) were kept in the dark and fed "absurd" myths.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>

But towards the Order's own members, one should not have made a secret of its origin. If it was to be withheld from the lower <term>degrees</term> <note>original: Graden; the hierarchical levels of initiation through which a member progresses.</note> still: why was it not told to us when we became <term>Masters</term> <note>original: Meister; the third and, at the time, highest standard rank in a Masonic Lodge.</note>? And why were we still entertained even then with so many incredible and absurd things? <term>Masons</term> <note>original: Maurer; a common shorthand for Freemasons.</note> are indeed capable of keeping a secret; the world has seen enough proof of this. Why could we not also have kept the history of our society’s origin hidden? Or did our very <term>Worshipful</term> <note>original: ehrwürdige; a traditional title of respect for Masonic officers and lodges.</note> and highly enlightened masters perhaps not know it themselves? Or did they fear, perhaps, that this discovery might make us indifferent toward the Order?

Nothing, however, is easier than to discover the origin of Freemasonry. The documents from which it can be proven lie open to everyone; it only matters that one looks at them correctly. From these I have drawn my narrative; in doing so, I made it my primary task to carefully distinguish that which is certain from that which is merely probable. Meanwhile, I have not stopped merely at the origin of Freemasonry, but have continued its history as far as my purpose required. If the world wishes to see, then

<meta>catchword: kommt</meta>

<summary>The author argues that there was no reason to hide Masonic history from the "Master" rank of the Order, suggesting that the truth is actually easily accessible through public documents for those willing to look.</summary>

<keywords>Freemasonry, Masonic degrees, Master Masons, secrecy, historical evidence, transparency, Worshipful Masters</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the author's argument from page 3, where he asserted that the origins of Freemasonry are easily discovered if one looks at the correct documents. He concludes that thought here by opening the "curtain" on his findings.</meta>

it now depends solely on them: the curtain has been drawn back wide enough!

Naturally, one will find some observations here that have already been made by others—especially those who have come upon the trail of truth—though none of them have followed this trail far enough. Regardless, I venture to assure the public <note>original: Publicum; the author is addressing the "Republic of Letters," the educated reading community of the Enlightenment era.</note> that they are receiving a work that is entirely new, not only as a whole but also in most of its parts, containing no small amount of revelations which others until now were either unable or unwilling to provide.

I could have been more brief if I had chosen to omit the evidence for my assertions, along with the often necessary preliminary <term>critical investigations</term> <note>original: kritischen Untersuchungen; a hallmark of the Enlightenment, referring to the systematic, rational analysis of historical sources.</note>, or refutations of one or another opposing opinion <note>original: Meynung; an archaic spelling of Meinung.</note>, and had instead wished to present only the results of my efforts. I could also have easily imitated the dictatorial tone of our age, and perhaps thereby caused a sensation at first glance. But I did not want this; I wanted to instruct and convince the public, and to deliver a work that would be no mere trifle of the day <note>original: Piece du jour; a French term for a fashionable but ephemeral work of no lasting substance.</note>, read cursorily, admired by the ignorant, and forgotten by the wise. Instead, I sought to bring light into a subject <note>The original German word is split across pages: "Ma-terie" (matter or subject matter).</note> that has hitherto been so dark—

<meta>catchword: matter</meta>

<summary>The author presents his work as a rigorous and original historical investigation, positioning it against the superficial and sensationalist writings common in his time. He emphasizes his commitment to providing evidence and "light" rather than merely stating conclusions or seeking fleeting fame.</summary>
<keywords>Enlightenment scholarship, historical evidence, public instruction, Masonic origins, critical investigation, sensation vs. truth</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page completes the sentence started on page 5 regarding the lasting utility of the work. The author then moves to a critical evaluation of his primary source: James Anderson's "Book of Constitutions."</meta>

...matter, and through its inner content should remain useful even in the future.

Since I must frequently refer to the <term>Book of Constitutions</term> <note>original: Constitutionsbuch; the foundational document for modern Freemasonry, first published by James Anderson in London in 1723.</note> published by Anderson, particularly at the beginning of this work, I wish first to establish its credibility and determine its extent, so as not to build upon uncertain ground. I have the fourth edition of the German translation before me. It bears the title: "The improved *Book of Constitutions* of the ancient honorable Brotherhood of Freemasons, composed by order of the Grand Lodge from its records, traditions, and lodge books for the use of the lodges by James Anderson, D. D. <note>original: Jacob Anderson; James is the standard English form of the name.</note> (Doctor of Divinity), translated from the English, octavo <note>original: 8; a bibliographical term for the size of the book.</note> Frankfurt am Main, at the Andreä Bookstore, 1783. Two parts." Strictly speaking, only the history and statutes of the Masonic society belong here. For in this edition, as well as in the preceding third edition of 1762, many other writings translated from English and French—and some German writings as well—have been added, which were not approved by the <term>Grand Lodge</term> <note>The governing body of Freemasonry.</note> like the history and statutes were, and thus do not possess the same credibility. Regarding these additions, these two editions are themselves different; in

<summary>The author begins his investigation by evaluating the reliability of James Anderson's "Book of Constitutions," a foundational Masonic text. He warns that while the history and statutes are official, later German editions contain unapproved supplemental materials of varying quality.</summary>
<keywords>James Anderson, Book of Constitutions, Grand Lodge, Masonic History, Andreä Bookstore, 1783 edition, source criticism</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the bibliographical analysis of James Anderson's "Book of Constitutions," specifically comparing the contents and publication dates of various German editions mentioned at the end of page 5.</meta>

the fourth edition there are some which are not found in the third; whereas some have also been omitted that are found in the third. I do not have the first and second editions of the translation at hand. As far as I recall, the first edition appeared in 1741; I do not know the year of the second.

The <term>Book of Constitutions</term> <note>The foundational governing document of modern Freemasonry.</note> was published in 1723 by order of the <term>Grand Master</term> and the <term>Grand Lodge</term> after it had been approved on March 25, 1722. (See pp. 421, 442 of the aforementioned fourth edition, which I will always cite.) In the year 1738, a new edition appeared, which was approved on January 25 in the same manner, and to which the <term>approbation</term> <note>original: Approbation; a formal statement of official approval.</note> was added word for word. (p. 544.) The history therein is continued up to the year 1738.

Anyone who wished to doubt whether the <term>Statutes</term> <note>The formal rules and regulations of the organization.</note> published by such an order and for such a use were authentic, and were in force within the Society at that time, would have to provide grounds for their doubt; however, I have nowhere heard or read of such grounds, though I have certainly heard <note>original: "Machtsprüche"</note> dogmatic assertions. Regarding the history, however, which fills the greater part of the work: here one must judge with a sense of distinction.

<summary>The author evaluates the publication history and authenticity of Anderson's "Book of Constitutions," arguing that while the legal statutes are undoubtedly authentic, the historical narrative requires a more critical assessment.</summary>
<keywords>James Anderson, Book of Constitutions, Grand Lodge, Freemasonry, bibliography, Masonic statutes, historical criticism, German translations</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, the author discussed the publication history of the Masonic "Book of Constitutions," distinguishing between its legally binding statutes (which are considered authentic) and its historical narratives, which require more critical scrutiny.</meta>

<page-num>7</page-num>
<sig>A 4</sig>

Where reference is made to <term>Acts of Parliament</term> <note>Official laws enacted by the British legislature.</note>, no doubt can arise; for these acts are also printed elsewhere. The very same applies with regard to other printed books when they are cited, as for example <term>Ashmole's Diary</term> <note>The diary of Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a prominent antiquary and one of the earliest recorded men to be "made" a Freemason in an English lodge.</note>. Nor do I see why one should doubt the <term>documents</term> <note>original: Urkunden; refers here to charters or official records.</note> communicated from the papers of the Society, provided that nothing is contained therein that contradicts other authentic reports. Nowhere is there a reason to surmise that the Society, or the editors, fabricated such documents. Everyone—and thus every writer, and even more so a complete, formal society—enjoys the presumption that they are recounting true events; and this holds until the contrary can be demonstrated.

However, this applies only to the narrative of events that occurred during the times in which the editors lived, or shortly before, when the circumstances could still be well known; and in which the place, the persons, the year, and often even the day are named.

It is a different matter with the preceding histories that ascend into <term>antiquity</term> <note>The author is referring to the legendary history of Masonry, which often traced the craft back to biblical figures or ancient Egypt.</note>: for here, further proofs must be given and the <term>sources</term> <note>original: Quellen</note> must be cited,

<meta>catchword: aus</meta>

<summary>The author argues for the reliability of recent Masonic history and official documents when they align with public records like Acts of Parliament, but cautions that legends dating back to antiquity require much stricter evidentiary proof.</summary>
<keywords>Acts of Parliament, Elias Ashmole, historical evidence, authenticity of documents, Masonic history, antiquity</keywords>

Many people could not reconcile themselves to this, as it did not seem believable to them that what is now called Freemasonry should have originated from a trade guild.

Some may well have had the correct insight; however, they considered it more glorious and advantageous for the **reputation of the Order** not to admit this, and instead, by an authoritative decree, to disparage the <term>Book of Constitutions</term> <note>original: Constitutionsbuch; the rulebook and history of the Grand Lodge.</note> as a wretched and unreliable work. ...manner, it was easier to bring forth and popularize other **hypotheses** regarding the origin and age of the **Order**; of which there has been no shortage. For it is a long-established maxim among us that we declare all books dealing with **Freemasonry** <note>original: Freymaurerey</note> in which truths are found—truths that we do not wish to be recognized as such—to be spurious, unreliable, and absurd, without ever distinguishing what in them is true and what is false. Were we to do this, we would expose ourselves; our so highly-praised **secrets** would no longer be considered secrets. 

Therefore, we make use of the workaround of declaring all books in which these secrets are described to be false, if even the smallest, most insignificant error has crept in, or if circumstances, ceremonies, words, and phrases appear in them that our ancestors had introduced, but which are no longer in use among us. Freemasons are called **Free-Masons** in English. Many learned investigations have been conducted regarding the word **Masons**, from which nothing has ultimately emerged. Suffice it to say, **Masons** means masons and builders, and **Free** means free. But what does "free masons" mean? Exactly what are called <term>guild masons</term> <note>original: zünftige Maurer. In the guild system, a "zünftig" craftsman was one who was a full, legal member of a professional corporation (guild), entitled to all its privileges and secrets.</note>.

This is already found in common dictionaries. However, Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a prominent Berlin Enlightenment writer and bookseller who wrote extensively on the history of the Templars and Freemasonry.</note> has proven it further (*Essay on the Templars*, Part 1, p. 195). "**Free**," he says, "means in English anyone who receives the right of a certain society or guild, and the granted right itself is called **Freedom of a city**." To this, he added the following note: "E.g., in **Wood’s Athenae Oxonienses** <note>A biographical dictionary of Oxford writers and bishops by Anthony Wood, first published in 1691.</note> Vol. I, p. 372, it is said: a certain Simon Forman, who cured the sick, **had much trouble with the Doctors of Physic, because he was not Free among them**," that is, he had many frustrations with the doctors of medicine because he was not, like them, a member of the guild, and was not graduated. Further on (p. 198), Mr. Nicolai cites from **Maitland’s History of London** (p. 598) the expression "**the Mystery of freemen of the Carpentry**" (of 

<summary>The author examines the etymological origins of the term "Freemason," arguing that "Free" simply refers to being a legally recognized member of a trade guild (a "freeman"), rather than having a more mystical or ancient origin.</summary>

--- Page 26 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the parenthetical explanation from page 13 regarding Friedrich Nicolai’s research into the term "Free" and its relationship to guild structures.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>

*Mystery*, or *Mastery* <note>original: "Meisterschaft, Zunft." The author is noting the linguistic confusion between the Greek-derived "mystery" (a secret) and the Latin-derived "mistery/mastery" (a craft or trade guild).</note>, which is often confused with *Mystery* (a secret) because of their identical pronunciation), the guild of free masons of the carpentry trade, or the guild-incorporated carpenters.

The reason for this naming seems, at first glance, not far to seek. They are called *Freemen*, free men, or free masters, because they possess all the liberties, rights, and privileges that belong to the guild, and which one receives precisely by being admitted into the guild.

However, the word *Freymeister* <note>Free Master</note> has exactly the opposite meaning in German. According to the *German Encyclopedia* (Vol. 10, p. 531), <term>Free Masters</term> <note>original: Freymeister. In the German trade system, a "Freymeister" was often a "hedge-specialist" or independent worker who operated outside the strict, often restrictive guild system by special permission.</note> are those who have attained the right to practice a trade not through the usual means, but through special sovereign favor, with exemption from the guild articles and guild jurisdiction. Such individuals are thus outgrowths of territorial sovereignty in <term>administrative matters</term> <note>original: Policeysachen. In this period, "Polizei" referred to the general internal administration and regulation of the state, rather than just modern law enforcement.</note>, and their creation occurs through privileges—therefore, the powers of a Free Master must be judged not according to the rights of other masters or the guild regulations, but according to the clear and actual content of his <term>Letter of Freedom</term> <note>original: Freyheitsbrief. A formal charter or patent granting specific legal rights to an individual.</note>."

<summary>This page explores the etymological and legal distinctions of the term "Freemason," contrasting the English "Freeman" (a guild member) with the German "Freymeister" (a craftsman exempt from guild rules by royal decree).</summary>

<page-num>15</page-num>

This leads to the conjecture that the Free Masons in England originated in the same manner; which, when one considers the history, is no longer a mere conjecture. Not all masons were originally Free Masons; rather, the latter were those who had received certain privileges from the kings—mostly foreigners, who were then joined by locals wishing to share in these privileges. And it is in this sense that the word is used in the **Book of Constitutions** <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch." This refers to James Anderson's *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), the first printed work to outline the history and rules of modern Freemasonry.</note>; at least in the history of masonry in England. Even if, in the earlier times of which Anderson speaks, there may not yet have been any guilds, incorporations, or fellowships <note>The author uses three German terms for guilds: "Zünfte," "Innungen," and "Gilden," reflecting the various regional names for trade organizations.</note>, it is nonetheless not improbable that many people of the same or even different trades—who, as in the case of buildings, must work together—formed a kind of society and likely made certain arrangements and agreements among themselves. Indeed, they likely did so earlier than other craftsmen, such as tailors, shoemakers, and so on, where each could work individually and for his own person; until these eventually imitated the former and thus also united themselves into guilds.

The freedom <note>In this context, "freedom" (Freyheit) refers to legal exemptions or "liberties" granted by a sovereign to operate outside the usual restrictions of local trade laws.</note> which those masons, carpenters, stone-cutters, tilers, and the—

[...and other trades-]men, <note>The text begins mid-word, completing the list of trades from the previous page who were exempt from local laws.</note> who, according to Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), whose *Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723) established the traditional history of the fraternity.</note>, all belonged together and were comprised under the general name of **Free Masons**, and are even now here and there reckoned as belonging to one and the same guild—that which they received from the kings consisted in nothing other than the **exemption from the otherwise usual jurisdiction.** <term>Jurisdiction (Gerichtsstand)</term>: The legal authority to be tried in a specific court; "exemption" meant they were allowed to settle their own legal disputes rather than appearing before local town magistrates. It was natural that foreigners, who were knowledgeable in neither the laws nor the language of the country, stipulated that they should not be judged according to these laws foreign to them, but according to their own manners and customs in disputes arising among themselves. This still happens occasionally even now; I myself know of an example where, when several hundred stonemasons were summoned from abroad for a large construction project, they negotiated that their disputes should be settled by people from their own midst, albeit under the chairmanship of an official, which was indeed granted to them.

It is therefore not at all surprising that the same was demanded and granted in England, and that perhaps even the kings themselves became **High Masters** or **Grand Masters** of such people, or filled their positions with other respected men, primarily those knowledgeable in architecture—even Lords and Bishops, especially since...

Viewed as mere legends, they prove that the freedom which was granted to the masons was seen as nothing more than the permission to organize and govern themselves, without being dependent on ordinary judges in matters concerning the craft. It was foreigners, who came from France to England both at that time and later under King William the Conqueror <note>William I (reigned 1066–1087), the first Norman King of England.</note> (p. 262), who first received this exemption. They were the actual masters, overseers, and leaders, under whom the locals—who did not yet understand as much—worked. Later, some of these locals also became masters themselves, and so they received the same freedom. They joined the already established society; and for that reason, they were likely called **accepted masons** <term>Accepted Masons (angenommene Maurer): Historically, this referred to local English craftsmen "accepted" into the elite guilds of foreign master builders; later, it referred to non-masons (gentlemen) admitted to the fraternity.</term> (p. 255); a term, however, which subsequently acquired a different meaning.

What I have recounted here regarding the origin of the Free Masons is so natural that I do not see why the matter, viewed as a whole, should be doubted. Concerning the freedoms granted to the masons, Anderson (p. 300 f.), where he [discusses] the history of the—

His office granted him the power to settle matters within the brotherhood that did not fall under the jurisdiction of the courts of law. Both masons and patrons <note>original: "Bauherrn"; referring to those commissioning the buildings.</note> appealed to him if they came into dispute with one another, in order to prevent legal proceedings; and in his absence, they appealed to his deputy or Grand Warden <term>Grand Warden (Großvorsteher): A high-ranking officer in a Masonic lodge who assists the Grand Master.</term>, who resided nearby.” Anderson adds: “This is the tradition of the old Scottish masons, and is found in their documents.”

I believe, therefore, that I have reason to make a distinction between the "common" masons and the "free" masons. The former were scattered here and there across the country and worked for themselves; even if they maintained a connection among themselves in certain regions and thus formed a <term>guild</term> <note>original: "Zunft"; a medieval association of craftsmen that regulated their trade, training, and local business.</note>, this was nonetheless not a single, universal guild. Instead, there were several individual guilds organized by cities or districts, much as it still is in Germany, where the collective masons of a city or region form a guild for themselves which has no close connection with a guild in another city or region. 

The guild of the Freemasons, by contrast, was a single guild extending throughout the entire kingdom. For this reason, it was uniquely worthy of the kings' attention, and they themselves often became Grand Masters of the—

This organization was a single, great whole, comprising several divisions or **lodges** <term>lodges</term> <note>original: *Logen*; the basic organizational units of Freemasonry, referring both to the group of members and the room or building where they meet.</note>, which were overseen by a superior lodge. This superior body consisted of a **Grand Master** <term>Grand Master</term> and several **Grand Wardens** <term>Grand Wardens</term> <note>original: *Großvorsteher*; senior officers who assist the Grand Master in governing the fraternity.</note> who, together with the **deputies** <note>representatives sent from the local branches to the central authority.</note> from the individual lodges, formed the **Grand Lodge**. The individual lodges gathered occasionally on their own, but at certain times a **general assembly** was held. However, when such an assembly did not take place and no deputies were present in the Grand Lodge, the Grand Master and the Grand Officers conducted a form of government on their own in the interim.

These **Freemasons** <term>Freemasons</term> <note>original: *freyen Maurer*; literally "free masons," referring to craftsmen who were not bound to a single local lord or city, allowing them to travel for major projects.</note> were employed throughout the country whenever large, magnificent buildings were being erected that required long periods of labor. Such projects necessitated many skilled people of every variety who maintained a close connection and a good understanding with one another, so that they could work together effectively. All, or at least most, of the masons, master carpenters, and so forth, who lived in **London** and **Westminster**, belonged to this society; however, individual lodges could also be found at times in other parts of the kingdom. At the time of the decline of the **Freemason society**, which I shall discuss further on, these lodges were falling into disuse...

One of these deputies was named Heinrich Hevele <note>Henry Yevele (c. 1320–1400), a famous English master mason and architect for King Edward III and Richard II.</note>, who in the old records is first called the **King’s Freemason** <term>King’s Freemason</term> <note>original: *Königs Freymaurer*; a title indicating a master builder in service to the crown.</note>. <meta>This page continues the discussion of a historical record from the reign of King Edward III, which claimed that all the Lords of the realm were Freemasons at that time. It follows the previous page's mention of the Grand Lodge establishing its governing laws.</meta>

That all Lords at that time were Freemasons is quite unbelievable. Neither before nor after is such a case found in Anderson’s history <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739) published the *Constitutions of the Free-Masons* in 1723, which included a "history" of the craft that blended fact with legend.</note>. There is also no apparent reason why all of them should have joined specifically at that time. The guild required at most a few respected men to serve as protectors, Grand Masters, and Wardens. Perhaps this record was only composed later from a merely oral legend, and thus the matter was exaggerated. That some Lords, however, presided as heads of the society cannot be doubted; for it was so before and after the times of Edward III.

However, this document proves that the Freemasons—with the exception of the Grand Master and perhaps the Wardens—were at that time nothing more than actual masons and craftsmen. For it says therein: “The Master Masons or Master Builders shall be examined [to see] if they are experienced enough in their science to assist their <term>respective</term> <note>original: *resp.*, an abbreviation of *respektive*.</note> lords, from the highest to the lowest, to the honor and dignity of the aforementioned art, for the benefit of their masters—that is, those who employ them and pay for their work. Furthermore: The apprentices shall be exhorted upon their acceptance not to be thieves nor receivers of stolen goods. The journeymen shall

<meta>This page continues the legal text of the 1425 Act of Parliament aimed at suppressing the assemblies of masons, followed by historical commentary on the Act's enforcement and eventual obsolescence as argued by Masonic historians like James Anderson.</meta>

...that such chapters and congregations <note>In this context, "chapters" and "congregations" refer to the formal organizational meetings and assemblies held by the stonemasons' guilds.</note> shall no longer be held in the future; and in the event that such should occur, those who are found to be the cause of the holding and assembling of such chapters and congregations—where they can be convicted of it—shall be regarded as grievous felons <note>original: "Missethäter." While this literally means "evildoers," in the legal language of the time, it denoted someone committing a serious crime or felony.</note>; and other masons who attend such chapters and congregations shall be punished with imprisonment of their bodies and be required to pay a fine and ransom at the King's pleasure. (p. 275.)

Presumably, at such assemblies, all manner of regulations were established that ran contrary to the previous statutes and were detrimental to the masters <note>The "Bauherrn" were the patrons or employers who funded and oversaw the construction of cathedrals and public buildings.</note>, such as the raising of wages and similar matters. > "that they were right, good, and fair to observe, as they had been extracted and collected from the records of old times." (p. 279).

Clearly, the Parliament is speaking here of **actual masons** <note>The author is referring to "operative" masons—those who actually worked with stone—distinguishing them from "speculative" masons who joined for social or philosophical reasons.</note>. That it does not call them **Freemasons** may come from the fact that not all masons were simultaneously Freemasons, which is why the general expression was used so as to leave no room for evasion. Perhaps the **Freemasons** had also adopted this name for themselves and on their own authority, without it ever having been officially recognized by the authorities. In any case, the people whom the Parliament calls **masons**, **Anderson** calls **Freemasons**; and this is also how the document under **Edward IV** <note>Edward IV reigned as King of England from 1461–1470 and again from 1471–1483.</note> speaks. Although **Anderson** distinguishes between the working and the noble brothers, he applies the name of **Freemasons** to both, and by no means to the latter alone. Nor does he say that the number of noble brothers was large, as seems to follow from the above act under **Edward III** <note>Edward III (reigned 1327–1377) is often cited in Masonic legend as a patron of the arts and sciences, including architecture.</note>. Likely there were only a few of them, perhaps the leaders along with the **Grand Masters**. The reason noble persons were chosen for this was to more easily maintain order and respect among the masons, among whom—at least in the earliest times—there were many foreigners. For such a large [group] scattered throughout the entire realm

During the disputes of the two Roses <note>The Wars of the Roses (1455–1487), a series of dynastic civil wars for the throne of England between the Houses of Lancaster and York.</note>, **Masonry** <term>Maurerey: In this context, the author refers to the legendary history of Freemasonry as described by James Anderson.</term> was greatly neglected. **Anderson** says nothing further; however, it will be shown below that the masons actually took sides at that time. Under **Edward IV** (A. 1471 — 1483), a bishop was Grand Master. During the short reigns of **Edward V** and **Richard III** (A. 1483 — 1485), there seems to have been no Grand Master; at least **Anderson** mentions none. **Henry VII** (A. 1485 — 1509) was himself Grand Master, but under him there were two deputy Grand Masters, through whom the King assembled a **lodge** of masters in the palace and laid the foundation stone to a chapel; the feast of the builders in the year 1507 was also solemnly celebrated. Under **Henry VIII** (An. 1509 — 1547), **Cardinal Wolsey** and later two secular lords were Grand Masters; **Masonry** and architecture were improved under him. Under **Edward VI** (A. 1447 — 1553) <note>Edward VI reigned from 1547–1553; the "1447" in the text appears to be a typographical error for 1547.</note>, first a duke, then a bishop, was Grand Master. Under **Queen Elizabeth** (A. 1553 — 1603) <note>Elizabeth I reigned from 1558–1603. The author's date of 1553 likely refers to the death of Edward VI and the subsequent political transition.</note>, there were four secular lords. (Pages 281 — 292, p. 417.)

These men then attended the meeting and provided the Queen with such a favorable report that she never again thought of banishing or disturbing the **Freemasons**. Instead, she regarded them as a special class of people who sought to practice peace and friendship, arts and sciences, without interfering in matters of church or state. This tradition, **Anderson** adds in a note, was steadfastly believed by all the old English masons. (Page 289.)

From this it is evident that no lady was admitted to the <term>office of Grand Master</term>, a point which **Anderson** explicitly repeats again later, adding that neither a minor regent nor anyone who is not a brother <note>i.e., someone not initiated into the fraternity.</note> can attain the office of Grand Master. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the 1561 incident in York where Queen Elizabeth I’s emissaries were initiated into Freemasonry. It explores the historical social structure of guilds and the precedent for "accepted" (non-operative) members within the craft.</meta>

From all appearances, the <term>regents</term> <note>Referring to the presiding officers or leaders of the Masonic body.</note> had to be formally admitted even back then. Also, even at that time, they employed the tactic that the **Freemasons** later used on several occasions; namely, they sought to win over the emissaries <note>The officials sent by the Queen to investigate the assembly, as described on the previous page.</note> and admitted them into the society themselves.

In the time of **Queen Elizabeth**, it must not have been unusual for people of other ranks <note>People who were not professional stonemasons or "operative" builders.</note> to associate with the society of masons, even without becoming a <term>Grand Master</term> or an official of the <term>guild</term> <note>original: "Zunft"</note>. For had this been something new, the emissaries would have hesitated to allow themselves to be made **Freemasons**.

At that time, the custom must already have existed that every citizen was required to belong to a guild, and that if he was not himself a craftsman, he could choose a guild to join—indeed, he could even be a member of more than one guild. For among the emissaries who allowed themselves to be made **Freemasons**, there were likely individuals who were already citizens and consequently already belonged to a guild.

This remains the custom even today. When **Pitt**, later Lord and **Earl of Chatham** <note>William Pitt the Elder (1708–1778), a prominent British statesman.</note>, received the <term>citizenship</term> <note>original: "Bürgerrecht"</note> of London,

It should therefore surprise no one that people who were not themselves masons joined the **Masons' Guild** <note>original: "Maurerzunft"</note>, for this is the case with all guilds in **England**.

But there are very good reasons why the most distinguished gentlemen in **England** do not consider it beneath them to belong to a guild, to appear at meetings, to take part in deliberations, to attend banquets, and—if the guild has its own clothing—to wear it during these events and participate in all guild customs. All seats in the **Lower House** <note>original: "Unterhauß"; the House of Commons in the British Parliament.</note> of **Parliament** are assigned through the election of certain persons in the **counties** <note>original: "Grafschaften"</note> and cities. Anyone who wishes to be elected as a **representative** of a city must be a citizen of that city and belong to a guild. The guild considers it an honor to have one or another distinguished man among its members; and such a man can usually count on the votes of its members. These members, in turn,

Yet the masons had another secret besides their passwords <note>original: "Spruch"; literally "saying" or "motto." The little that these good people knew seemed a great deal to them; and to maintain a certain advantage, they made a secret of it—as was customary even in ancient times—and even boasted that all the arts in general had been invented by them. All of this is confirmed by an old document, of which the famous **Locke** <note>John Locke (1632–1704), the influential English Enlightenment philosopher.</note> found a copy in the **Bodleian Library** and shared with the **Earl of Pembroke** in a letter dated May 6, 1696. The notes which ***Locke*** added to it appear in both translations; however, in ***Preston’s*** version, there are additional notes appended to ***Locke’s***, which further clarify certain points.

In this document, the respondent speaks much the same language that is still common today. He describes **Masonry** as a knowledge of nature, and a science of number, weight, and measure, and of the proper way to arrange all things for human use—primarily dwellings and buildings, and all other things that benefit mankind—and traces it back to the first men of the East, from whence it came to the West by way of the **Venetians** (<note>original: "Venetianer." <meta>This page continues the analysis of the "Leland Manuscript," specifically addressing the claims made about the character and knowledge of Masons. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing the "universal language of Masons."</meta>

letters and credentials <note>The original terms "briefe und Kundſchaften" refer to formal certificates of character and traveling papers used by journeymen to prove their identity and skills to distant guilds.</note> emerged, and are partly still in use.) The Masons do not exactly all have more knowledge than other people; but they have more right and opportunity for it; but some lack the ability, and even more lack the diligence that is so necessary for the acquisition of all knowledge. Some Masons are not as virtuous as other people; but on the whole (it says "for the most part" in the original) they are better than they would be if they were not Masons. They also love one another greatly, which cannot be otherwise; for good and upright men, who know each other as such, love each other all the more, the better they are."

As exaggerated as much in this speech is, it is nevertheless clear that the Masons boasted of a secret, that they revealed the little they knew—and which they valued so highly—to no one outside their circle, and that they had a kind of their own language, expressions, and signs among themselves and kept them secret; in all of which there is nothing incomprehensible, since in the Middle Ages <note>original: "mittlern Zeiten."</note> both scholars and craftsmen used to make a secret out of many, often quite insignificant, things. Moreover, in the entire document, the discussion is merely about actual masons <note>Referring to "operative" masons—manual laborers and stone-cutters—rather than the "speculative" Freemasons of later centuries.</note>, and by no means about Free-

After his return, he practiced architecture <note>original: "Baukunst."</note>, and the King employed him and the then-Grand Warden, an Earl of Pembroke <note>William Herbert, 3rd Earl of Pembroke, a famous patron of the arts.</note>, who had sponsored Jones's travels, for various buildings. Alongside the Earl, Nicholas Stone, a sculptor, was also a Grand Warden. The best fellows of the craft gathered from all sides around the <term>Grand Master</term> <note>Großmeister</note> Jones, who always granted good wages and appropriate time for instruction in the <term>lodges</term> <note>Logen</note>, and provided these with excellent by-laws, through which they were made equal to the schools or academies of designers in Italy. During such a flourishing period of <term>Masonry</term> <note>Maurerey</note>, many noble, wealthy, and learned people were admitted as brothers for the honor of the guild, at their own request.

<meta>This page continues from page 56, completing the word "masons" (Maurer) to explain how the guild suffered following the downfall of Prince Edwin. It tracks the craft's reputation from the Norman conquest through the reign of Henry VI.</meta>

sons had shared in his fall and were forced to suffer for a time, but later their reputation rose again; and we find under our **Norman princes** <note>The kings of England following William the Conqueror’s invasion in 1066, who were known for their extensive castle and cathedral building</note> that they often enjoyed extraordinary marks of royal favor. One must not doubt that the skill of the **masons**, which was excellent at all times even in the most barbaric ages, their admirable helpfulness and attachment to one another—regardless of how much they differed in their personal circumstances—and their inviolable loyalty in the conscientious concealment of their secret, must have exposed them in ignorant, confused, and suspicious times to a great variety of adventures, according to the varying fate of political parties and other changes in the government. In passing, I wish to remark that the masons have always been faithful subjects; because of this, in times when might prevailed over right and when those who actually practiced treason punished honest people as traitors, they were exposed to great cruelties. Thus, in the third year of the reign of **Henry VI**, an <term>Act of Parliament</term> (8) <note>original: Parlamentsacte; a formal law passed by the legislature</note> was created to abolish the society of masons and, under heavy penalty, [prohibit them] from set-

In the confused times that followed, the Freemasons in this kingdom were commonly *Yorkists* <note>Supporters of the House of York during the 15th-century dynastic civil wars known as the Wars of the Roses</note>, and just as this earned them exceptional favor from *Edward IV*, so the wise *Henry VII* <note>The first Tudor king, known for his shrewdness and for ending the Wars of the Roses</note> considered it more advisable—by presenting himself as a great friend of the masons—to press a multitude of his friends into this worthy brotherhood. In this way, he never lacked sufficient informants within their lodges, rather than making enemies through untimely persecution as some of his ancestors had done 10). Because this society is so ancient that it reaches back almost further than written records extend, it is no wonder that a mixture of fables can be found in its history; and in my opinion, a more recent, devious writer would have done better to spend his time clarifying the history of *St. Alban* <note>Saint Alban, the first British Christian martyr, who in Masonic tradition is said to have provided the craft with its first "charges" or constitution</note> or the death of Prince *Edwin* <note>Prince Edwin of York, a legendary figure in the "Old Charges" of Masonry, said to have summoned a great assembly of masons in 926 AD</note>

<meta>This page continues from page 58, completing a sentence regarding the historical research that a critic (likely Dr. Robert Plot) should have undertaken into the legends of St. Alban and Prince Edwin rather than attacking the Masonic society. It also addresses the conflicting historical accounts of Elias Ashmole and James Anderson regarding the craft’s origins in England.</meta>

would have applied, since either of these subjects would have given him sufficient work to do—certainly more than he accomplished by disparaging a society whose foundation and history are evidently so little known to him 11), and with whose history and conduct Mr. *Ashmole* <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a celebrated antiquary and one of the earliest recorded "speculative" Freemasons</note>, who knew them much better, was perfectly satisfied 12), and so forth. (With this, the letter concludes in both books in which it is contained.)

The anonymous author of the letters concerning Freemasonry has already noticed the discrepancy found between this letter and the *Andersonian* account <note>Referring to the historical narrative provided by James Anderson in his 1723 "Constitutions," which often blended legend with history</note>. *Ashmole* says, according to the attached letter: *St. Alban* <note>Saint Alban, the first British Christian martyr, who in Masonic lore is credited with organizing the masons and providing them with their first statutes</note> introduced the craft of masonry to *England*. He likely understood this to mean nothing more than that the masons united into a society at that time.

For the craft itself and individual masons certainly existed beforehand; one need only think of the wall which the Emperor *Severus* <note>The Roman Emperor Septimius Severus, who arrived in Britain in 208 AD to fortify the northern frontier</note> established in *207 AD*; the temple built in *238 AD* (p. 237), not to mention other buildings mentioned by *Anderson*. Of *St. Alban* it is told—

he recounts that he <note>Referring to St. Alban, mentioned on the previous page</note> had obtained a patent for the Freemasons to hold a general meeting, which he gave the name of an assembly (p. 239). Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), the Scottish minister who authored the "Constitutions of the Free-Masons" in 1723</note> passes over this circumstance in complete silence (p. 267), likely so that no one might take occasion to claim that the guild <term>Zunft</term> <note>A medieval association of craftsmen or merchants</note> of Free Masons was founded by a Pope, because the Pope is so hated in England. It also remains uncertain whether Anderson found this circumstance in the documents he used, and whether he consulted the Ashmolean manuscripts; for no trace of these is found in his work.

He also does not say, as Ashmole does, that King Henry VI himself became a fellow <note>original: "Mitgenoß"</note> of the craft according to the aforementioned Act of Parliament, but merely states that the laws of the Freemasons were approved by this King and by the lords of his High Council.

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding King Henry VI's relationship with the Freemasons. This page argues that the King could have later reviewed and approved Masonic statutes once he reached adulthood, despite the earlier prohibitions enacted during his minority.</meta>

He had knowledge of our chronicles and laws.

Note: **Ashmole** <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692) was an English antiquary and early Freemason who collected various versions of the "Old Charges" or legendary histories of the craft.</note> was aware of this, and also cites the *Collection of Statutes* by the same **Ferdinand Pulton** <note>Ferdinand Pulton (1536–1617) was a legal scholar known for his systematic collections of English law.</note>, to which **Plot** <note>Dr. Robert Plot (1640–1696), author of *The Natural History of Stafford-shire*, provided one of the earliest skeptical accounts of Masonic history.</note> also refers; and although **Anderson** does not cite this book, he has nevertheless provided the **Act of Parliament** verbatim. It was drafted during the King's minority; however, after the year 1425 when this occurred, he continued to reign without a rival until 1453, and in total until 1471. In this intervening period, the King could still have read and approved the **statutes of the Freemasons**. **Anderson** does not state the year when this occurred; and although he refers to a document containing this circumstance along with another—namely, that the **Masons** held assemblies in the year 1434 when the King was 13 years old—it does not follow directly from this that the King also approved the statutes in that same year, which the document we provided above also does not say. And even if it had happened in that same year, it is still no contradiction.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of historical legal statutes affecting Freemasons. The previous page explored whether King Henry VI approved Masonic statutes in the mid-15th century, despite an earlier Act of Parliament during his minority that banned their assemblies. This page transitions to the views of Dr. Robert Plot regarding how later legislation under Queen Elizabeth I affected these earlier rules.</meta>

73

E 5

> “Although these statutes (namely those of the Masons of whom we spoke above) were repealed <note>original: *repealed*</note> by a subsequent Act in the 5th year of the reign of Queen Elizabeth, by which Act servants and laborers can be compelled to serve, and their wages are restricted, and all masters—if they give more wages than is prescribed by the courts—and the journeymen (*Servants*)—if they accept it—are declared punishable: yet, since even this Act is little followed anymore, it is still to be feared that these chapters <note>In this context, 'chapters' refers to the organized meetings or lodges of the Freemasons.</note> might cause as much mischief as before, which, judging by the fine, was formerly so great that it might perhaps be useful to examine them now.”

So far *Plot* <note>Dr. Robert Plot (1640–1696), a natural historian who was one of the first to write a skeptical account of Masonic history in his *Natural History of Staffordshire* (1686).</note>.

**Note.** The translator here plays the critic, but not very successfully. In the original, it states: this statute was repealed under Queen *Elizabeth*: *repealed*. The translator believes it must be read as *repeated*, and refers to a candidate—whom he calls a tasteful connoisseur of the language—who suggested this “improvement” to him. However, the language itself has nothing to do with the matter: for *repealed* is just as good English as *repeated*. *Plot* cites the aforementioned *Pulton* <note>Ferdinand Pulton (1536–1617), a legal scholar who compiled English statutes.</note>, and Lord *Coke’s Institutes* <note>Referring to Sir Edward Coke’s *Institutes of the Laws of England*, a foundational series of legal treatises. The OCR text reads "Cook," a common contemporary misspelling of Coke.</note>.

<summary>This page quotes Dr. Robert Plot’s skeptical view that Masonic assemblies remained a potential source of social mischief despite being legally superseded by Elizabethan labor laws. The author also defends Plot's original English terminology ("repealed") against a translator's suggested correction to "repeated."</summary>
<keywords>Robert Plot, Queen Elizabeth I, labor statutes, Masonic chapters, legal history, Edward Coke, Ferdinand Pulton, linguistics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the legal and historical analysis of statutes affecting Freemasons in England. The author is specifically critiquing how various historians (Anderson, Coke, Plot) and previous translators have interpreted the status of the 1425 Act of Parliament against Masons under later monarchs like Queen Elizabeth I.</meta>

74

Anderson also refers to the same man, but calls him Coke; I have found his name written both ways in other sources. Coke merely says: By the Act of Queen Elizabeth, all statutes concerning laborers preceding it were repealed; and from this he concludes that the Act under Henry VI was thereby rendered invalid, without explicitly stating that it was repealed—something Anderson likewise does not do, but contents himself with adding that the Act was never enforced (276). Dr. W. <note>Likely Dr. Richard Rawlinson (1690–1755), who studied the Ashmolean manuscripts and was involved in early Masonic historiography.</note> reports, indeed from the Ashmolean manuscripts, that the Act was revoked, and specifically during the reign of King Henry VI; however, Coke and Anderson know nothing of this, or they certainly would not have omitted it. Plot agrees with these latter authors; however, the words *which Statute though repealed by a subsequent act in the 5. of Eliz.* should not be translated as they were in the aforementioned letters: "Although this decree was repeated <note>The German text uses *wiederhohlt* (repeated), which is a mistranslation of the English *repealed* (annulled/revoked).</note> by a subsequent Act in the 5th year of Elizabeth." <meta>This page continues the analysis of Robert Plot’s "Natural History of Staffordshire," specifically concluding the critique of a translator’s misunderstanding regarding the legal term "Statute." It then moves into a historical summary of the origins of Freemasonry as a transition from a trade guild to a speculative society.</meta>

number appeared; therefore, he made two distinct things out of it and believed that the last-used singular term, *Statute*, designated the Act of Parliament under Henry VI <note>Henry VI (r. 1422–1461). The author is referring to a specific 1425 law (3 Hen. VI c. 1) that prohibited masons from organizing "chapters and congregations."</note>, even though it is actually the same as the preceding *Statutes*.

From all of this, the following conclusion emerges: the Freemasons were originally actual, working masons, and this was true even as late as Ashmole’s <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a celebrated antiquary whose diary provides the earliest record of a non-mason being initiated into a lodge in 1646.</note> time, when he was initiated in the year 1646. Yet, not all masons were *Free*masons; rather, the latter formed their own guild or society which extended throughout the entire realm. Even then, as had been the case before, there were people from other stations of life among them who associated themselves with the society. At what time such people first joined cannot be precisely determined. The occasion likely arose because the Freemasons received Grand Masters and Wardens <note>original: "Vorsteher," referring to the presiding officers of a lodge.</note> from among the nobility, which then opened the way for other people who were not of the trade to join. This joining occurred more frequently in the time of Jones <note>Inigo Jones (1573–1652), the influential English architect often credited in Masonic legend as a Grand Master who brought Classical style to the craft.</note> than before, presumably because of the instruction that was now given in the lodges in an improved form. Those members who were not actually working masons were termed "accepted"—

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 76 regarding the terminology of early Freemasonry. It specifically completes the word "angenommene" (accepted) from the previous page and begins to define the distinction between operative and non-operative members.</meta>

77

...accepted <note>original: "angenommene," completing the word from the previous page</note> Masons, and this expression, which originally designated all masons admitted into the society, gradually acquired a more restricted meaning. They are contrasted with the working—that is, the actual—masons; both taken together are understood under the title of **Freemasons**. If, however, as sometimes occurs (for example, in the *Old Charges* <note>original: "alten Pflichten," referring to the traditional rules and legendary histories of the craft compiled by James Anderson</note> of the Freemasons in Anderson, p. 428), *free* and *accepted* masons are placed side-by-side, then the former are the actual working masons, while the latter are members from other social classes. But in those times, the members from other classes were never called Freemasons exclusively or primarily.

It is certain that the actual masons received the name **Freemasons** from the freedoms and privileges granted to them, according to which, in matters concerning the <term>trade and the building arts</term> <note>original: "Handwerk und das Bauwesen"</note>, they were not subject to the ordinary judges, but rather to people from their own ranks. However, it is uncertain whether this took place as early as the 3rd century in the time of **Alban** <note>St. Alban (d. c. 209 or 304 AD), whom Masonic legend claims organized the first assembly of masons in England and secured them higher wages</note>, or in the 6th century in the time of **Augustine** <note>St. Augustine of Canterbury (d. 604 AD), often credited in legend with overseeing the construction of early English churches</note>, or at the beginning of the 8th century. For Anderson derives his evidence solely from the fact that this is asserted in the old Constitutions, and by the...

However, it is probable that such practices were already common in the 10th century during the time of King **Athelstan** <note>Athelstan (r. 924–939) was the first King of all England; Masonic tradition, specifically the "Old Charges," claims he granted the craft a royal charter at a great assembly in York</note>, and that the foreigners—who were brought into the country under him, as well as after him in the 11th century under **William the Conqueror** (who brought many people from Normandy to England anyway)—received such <term>freedoms</term> <note>original: "Freyheiten," referring here to the specific legal exemptions and privileges granted to the guild</note>.

By way of a passing reminder, one can see from this why, although England is undeniably the fatherland of the Freemasons, some have nevertheless claimed that Freemasonry was brought over to England from France. This is true insofar as masons came to England from France and, as **Anderson** (p. 250) <note>James Anderson (1679–1739), the author of "The Constitutions of the Free-Masons" (1723), the first printed manual of the Grand Lodge of England</note> recounts, brought certain laws and customs from there. However, it does not follow from this that these people already enjoyed an exemption from the ordinary <term>legal jurisdiction</term> <note>original: "Gerichtsstand"</note> in their own fatherland and were thus already "Free" Masons; rather, this was likely first introduced in England. This occurred precisely because they, as foreigners, were ignorant of the English language and laws—although it is not unbelievable that in France, too, some of their internal disputes may have been decided by people from among their own ranks. Such—

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 78, completing the word "solche" (such) from the previous page. The author is disputing the claim that Freemasonry originated in France, arguing instead for its English roots.</meta>

...such things do not arise all at once, but rather bit by bit; and even if the masons in France already possessed certain **freedoms** <term>freedoms</term> <note>original: "Freyheiten"; specifically the legal exemptions from local taxes and jurisdiction granted to traveling builders</note>: the actual establishment of the matter only occurred and was brought to completion in England. Only in England are there reports and legends of **Freemasons** in the 3rd, 6th, 8th, and 10th centuries, but not in France. Therefore, if some claim that there were **regular lodges** of exempt actual masons—or even **Freemasons** in the modern sense of the word—in France earlier than in England, from which this institution was first brought to England, they should rightly be expected to prove it. No traces of either type of free masons are found in France. It is not sufficient to say that properly organized lodges of modern Freemasons already existed in France in the 17th century, nor to appeal to **documents** that no one in the **Order** <note>The author refers to the Freemasonic Order</note> itself is familiar with and which are never produced. One cannot excuse oneself by saying that anyone who knows the history of the Order is convinced of this, or that the world would trust documents no more than the mere assurance that "it is known within the Order." For who does not see that this is a mere excuse?

<meta>This page continues the author's argument regarding the historical identity of Freemasons. The catchword from the previous page, "ent-," likely completed a word like *entstanden* (originated) or *entlehnt* (borrowed), following the discussion of French versus English origins.</meta>

Evidence shows that the fundamental membership <note>original: "Grundstoff," literally 'base material' or 'substance'; here referring to the social makeup of the group</note> of the **Freemasons** consisted of actual masons until a certain point in time. **Anderson's Book of Constitutions** <note>Published by James Anderson in 1723, this was the first printed book of laws and history for the Grand Lodge of England</note> and the documents cited therein use both designations consistently as synonymous, and this not only in ancient history—for example, during the construction of **Solomon’s Temple**, where the number of **Freemasons** employed is calculated at 113,600 (p. 42)—but also in subsequent history up to the most recent times. 

Likewise, **Ashmole** <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a celebrated English antiquary and one of the first recorded "speculative" (non-craftsman) members of a Masonic lodge</note>, as far as one can judge from Dr. W.'s letter, considers Freemasons to be [actual] masons; and **Plot** <note>Dr. Robert Plot (1640–1696), a naturalist whose *Natural History of Staffordshire* (1686) contains one of the earliest, albeit critical, accounts of Masonic customs</note>, who was quite well informed as can be seen from the passage he provided, holds the same view. It does not hinder his credibility that he doubts the high antiquity of the society; for even if he were right in this—which he is not—it has no influence on the description he gives of the Freemasons of his own time, and thus detracts nothing from his reliability. The author of the frequently cited letters concerning Freemasonry has likewise recognized and demonstrated this point.

Yet he believes that the name **Freemason** could, at most, have come from the Italian **builders** <term>builders</term> <note>original: "Bauleute"; often used specifically for the traveling medieval master builders</note>

He also cites the records from other more recent books, which they nonetheless took from the **Book of Constitutions** <note>This refers to James Anderson's *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), the foundational text for organized Freemasonry.</note>. Did he want to avoid the necessity of discussing the latter, and thereby remove the significant objection that can be raised against his own manner of presentation (which will be described further shortly) based on this evidence? Regarding the reports from **Ashmole** <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), an English antiquary and politician, whose diaries contain some of the earliest recorded evidence of non-masons being "made" Freemasons.</note>, this circumstance did indeed occur to him. He does not dare to deny that Ashmole speaks of actual masons; but he tries to extricate himself from the matter by claiming (Part 1, p. 196) that Ashmole acted as an **antiquarian** <term>antiquarian: a historical scholar who studies the artifacts and records of the past</term> and, in order to make his society appear ancient, substituted **Masonry** for **Freemasonry**, attributing what he found regarding the former to the latter.

However, this is what one calls "cutting the knot" <note>A reference to the Gordian Knot; solving a complex problem by a bold, simple stroke that avoids the actual difficulty.</note> rather than untying it. Plain evidence shows that Ashmole—and he surely would have known—regarded the Freemasons as Masons, just as the **Book of Constitutions** does. To refute this, actual reasons would have had to be provided. Ashmole was far too good a historian and too learned a man for one to believe him capable of such a confusion, which anyone would have had to discover at first glance.

Of course, if the conception Mr. *Nicolai* <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811) was a prominent writer and bookseller of the Berlin Enlightenment who published a famous, though controversial, historical inquiry into the origins of Freemasonry.</note> previously provided regarding the origin of Freemasonry were correct, no other alternative would remain. But it is precisely here that the difficulty lies; despite a great display of scholarship—which might still be useful in other contexts—Mr. *Nicolai* has left the main points at issue here unproven.

His conception is essentially as follows: At the beginning of the seventeenth century, the Rosicrucians emerged. *Valentin Andreä* <note>Johann Valentin Andreae (1586–1654), a German theologian. He is widely considered the author of the foundational Rosicrucian texts, though he later claimed they were a youthful "play" or "joke."</note> published the *Fama Fraternitatis* <note>original: *Fama Fraternitatis*, "The Fame of the Brotherhood," the first Rosicrucian manifesto.</note> in 1614, and in 1616, the *Chemical Wedding of Christian Rosenkreuz*. In the former work, he first mentioned a brotherhood of Rosicrucians; and although the whole thing was merely a literary fiction <note>original: *Gedicht*, here meaning a poetic invention or allegory intended to spark moral reform.</note> through which *Andreä* intended to encourage the wise to reform everything corrupted in the world and to spread true wisdom—to which end they were to join in a society unknown to the world—he nonetheless caused such secret associations actually to arise afterward. Both writings caused a sensation; *Germany* and *England* were filled with devotees of secret sciences, especially *alchemy* and *astrology*. All those fond of mysteries believed they would find their fulfillment <note>original: *ihre Rechnung zu finden*, a German idiom meaning to find what one is looking for or to find something to one's advantage.</note> in the Rosicrucian society—

...to find. They wanted to be admitted into it, they sought further communication; but no one could find them. Now various individuals came forward who boasted of being members of the Rosicrucian Society; however, they moved far away from Andreae’s <note>Johann Valentin Andreae (1586–1654), the theologian mentioned on the previous page as the author of the Rosicrucian manifestos.</note> ideas; therefore, he later declared himself against them and even stated that that entire brotherhood was a fiction <note>original: *Fabel*, meaning a fable or literary invention.</note>.
