At the same time, Bacon of Verulam <note>Francis Bacon (1561–1626), the English philosopher and statesman who is often credited with developing the scientific method.</note> arose in England and opposed the obscure and extravagant philosophers of the time, who wished to be understood only by their followers and therefore wrote only esoterically <term>esoterically: knowledge intended for an inner circle of initiates</term>; in contrast, Bacon wanted the sciences to be treated exoterically <term>exoterically: knowledge intended to be understood by the general public</term>, that is, in such a way that they would become understandable and useful to everyone. Therefore, he wrote not only (in the year 1605) his work *On the Advancement of Learning* <note>original Latin: *de augmentis scientiarum*</note>, but also a novel titled *The New Atlantis*. In this he imagines that a ship landed on an unknown island, which he calls Bensalem, on which a King Salomona had formerly reigned, who had established a society that [bore] the name

<meta>This page continues the investigation into whether Friedrich Nicolai conflated different groups—specifically the Brotherhood of Astrologers and a "Rosicrucian" society of scientists—to support his theory on the origins of Freemasonry. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing a meeting of scholars.</meta>

Whether, however, Mr. Nicolai, by his Rosicrucian society of natural scientists, also meant the aforementioned society of astrologers into which Ashmole was actually admitted, is not entirely clear, since he does not explicitly speak of any society of astrologers. Nevertheless, it is likely: for he says that Ashmole was admitted into the society established around 1646; and it was precisely in this year that he became a member of the Brotherhood of Astrologers. In Mr. Nicolai’s society, there was also Lilly <note>William Lilly (1602–1681) was the most famous English astrologer of the 17th century; his association with Ashmole is well-documented</note>, whom he mentions by name; and according to the Ashmolean biography, he too was in the Brotherhood of Astrologers. There, however, two other men are mentioned whom Mr. Nicolai does not name in his society, whereas he identifies several others who do not appear in the biography.

But it is enough that Mr. Nicolai explicitly asserts that Ashmole was a member of a Rosicrucian society <note>The Rosicrucians were a legendary secret brotherhood of alchemists and sages; Nicolai argued they were the true precursors to the Freemasons</note> from which Freemasonry originated. In the second part of his *Essay* (p. 193), he repeats this with the addition that Ashmole was a zealous Rosicrucian at the very time he was admitted into the Society of Freemasons.

rthermore, as **Mr. Nicolai** has in part proven himself, that Gnostic ideas have continuously persisted and are still present today: so the conclusion that the **Freemasons** <note>original: "Freymaurer"</note> borrowed these symbols from the **Rosicrucians** <note>original: "Rosenkreuzer"</note> is not cogent enough; because it is just as possible that they obtained them from the **Gnostics**, from whom the **Rosicrucians** may also have retrieved them.

They borrowed the depiction of these from their own craft, just as the coats of arms of all other tradesmen also originated. The weavers carry a shuttle, the tailors a pair of scissors, and so on, as everyone can see from the signs that they hang partly in front of their dwellings and partly in front of the house where the **guild** <note>original: "Zunft"</note> assembles.

--- Page 126 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial symbols (sun, moon, stars) in Freemasonry, transitioning from a practical explanation of builders' working hours to a critique of Friedrich Nicolai’s alchemical interpretations involving Paracelsus and the "Archaeus."</meta>

also not far-fetched; they strike everyone's eye of their own accord and are used on countless occasions. They were intended to signify the diligence of the builders <note>original: "Bauleute"</note> not only by day and in the open air, but also at night; when the day's work was over, they were to occupy themselves in the evening with sketches, drawings, and the like. What if the **flaming star** <note>A prominent symbol in Masonic lodges, often representing divine providence or the light of reason.</note> were nothing other than the evening and morning star, indicating that the builders should begin their work early in the morning and conclude it late in the evening? Yet further below, I will indicate another way in which one might have come to adopt the sun, moon, stars, and even a sphere as symbols. I should also mention, however, that **Mr. Nicolai** considers the flaming star to be **Mercury**. To **Paracelsus** and his followers (p. 214), **Mercury** was the **Archaeus** <note>In the system of Paracelsus, the Archaeus is the "internal chemist" or vital force that governs the functions of life.</note>, the *ruling spirit* <note>original: "Spiritus rector"</note>, the *vivifying force* <note>original: "vis vivificans"</note> which, according to his and older philosophers' opinions, was spread throughout all of nature. It is actually upon the misunderstanding of this opinion that the daydreaming of all **hermetic philosophers** <note>A term for alchemists and seekers of secret wisdom, named after the legendary Hermes Trismegistus.</note> is based; for because they believe this Archaeus to be a self-existing thing, they think it is possible to extract it from bodies, to fix it, and to use it according to their purpose.

<meta>catchword: Well! [Wohl!]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that celestial symbols in Freemasonry likely represent the long working hours of practical stonemasons rather than the esoteric alchemical concepts, such as the "Archaeus," suggested by Friedrich Nicolai and Paracelsus.</summary>

Well! But, if older philosophers held this opinion, it could have come among the Masons from that source. As is known from the Book of Constitutions <note>Refers to James Anderson’s *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), the foundational document for modern Freemasonry.</note>, and which is so consistent with the entire English constitution, these [Masons] in England have for a long time had scholars among them who were able to augment the symbols of the Masons from the treasury of their own erudition; this will seem all the less incredible when one considers how much has been inserted into Freemasonry by scholars and semi-scholars in more recent times. If the flaming star is meant to be Mercury, and yet is said to have been taken from elsewhere, it could very well have been derived from the Eleusinian Mysteries <note>The most famous of the secret religious rites of ancient Greece, held annually in honor of Demeter and Persephone.</note>. For it was not at all unnatural that, since Masons boasted of a secret (**Mystery**), scholars took this opportunity to recall the ancient mysteries. And who is unaware of how often in recent times Freemasonry has been compared with the ancient mysteries? Now, however, according to the testimony of Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 263–339 AD), a historian of the early Christian Church whose works often described pagan rituals.</note> (See: **Characteristics of the Ancient Mysteries, large octavo, Frankfurt and Leipzig, p. 147.**), the sun, the moon, and Mercury were also present in the Eleusinian Mysteries; the torch-bearer was like the sun, the altar-priest like the moon, and the herald like

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Masonic symbols and their origins. ...Mercury was adorned. There are several more such similarities.

Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a prominent Berlin writer and bookseller of the Enlightenment who published influential theories on the origins of Freemasonry.</note>, it is true, does not want the word **Mystery** translated as "secret," but rather as "guild" (Part 1, p. 297). He is not entirely wrong. In English, *Mastery* or *Mistery* means mastery, craft, or guild, and because of the similarity in pronunciation, these two words could easily be confused with *Mystery* (secret). However, one must very often translate it as "secret" if one wishes to make sense of the text; for example, when the *Book of Constitutions* <note>The foundational rulebook of modern Freemasonry, first published by James Anderson in 1723.</note> says (p. 289) that Queen Elizabeth had heard that the Masons had certain secrets <note>original: *Geheimniße*</note> that could not be revealed to her; and because all secret societies were suspicious to her, she intended to destroy the Grand Lodge at York.

In reality, the actual masons did already have a secret at that time. Besides the fact that they made a secret out of their small amount of science <note>Refers to the technical and geometric knowledge that medieval stonemasons kept within their craft to protect their livelihood.</note>, as I have shown above, they also had a kind of password <note>original: *Spruch*</note> by which strangers identified themselves as genuine guild-fellows. Certain prescribed movements of the body or other signs could also be linked to such a password. A traveling journeyman <term>Gesell: a "fellow" or craftsman who has completed an apprenticeship but is not yet a master</term> who wanted work or provisions <note>original: *Zehrung*; money or food given to traveling craftsmen.</note> had to [undergo] a formal...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the secret traditions and passwords used by medieval craft guilds, specifically comparing these historical "misteries" or trade secrets to the rituals of Freemasonry.</meta>

...recite a motto or greeting composed in ancient times without any modification. This was also common in Germany, as proven by the Imperial Decree <note>original: *Reichsschluß* — a formal decision or law passed by the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire.</note> of June (22 or) 28, 1731, regarding the abolition of craft abuses, where it states among other things:

> „ Item <note>A Latin term used in legal documents to mean 'likewise' or 'similarly.'</note> when discharging <term>Loszählung: the formal ceremony marking the end of an apprenticeship and the promotion to journeyman.</term> apprentices, craftsmen have all sorts of strange customs, such as planing, grinding, preaching, 'baptizing' as they call it, putting on unusual clothes, parading them through the streets, or sending them around, and the like; Likewise, they adhere so strictly to their craft-greetings, silly phrases, and other such absurd things that anyone who fails by even a single word or iota in reciting or relating them must immediately submit to a certain fine, continue wandering, or even frequently run back a long way to the place he came from to obtain the greeting correctly; no less do the craftsmen use certain formulas in their birth certificates and other references <term>Kundschaften: formal certificates of conduct and skill that traveling journeymen were required to carry to find work.</term>, which contain clauses that are partly irrational and superfluous, and partly contrary to the laws and the Imperial Constitutions <note>original: *Reichs-Constitutionibus*</note>, etc. „

All these and many other abuses are now prohibited in the Imperial Decree. In particular, this also applies to the greeting, in place of which the official certificate is mandated;

--- Page 130 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the 1731 Imperial Decree aimed at abolishing irregular craft guild practices. The previous page ended with a mention of the official certificates being mandated by the decree, and this page completes that thought.</meta>

is <note>The sentence completes the thought from the previous page: "...the official certificate is mandated [is/will be]."</note>; although regardless of this, many abuses still remain here and there; as I myself remember from my younger years having heard such greetings.

For nowadays, one no longer makes such a great secret of this as in former times, when little was written and no letters of apprenticeship or certificates of conduct <term>Lehrbriefe and Kundschaften: formal documents proving a craftsman's training and legal standing; without these, secret passwords were the only proof of identity.</term> were issued. In those days, there was no other means available to recognize people of the trade than certain formulas and figures of speech that were made known to no one else. From this came certain words, like the watchword <note>original: *Parole* — a military password used to distinguish friend from foe.</note> among soldiers, and the sacred words and passwords among the Freemasons. From this came the sayings and greetings; and to be even more certain, the foreign journeymen were examined and presented with all sorts of questions related to the trade, which were partly couched in a riddling manner, so that no one could easily answer them except he who had properly learned their meaning beforehand. Such questions are still common here and there today; they were also customary among the masons in England during the time of Henry VI, as shown above; and from these the Freemasons received them, expanding and embellishing them after their own fashion; and this is the origin of the so-called catechisms <term>Catechisms: a series of questions and answers used to instruct and test initiates in the "mysteries" of their craft.</term>.

Remnants of this can even still be found among today's masons. The former legitimize themselves solely through their certificate of apprenticeship <term>Lehrbrief</term> as masons; the others through a very secret greeting. The customs during their admission as apprentices, journeymen, and masters are partly shared; however, the **Greeting-masons** have their own special ceremonies of which the others learn nothing. These **Greeting-masons** currently stand under three German main lodges <term>Haupthütten: the central administrative lodges that governed the stonemasons' guilds</term>, or highest lodges (as they are called shortly before on pages 107 and 105; though the letter-writer has not named them), and they might well have previously stood under the **Strasbourg lodge**, and only subjected themselves to the current ones since the Imperial Diet’s decree of 1707 <term>Reichstags-Schluß: a legislative resolution of the Holy Roman Empire</term> (which will become clearer further below). They consider themselves a good deal better than the **Letter-masons** and attribute to themselves a relationship with the Freemasons."

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the divisions within the masonry trade, specifically focusing on how secret oral traditions were replaced by written documentation and how this relates to the origins of the term "Freemason."</meta>

unusual among craftsmen. Every trade thus invented greetings and signs through which a journeyman <term>Gesell: a craftsman who has completed an apprenticeship but is not yet a master</term> could identify himself to another journeyman (and why not also to a master?) to prove he was a properly trained guild craftsman; and these were treated as the greatest secret. In more recent times, these secrets were lost through the introduction of travel certificates <term>Kundschaften: official written documents issued by a guild to prove a journeyman's identity and employment history</term>. The masonry trade retained these secrets the longest; indeed, a certain type of them, who still call themselves **Word-masons** <term>Wortmaurer: masons who rely on secret oral passwords rather than written papers</term> to this day, still possess such a secret by which they recognize each other as genuine Word-masons; and they must never utter their greetings nor their verbal signs except behind closed doors. They therefore have no need for a written certificate, but rather travel under the protection of these secrets in both **England** and **Germany**. Just as in **Germany** the masonry profession has split into two types—namely the Word-masons and **Letter-masons** <term>Schriftmaurer: those who identify themselves via written documents</term>, as they call themselves for distinction—so too do they divide in **England**: The Word-masons call themselves "free" and the Letter-masons "accepted" masons, because they have "accepted" travel certificates for their **identification**. The English designation **Free Masons** should therefore, according to true German usage, be [translated as] Word- <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with 'Word-masons' (Wortmaurer) on the next page.</note>

Regarding the proposed translation of the expression **Free and accepted Masons**, the author is mistaken, as is evident from what I have stated above on this matter. His report concerning the two classes of masons agrees with what the author of the aforementioned letters has shared; however, he uses different, though equally meaningful, designations, which are perhaps common in the region where he was writing. As far as **England** is concerned, it is not unlikely that these two classes of masons existed there as well, or perhaps still do. Nevertheless, I would have wished for the author to have provided evidence for this. For while a German can certainly judge German customs, he cannot so easily judge English ones; especially if he has not been in England himself for a sufficient length of time.

Concerning **Germany**, however, we have an authentic testimony in the previously mentioned **Imperial Decree** <term>Reichstagsschluß: a formal resolution or law passed by the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire</term> of June 28, 1731, proving that these two types of masons were common—a fact that escaped the notice of the two mentioned writers. It states therein:

> “The distinction in the masonry craft between **Greeters** <term>Grüßern: masons who identified themselves through secret oral greetings</term> and **Letter-carriers** <term>Briefträgern: masons who identified themselves using written certificates</term> shall be abolished.”

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which discussed the 1731 Imperial Decree regulating different classes of masons in Germany, the author now transitions to explaining the symbolic origins of the craft's tools.</meta>

That the masons, or to speak more specifically, a society of builders, possess more symbols than perhaps any other guild is rooted in the very nature of the matter and in the diverse tasks they undertake. The compass, the square, the level <note>original: "Bleiwage" - specifically a plumb-level used to determine horizontal surfaces</note>, the plumb bob, and the drawing board require as little explanation as the rough and hewn stone <note>the "rough ashlar" and "perfect ashlar," representing the moral development of a person</note>, the trowel, and the hammer. These are all things that are indispensable for the design and construction of a building. The society once took its name from the masons; hence, while the trowel and hammer appear, there is no axe or saw to be found—especially since there were already symbols that the carpenters could also claim as their own, to which the compass and the square preeminently belong.

Because people who must partly create designs themselves and partly work according to designs must necessarily have some knowledge of geometry, the initial letter of this word—which is as common in the **English** language as in all others—the great **G**, was included among the symbols. People who possess some mathematical knowledge soon become eager for more. Nothing stimulates the attention more than the path of the heavens: the sun, the moon, and the stars. Even common people—shepherds, for example—through their own experi-

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described how even simple people like shepherds develop their own understanding of the heavens through observation, the author now explains how these concepts were refined within the early Masonic lodges through the interaction between craftsmen and scholars.</meta>

-ence of these matters, forming some concepts that, to be sure, often border on the ridiculous. It is therefore not unbelievable that even simple craftsmen, in a society that included scholars, would have inquired about these things, and that the scholars would not have failed to provide them with some light and to correct their ideas. This is how the sun, moon, stars, and even the celestial sphere <note>the "sphere" likely refers to an armillary sphere, a model of objects in the sky used to show the movement of stars</note> came to be among the symbols. One does not need to search far for their origin, as they provided the opportunity for instruction during the time when the Freemasons still consisted of a society of masons and other builders.

In any case, during the time of Jones <note>Inigo Jones (1573–1652), the famous English architect whom Masonic tradition often credits with introducing Palladian architecture to the craft</note>, who lived under King James I <note>original: "König Jacob I."</note>, and from whom the instruction in the lodges—if not originated—was at least improved, as the previously mentioned passage from the Book of Constitutions <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch" - likely referring to James Anderson's 1723 "The Constitutions of the Free-Masons"</note> (p. 322) indicates, both astronomy <note>original: "Sternkunst"</note> and even astrology <note>original: "Sterndeuterey"</note> were held in high regard. Is it any wonder, then, if people were fond of speaking about these things, or were eager to learn something of them!

The so-called indented tassel <note>original: "zakichte Quaste" - in English Masonic tradition, this is the "tessellated border" or "indented tassel" that surrounds the tracing board</note> is a mere ornament; since such tassels are used to draw up curtains, it could signify roughly that for the person being admitted as a member of the society, many things which were previously unknown to him and, as it were, covered by a curtain,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Masonic symbols, specifically finishing a thought about the "indented tassel" (tessellated border) mentioned on the previous page, and transitioning into a historical account of the transition from operative to speculative masonry in the mid-17th century.</meta>

In architectural drawings <note>original: "Rissen" – refers to technical plans or sketches</note>, all sorts of decorations are placed in the so-called cartouches <note>ornamental frames or tablets, often used in masonry and cartography to enclose text or symbols</note>, which, while often relevant, do not necessarily have a direct connection to the items depicted within them. Perhaps the tassel also has its origin there; at least it was well-suited for this purpose. In short! All the symbols named so far are as simple and natural as possible, taken from the art and the occupations of builders and masons, and are so easy to explain that one must wonder how anyone could search for great secrets behind them, or attempt to derive them from afar, from ancient philosophers and so forth. However, it will be shown further below for what reasons people later hit upon these ideas.

At the time when Ashmole <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), a celebrated English antiquary and politician who is one of the first recorded "speculative" or non-working masons initiated into a lodge</note> was admitted, in the year 1646, and perhaps until the death of Charles I, who was executed in 1649, the society of Freemasons was nothing more than the great guild of actual free masons. This guild differed from the common masons, who did not belong to it, not in the nature of their work, but rather by the fact that they held certain privileges. The fact that people from other social classes were already among them does not change this; no more than the current Butchers' Guild <note>original: "Mezgerkunft"</note> in London ceases to be a butch-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of whether a guild loses its character when prominent non-craftsmen join it, using the example of Minister Pitt in the Butchers' Guild. But now the question arises whether, shortly after the death of Charles I <note>King of England, executed in 1649 during the English Civil War</note>, certain changes occurred; for there is no lack of writers who claim that Freemasonry <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> either originated then as a society entirely distinct from actual masonry <note>original: "Maurern" — refers to operative or "working" stonemasons</note>, or at least, if it already existed before, was transformed into a different shape and adopted a different purpose. The former is asserted by the anonymous author of the previously cited work: *Clarification on Important Subjects in Masonry*; the latter, however, by Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a prominent Berlin Enlightenment writer and bookseller who wrote extensively on the history of secret societies</note>—though in such a way that he distinguishes the current constitution from it and derives the latter only from later times.

According to Mr. Nicolai, after the death of Charles I, there was an attempt to carry out a political objective through Freemasonry. This objective was abandoned as no longer necessary after the Restoration <note>The 1660 restoration of the Stuart monarchy</note>, when his son Charles II became king; however, at the time, it was of great importance and necessarily brought about many changes. According to him, Freemasonry was already a secret society, entirely distinct from [actual] masonry...

society; but now, nevertheless, many things were introduced that were not there before. Although the former assertion, as I have shown, remains unproven, the latter could still be true: that even within a <term>Masons' society</term> <note>original: "Maurergesellschaft"; here referring to the operative guilds of working stonemasons</note>, specifically because of political intentions, many changes were made at that time. Consequently, from this point forward, <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> would have to be regarded as a society entirely distinct from <term>operative masonry</term> <note>original: "Maurerey"</note>. In this respect, the perspective of Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai, a Berlin Enlightenment author who proposed that Freemasonry was a political tool for the restoration of the English monarchy</note>, and the information he may have provided, could still be worthy of all attention, as indeed they truly are.

According to him, the members of the Freemasons' society were entirely against the Parliament and were of a Royalist mind <note>Supporters of the monarchy, known as Cavaliers, during the English Civil War</note>. After the death of Charles I <note>The English King executed in 1649</note>, the intentions of Cromwell <note>Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658), who led the Parliamentary forces and later became Lord Protector</note> came increasingly to light. The party faithful to the Royal House united even more closely, and for their frequent meetings, they chose, among others, the already Royalist-leaning Freemasons' society. Various people of high rank had themselves admitted into it because, under the guise of this already well-known society, they could assemble without suspicion (Vol. 1, p. 199). Their intention was the advancement of the re- <note>The word breaks off here; the likely intended word is "Wiederherstellung" or "Restoration" of the monarchy</note>

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Friedrich Nicolai’s research into the historical origins of Freemasonry. It specifically addresses why Nicolai may have hesitated to fully develop his arguments and his reluctance to acknowledge existing Masonic literature. It then transitions into the ritual of the Master Mason degree, linking it to the mourning of a slain figure, likely referring to the legend of Hiram Abiff which Nicolai connected to the execution of Charles I.</meta>

to see what is actually right before one's eyes. Even if many had seen it, they did not find it advisable to make their discoveries publicly known. **Mr. Nicolai** <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a Berlin bookseller and prominent Enlightenment critic</note> himself merely tossed out the proof without fully elaborating on or applying it. Presumably, he felt some hesitation in doing so, lest he be considered a babbler; although, in truth, he had already said too much by then. I have set aside this hesitation, and I will justify myself for doing so further below. Granted, **Mr. Nicolai** could have referred to several already printed books to give his proof the proper clarity and strength. But in doing so, he would have had to admit that at least some truths were to be found in these books. Thus, he would have acted against the generally accepted <term>maxim</term> <note>original: "Maxime"; here referring to a general rule of thumb among the fraternity</note>—which I have already mentioned above—by virtue of which we declare everything written about **Freemasonry** to be false; and he did not want to do that.

history outside of the Bible. It is said that the narrative was taken from the **Talmud** <note>The central text of Rabbinic Judaism. During the initiation of a **Master**, a coffin is depicted on the floor of the **Lodge**, or on what is called a **carpet** <note>Also known as a "Tracing Board," these were illustrated cloths or boards used to teach the symbolic meanings of the degree.</note>. Above it is an opened **compass**, below a **square**, and beside it the tears shed over the death of the slain Master, **Hiram**, are depicted in great quantity and in an almost ridiculous manner. Not far from the coffin, a type of mound is drawn, which is meant to represent the heap of rubble under which the murderers had hidden the slain man. On this mound is stuck a **sprig of acacia** <note>In Masonic tradition, the acacia represents the immortality of the soul and was used to mark Hiram’s shallow grave.</note>.

The candidate is brought into the room and positioned and led about in such a way that he can see neither the coffin nor the faces of the **Masters** standing around it. Instead, he hears constant sobbing and groaning. Finally, he takes him by the fist, lifts him up, and places him in a certain position, in which he says into his right ear: Mac, and into his left ear: Benac; he also removes from his face the cloth sprinkled with blood-colored dye. Following this, the **Worshipful Master** explains to him what all this is meant to signify, and interprets it for him using the story of the murdered architect **Hiram** <term>Hiram: Hiram Abiff, the legendary chief architect of King Solomon's Temple, whose death and "raising" form the central allegory of the Master Mason degree</term>; and thus he learns the Master’s word <term>Master’s word: a secret password used to identify those who have reached the third and highest degree of Craft Masonry</term> **Mac-Benac** <note>These words are traditional Masonic passwords, often thought to be derived from Hebrew, though their exact translation is a subject of much debate among Masonic scholars.</note>. <meta>This page continues the bibliographical investigation into the dating and textual history of anti-Masonic "exposures." ...ever since the Battle of Dettingen <note>The Battle of Dettingen took place during the War of the Austrian Succession; it was the last time a British monarch, George II, personally led troops into battle.</note>, which occurred on June 19, 1743, is mentioned in this work (p. 14), it can at the very least not have been composed any earlier than that date.

On this occasion, I must mention that the new publication: ***Secret Undertakings of the Freemasons*** <note>original: "Geheime Unternehmungen der Freymaurer"</note>, octavo, London and Berlin 1787, is the very same work that has long been known under the title: ***The Shattered Freemasons*** <note>original: "die zerschmetterten Freymaurer"; a translation of the French *Les Francs-Maçons écrasés* (1747)</note>. The latter half is a literal reprint, though occasionally something is omitted—for example, on page 150, an entire period <note>In 18th-century grammar, a "period" refers to a complete sentence or a complex rhetorical thought.</note> is missing:

> "Just as we were speaking of the Masonic system of instruction, we already stated that they understood **Liberty and Equality** under the name of Solomon’s Temple" 
(***The Shattered Freemasons***, p. 272.)

This has been left out, so that the entirety of what follows makes absolutely no sense. The first half is also taken from that same source; however, occasionally things are omitted, a few items are added, but mainly the order of the passages is changed. This is the primary artifice <note>original: "Kunstgriff"; a clever trick or ruse used by the editor</note> of the editor, by which he seeks to conceal that he has also copied much of it word-for-word. This recently mentioned "Complete Collection of All Adonhiramite Masonry" was published in octavo in Leipzig, 1786, by Adam Friedrich Böhmen; it is a translation of the first part of the ***Precious Collection of Adonhiramite Masonry*** <note>original: "Recueil precieux de la Maçonnerie Adonhiramite", 1785.</note>. In this work, Adoniram (from whom the title is derived) is also held to be the one who was the Chief Architect of the Temple. Throughout this work, the name is written with an "h" in the middle <note>i.e., Adonhiram instead of Adoniram.</note>, which is an error that does not appear in the previously cited books, but is committed throughout the present one. In that work, the translation found in the **Book of Constitutions** was retained unchanged, even though it is not always accurate. But because the English original is rare in Germany, it is fortunate that it was also printed in the aforementioned translation by **Bonneville**.)

**Q.** How were you received as a Master?

**A.** By passing from the square to the compass over the grave of our worshipful master Adonhiram <note>While most Masonic traditions name Hiram Abiff as the central figure, the "Adonhiramite" tradition identifies the master as Adonhiram.</note>.

**Q.** What did you see when you were admitted into the Master’s Lodge?

**A.** I was not permitted to look around; I heard only sighs and lamentations (deep sighs; in the French it says merely *groans* <note>original: "Gemissements"</note>) — Now many more questions and answers follow; then a "key to all lodges" is discussed, which consists of the knowledge of the signs, words, and touches <note>The "touches" or "grips" refer to the secret handshakes used to recognize members of each degree.</note> of the three degrees, after which, following several more questions, there follows:

**Q.** Why do you wear the key on your left side?

**A.** By this I mean to say that it is contained within my heart, wherein all the secrets of our Order are locked away, and because it reminds me of the position in which the body of Adonhiram was found, whose left arm was outstretched, while the right formed a square, representing the breast-sign <note>A ritual gesture where the arm is positioned to form a right angle, symbolizing the "square" of morality.</note>.

**Q.** For what purpose have you come here?

**A.** To seek the lost Master's Word <note>The "Master’s Word" is the central secret of the third degree of Freemasonry, symbolizing divine truth or lost wisdom.</note>.

**Q.** How was the Master's Word lost?

**A.** Through three heavy blows.

**Q.** What do these three heavy blows signify?

**A.** They are those which our worshipful master received when he was murdered at the door of the Temple by three fellow crafts <note>In Masonic tradition, "fellow crafts" are the second rank of workers, below masters but above apprentices.</note>, who wished to rob him of either the Master's Word or his life.

Note: For a better understanding, I must insert the following narrative here. Hiram <note>While the previous page referred to "Adonhiram," the text here uses the more common name "Hiram," referring to the master architect of King Solomon's Temple.</note> had oversight over the construction of the Temple, and had to pay such a large number of workers that he could not know them all personally. In order not to run the risk of paying an apprentice as a fellow craft, or a fellow craft as a master, he agreed with them upon certain words and grips <note>These "grips" or secret handshakes allowed workers to prove their rank silently to the paymaster.</note>. The word of the apprentice was (and still is) Jachin, the word of the fellow craft (as it still is) Boas <note>Jachin and Boas were the names of the two great bronze pillars at the entrance of Solomon’s Temple.</note>, and the word of the master was Jehovah <note>original: "Jehovah"</note>. **Q.** How did one learn that it was Fellow Crafts <note>The second grade of Freemasonry, situated between the Entered Apprentice and the Master Mason.</note> who had committed this crime?

**A.** Through the general assembly of the workmen, where three Fellow Crafts were missing.

**Q.** But since the [Master’s] word was lost, how could it be found again?

**A.** Since the Masters suspected the murder of Adonhiram and feared that the intensity of the pain might have forced the Master’s Word <note>The secret password used by Master Masons to identify their rank and receive their wages.</note> from him; they agreed with one another that the first word spoken upon the rediscovery of the body should serve them in the future as a sign to recognize one another. The same occurred regarding the sign and the touch <note>original: "Berührung." In Masonic ritual, this refers to the specific handshakes or "grips" used for identification.</note>; (which were also changed. I have here, as in several other places, slightly altered the translation to bring it closer to the original.)

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the catechism or instructional dialogue regarding the Masonic legend of the search for the murdered architect Adonhiram. The previous page ended with the decision to change the Master’s Word and signs upon finding the body.</meta>

**Q.** How many Masters were sent out to search for Adonhiram?

**A.** Nine, who are represented by the nine candlesticks (which stand in the Lodge <note>original: "Loge." The meeting place or the body of Freemasons.</note>, always in groups of three).

**Q.** Where was the body of our venerable Master found?

**A.** Under a heap of rubble of approximately nine cubic feet <note>original: "Cubickschuhen." A historical unit of measurement; the "foot" varied by region but was generally around 30 centimeters.</note>, upon which an acacia branch <note>original: "Acacienzweig." The acacia is a central symbol in Masonic ritual, representing both the location of the grave and the concept of immortality.</note> was planted.

**Note.** Because the scene is set in the East <note>original: "Orient."</note>, from whence the acacia tree—commonly called the Egyptian pod-thorn <term>Schotendorn: likely referring to the *Acacia nilotica* or a similar thorny species</term>, for there are several species of it—has come to us, a branch of this tree was chosen. Originally, there was nothing more behind it, although later, after people lapsed into all kinds of mystical interpretations, it was claimed that this was a prototype <note>original: "Vorbild."</note> for the cross of Christ, which was supposedly made from such a tree; however, this cannot be proven.

**Q.** What purpose was this branch intended to serve?

**A.** As a marker for the villains <note>original: "Bösewichtern." Referring to the three "unfaithful" Fellow Crafts who murdered the Master architect.</note> to recognize the location where they had hidden Adonhiram’s body, which they intended to move to a more distant place.

<summary>This page describes the ritualistic search for Adonhiram's body, its discovery under an acacia branch, and provides a critical historical note dismissing the mystical association between the acacia and the True Cross.</summary>
<keywords>Adonhiram, nine Masters, nine candlesticks, acacia branch, Orient, pod-thorn, Cross of Christ, villains, cubic feet, Masonic ritual</keywords>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>Continuity: This page continues the instructional catechism of the Master Mason degree. Following the discovery of Adonhiram’s body marked by an acacia branch, the dialogue now explains the burial and the resulting ritual signs and tokens.</meta>

**Q.** What was done with the body of our venerable Master?

**A.** Solomon <note>King Solomon of Israel, who according to Masonic tradition was one of the three original Grand Masters.</note> had him buried in the Sanctuary <note>original: "Heiligthum." Referring to the Holy of Holies, the innermost and most sacred part of the Temple.</note> of the Temple, and had a gold medal in the shape of a triangle placed upon his grave, on which the old Master’s Word **Jehovah** <note>original: "Jehovah." The Tetragrammaton, or the four-letter Hebrew name of God.</note> was engraved, which in Hebrew signifies the Highest Being. —

**Q.** What are the distinguishing marks of a Master?

**A.** A sign, a touch <note>original: "Berührung." In a Masonic context, this refers to a specific grip or handshake used as a secret mode of recognition.</note>, two words, and the five perfect points of Mastership <term>five perfect points: Known in English Masonry as the "Five Points of Fellowship," these refer to ritualized physical contact points used during the communication of the Master's Word.</term>.

**Q.** Give me the sign.

*Instead of an answer, the sign is performed.* (This sign is of such a nature that it can hardly be made clear through words. Nor is it necessary, as these and some following matters are placed here merely for the sake of context.)

**Q.** What do you call it?

**A.** The Sign of Horror <note>original: "Zeichen des Schröckens (des Entsezens)." Also referred to as the Sign of Help or the Sign of Grief and Distress.</note>.

**Q.** Why?

**A.** Because it expresses the horror that overcame the Masters when they caught sight of the body of Adonhiram <note>Adonhiram is the name used for the master architect in certain continental European Masonic rites, such as the Adonhiramite Rite, where he is sometimes distinct from or substituted for Hiram Abiff.</note>.

<summary>This page describes the burial of the master architect Adonhiram within the Temple's Sanctuary and explains the symbolic origin of the "Sign of Horror" used by Master Masons.</summary>
<keywords>Solomon, Adonhiram, Jehovah, Temple Sanctuary, Sign of Horror, Master Mason degree, five perfect points, Masonic burial, ritual signs</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional catechism of the Master Mason degree, moving from the discovery of Adonhiram’s body to the specific ritual tokens (grips), words, and physical signs that identify a Master Mason.</meta>

**Question:** Give the touch <note>original: "Berührung." A specific grip or handshake used for recognition between members.</note> to the first brother (the Senior Warden) <term>Senior Warden: In German, "Oberaufseher." The second-highest-ranking officer in a Masonic lodge, responsible for supervising the craft during their labors.</term>.

*It is given. (Describing it is unnecessary for my purpose.)*

**Question:** Give him the sacred word.

*It is given to him as is in accordance with the order. (It is found neither in the translation nor in the original, but is called **Mac Benac**, and is given at the same time as the touch, so that **Mac** is whispered into the right ear and **Benac** into the left.)* <note>original: "Mac Benac." A variation of "Mah-Hah-Bone," the traditional word of a Master Mason. Ritual tradition suggests these words are whispered during the "Five Points of Fellowship."</note>

**Question:** What does this word mean?

**Answer:** The flesh leaves the bones. <note>This refers to the state of Adonhiram's body when it was discovered and raised from the grave in the degree's central allegory.</note>

**Question:** What is the password? <note>original: "Mot de passe" (French); also referred to in German as "Losungswort."</note>

**Answer:** **Sublime**, which is an epithet <note>original: "Zunahme," likely a variation of "Zuname," meaning a surname or title of honor.</note> that has been bestowed upon our venerable Master. <note>In Freemasonry, the Master Mason degree is often referred to as the "Sublime Degree."</note>

**Question:** What are the five perfect points of the Master's degree? <term>five perfect points: Known in English as the "Five Points of Fellowship," these are specific physical contact points—such as hand-to-hand or foot-to-foot—symbolizing the bond of brotherhood.</term>

**Answer:** The foot-sign, the bending of the knee, the joining of both right hands, the left arm on the shoulder, and the kiss of peace. —

<meta>This page continues the instructional catechism of the Master Mason degree, focusing on the symbolic architecture of the lodge and the traditional names and signs used for mutual aid and identification.</meta>

**Question:** By what is the Master’s lodge supported?

**Answer:** By three great and triangular pillars, which are called **Wisdom**, **Strength**, and **Beauty**. —

**Question:** What is a Master called?

**Answer:** **Gabaon**, which is the name of the place where the Israelites placed the Ark of the Covenant <term>Ark of the Covenant: A sacred chest described in the Bible containing the stone tablets of the Ten Commandments.</term> in times of unrest. (In many lodges, only "Gabaon" is given as the answer, without adding the explanation.) —

**Note:** The place is called **Gibeon** in 2 Chronicles 1:3, and there the Vulgate <note>original: "Vulgata." The 4th-century Latin translation of the Bible.</note> has **Gabaon**; the Greek <note>original: "Griechische." Referring to the Septuagint translation.</note> and French translations write it the same way; the English, however, has **Gibeon**. It is also called **Gibea** in 1 Samuel 7:3 and 2 Samuel 6:3, 4.

**Question:** What would you do if you found yourself in danger?

**Answer:** I would make the sign of help <term>sign of help: A secret physical gesture used to signal a need for assistance from fellow Masons.</term> and cry out: **Children of the widow, come to my aid!**

**Note:** This is also called the sign of distress <note>original: "Nothzeichen."</note> and it can be clearly described with words as little as the others, which is also not required here.

**Question:** Why do you say: Children of the widow?

<meta>This page continues the Master Mason degree catechism, providing the answer to the final question of the previous page regarding the "Children of the widow." It then shifts into a commentary on the nature of Masonic catechisms and the ritual use of "work" within the lodge.</meta>

A. Because after the death of our venerable Master <note>Likely referring to Hiram Abiff, the central figure in the Master Mason degree, though the text here names him Adonhiram.</note>, the Masons took care of his mother, who was a widow, and called themselves her children, because Adonhiram <note>Adonhiram is a figure in Masonic lore often identified with the master architect of Solomon's Temple.</note> had always regarded them as his brothers. —

Before I go further, I must say something more about the so-called catechisms, from which the Worshipful Master <term>Worshipful Master: The presiding officer and head of a Masonic lodge.</term> presents more or fewer questions to be answered before the closing of a lodge, which the Senior and Junior Wardens <term>Wardens: The two principal officers of a lodge after the Master; they assist in its governance and the supervision of the laborers.</term> answer. Such questions are also posed at the opening of every lodge session, even the Table Lodge <term>Table Lodge: A formal Masonic banquet involving ritual toasts and specific protocols.</term>, as well as at its closing, where, however, only a few are raised, which can be found in the cited collection. The so-called instruction consists of these questions and answers and is commonly given during the meal. In the strict sense, this is called **working**; for in a broader sense, everything that happens in the lodge is called so; even eating and drinking.

The first catechisms were, in all probability, brought into the **public** <note>original: "Publicum." Latin for the general public.</note> against the will of the Order. They also do not coincide completely, as can be seen from ***Masonry Dissected*** <note>A famous 1730 "exposure" of Masonic ritual published by Samuel Prichard.</note>, ***The Betrayed Freemason*** <note>original: "dem verrathenen Freymaurer." Likely referring to 'L'Ordre des Francs-Maçons trahi', a French exposure from 1745 that was widely translated.</note>, and so forth; partly because the publishers were not always accurately enough informed

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the unreliability of printed Masonic "exposures" and catechisms. The sentence begins mid-thought from the previous page, explaining why early ritual texts differed from one another.</meta>

were, partly because there were indeed variations here and there. The catechisms in the aforementioned collection are the most accurate; yet I have heard some things slightly differently, and other things not at all in the lodges I have attended. Because the Worshipful Masters <term>Worshipful Master: The elected leader of a Masonic lodge.</term> themselves were often not familiar with everything, did not know all the questions and answers, and could not always distinguish exactly whether something belonged to the Apprentice <term>Apprentice: The first degree of Freemasonry, representing a beginner in the craft.</term> or to the Fellow Craft degree <term>Fellow Craft: The second degree of Freemasonry, representing a craftsman who has completed his basic training.</term>, they often initiated a newcomer as both an Apprentice and a Fellow Craft at the same time in the same lodge meeting, and thus mixed both degrees. This happened to me as well, when I was made an Apprentice and a Fellow Craft at the same time in France. Thus, the said Worshipful Masters began to use the catechisms published by others themselves, and to correct what they considered incorrect while they were conducting the questioning.

The author of the collection complains about such incorrect catechisms and reports (p. 89) that the first catechism printed in France under the title *Catechism of the Freemasons, or the Secret of the Masons* <note>original: "Catechisme des Francs-Maçons ou le Secret des Maçons"</note> was from the year 1744. (I have not seen it, but by all appearances, it is the one mentioned in the preface of *The Order of Freemasons Betrayed* <note>original: "Ordre des Francs-Maçons trahi"</note>,

where the author reports that it was not entirely reliable, which is why he only extracted what was good in it, but improved the rest and also added several things.) The other edition is from 1747. The editor, who is thought to be an Abbé <note>Abbé: A title for a lower-ranking member of the Catholic clergy in France; here referring to Louis Travenol, who used the pseudonym Leonard Gabanon.</note>, signed his name as *Leonard Gabanon*. Since then, several works have appeared under various names, but they were merely imitations of the first. What he finds most surprising is that this work, which deserves only general contempt, is considered by many Masons to be the best of its kind.

Now I also wish to share the story of the slain master <note>This refers to the legend of Hiram Abiff, the central allegory of the Master Mason degree, who is said to have been murdered by three ruffians for refusing to reveal the secrets of a Master Mason.</note> from *Masonry Dissected* <note>Published by Samuel Prichard in 1730, this was one of the most famous early "exposures" of Masonic ritual.</note>, as it is the oldest writing of this kind, and likewise in such a way that I omit what is unnecessary for my purpose and translate more correctly here and there. Q. Where are you going?

A. Toward the West.

Q. What do you intend to do there?

A. To seek that which was lost, and has been found again.

Q. What is that, which was lost and found again?

A. The Master Mason's Word.

Q. How was it lost?

A. Through three great blows, or the death of our Master Hiram <note>Hiram Abiff is the central figure in Masonic allegory, a master architect said to have been murdered for refusing to reveal secret passwords.</note>.

Q. How did it happen that he was slain?

A. During the building of Solomon's Temple <term>Solomon's Temple: The biblical temple in Jerusalem, which serves as the central setting for Masonic ritual and moral instruction.</term>, he was the Master Mason, and at twelve o'clock noon, when the people had gone away to refresh themselves, he came, according to his custom, and inspected the work. When he entered the Temple, three villains were there, presumably three Fellows <term>Fellows: Also known as Fellow Crafts; in the ritual, these represent masons of the second degree who conspired to extort the secrets of the third degree.</term>, who had stationed themselves at the three entrances of the Temple. As he wished to go out, one of them demanded the Master's Word <note>The "Word" is a secret password used to identify a Master Mason and entitle him to higher wages.</note> from him. He replied that he could not obtain it in such a manner, but in time and with a little

**A.** At 12 o'clock at night, when the people were at rest.

**Q.** What did they do with him afterward?

**A.** They carried him to the summit of the hill, where they made a proper grave and buried him.

**Q.** When was he missed?

**A.** On that very same day.

**Q.** When was he found?

**A.** Fifteen days later.

**Q.** Who found him?

**A.** Fifteen compassionate brothers <note>In Masonic tradition, these are often identified as Fellowcrafts sent by the King to locate the missing Master Architect.</note> went out from the western door of the Temple at the command of King Solomon. They divided themselves to the right and left, yet in such a way that one could call out to the others. They agreed that if they did not find the [Master's] word upon or near him, the first word spoken [upon discovery] should become the Master's word <note>This refers to the "Substituted Word" in Freemasonry, as the true Master's Word was believed lost with Hiram's death.</note>. One of the brothers, who had become tired sooner than the others, sat down to rest; he grasped a shrub <note>This shrub is traditionally identified as a sprig of acacia, a symbol of immortality in Masonic lore.</note> which gave way, and he called out to his brothers. They examined the place and found him properly buried in a neat grave, which was six feet toward the east, six feet toward the west, and six feet deep. Its covering was green moss and turf, at which they marveled and said: *The moss of the house of God is by grace* <note>original Latin: "Muscus domus Dei gratia." This appears to be a form of "Masonic Latin" used as a mnemonic or ritual phrase.</note>, which according to Masonry means: Thanks be to God,

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the description of the Master Mason ritual (the burial of Hiram Abiff) to a critical analysis of its meaning. The author expresses skepticism toward a literal interpretation of the legend, proposing instead a political-allegorical reading related to the House of Stuart.</meta>

The variations are, as one can see, in-
significant. But what is the point of this whole sto-
ry? If one takes it literally, one must
believe that the people who invented it
intended to perform a **farce** <note>original: "Possenspiel," referring to a low-brow comedy or slapstick play.</note>:
for whom could the death of a
master slain during the construction of the Temple inter-
est, even if the matter had been true?
And how ridiculous to hold such
solemnities over the death of such a man after
three thousand years, to mourn him
with so many earnest ceremonies!
Even more ridiculous than when the ancient Egyptians
mourned the lost **Osiris** <note>The Egyptian god of the afterlife; his myth involves being murdered and his body parts being sought by his wife Isis, similar to the search for Hiram's body.</note>, and as **Seneca** <note>A Roman Stoic philosopher.</note>
mocks, sought and found what was not
lost (See ***Characteristics of the An-
cient Mysteries***, p. 169). How could sen-
sible people hope to entertain other
likewise sensible people with such a farce?
How could one make an inviol-
able secret out of it, and expose oneself to
mockery and contempt from the

<meta>This page continues the transition from describing Masonic ritual to interpreting its hidden political meanings. However, one must not extend the **allegory** to all incidental circumstances, nor attempt to apply every one of them individually to history. No! These serve only to embellish the whole and are taken from actual **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerey"</note>, because it was always in and through the **Masons' guild** <note>original: "Maurerzunft"</note> that people intended to operate; for this reason, some things that were perhaps already customary among the Masons were mixed in as well. These served as a veil, even among those whom one could not yet fully trust; therefore, the meaning of the allegory was neither stated nor explained, but merely left to reflection and guesswork. They served, as has already been mentioned, as a justification if anything should leak out—that these were merely matters concerning Masonry.

The first slain Master is therefore the unfortunate **King Charles I**; the three fellow-crafts are the rebels from the three kingdoms: **England**, **Scotland**, and **Ireland**. <meta>This page continues the interpretation of Masonic ritual as a political allegory for the Stuart Restoration. ...[...st] word is his son **Charles II**, as **Mr. Nicolai** <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811) was a famous Berlin bookseller and author who argued that Freemasonry was used by supporters of the House of Stuart to plot their return to the English throne.</note> has already explained most of it clearly enough. Evidently soon after the death of the King, and before the restoration of his son; for afterwards, no more lamentations were necessary.

All sacred and all pass-words, except for the word from **Mac Benac** <term>Mac Benac: A secret word used in the Master Mason degree. In the **Master degree**, the word was formerly **Jehovah**, which is likely still marked on the coffin <note>In the ritual for the third (Master) degree, the candidate participates in a symbolic reenactment of the death of the master builder Hiram Abiff, which involves a ritual coffin or shroud.</note>.

It was probably already customary among the actual masons <term>Actual masons: Often called "operative" masons, these were the professional stonemasons whose guild traditions and tools were adopted and given symbolic meanings by "speculative" Freemasons.</term>. For it is well known how not only Jews, but also many Christians, attributed a special power to this word, by which one could even perform miraculous things; and even now there are enough **Freemasons** who regret that the art of properly applying and using this word is no longer known; just as the Jews lament that its true pronunciation, through which one could have performed miracles, is lost.

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic analysis of the book "The Freemason Unmasked" and transitions into the central ritual legend of the Master Mason degree, explaining how the secret "Master's Word" was allegedly replaced after the murder of Hiram Abiff.</meta>

Masonry was little known, but according to Anderson <note>James Anderson (1679–1739), the Scottish minister who wrote "The Constitutions of the Free-Masons" in 1723, which established the historical framework for modern Freemasonry.</note>, it was in decline and only began to recover in 1717. Furthermore, I have the 1786 edition from Frankfurt and Leipzig before me, which has three titles: *True and Coherent System of the Society of Freemasons*; *The Freemason Unmasked, or the True Secret of the Freemasons* <note>original: "le Maçon demasqué ou le vrai Secret des Francs-Maçons"</note>; *The Discovered Mason or the True Secret of the Freemasons*. Facing the French text is a German translation. The appendix is purely German and more recent, and contains, among other things, the papal bulls of Clement XII from April 28, 1738 <note>Referring to "In eminenti apostolatus," the first papal prohibition of Freemasonry.</note>, and Benedict XIV from May 17, 1751 <note>Referring to "Providas Romanorum," which confirmed the previous ban.</note>, against the Freemasons. I have not seen an older edition.

The legend of how the aforementioned word is said to have originated is as follows: The brothers, who were tasked with searching for the slain Master <note>In Masonic tradition, this refers to Hiram Abiff, the master builder of King Solomon's Temple, who was murdered by three ruffians for refusing to reveal the secret word of a Master Mason.</note>, had agreed that the first word they would hear upon finding him should be the new Master's Word, because they feared the old, customary one had been forced from him <note>They feared his murderers had successfully extorted the original secret word, rendering it compromised.</note>. When they found him and wanted to pull him out of the heap of rubble, one of them grabbed him by a finger; however, the flesh came off and remained in his hand. He then said **Mac.** Another took him by the fist, and it happened to him

The final sentence does not explicitly state that Hiram was grasped once more, yet it certainly implies it. Current practice, as is evident from the above, agrees with the legend.

and the **Scottish Highlands**, although with some changes, it is still spoken; the word **Mac** means a son, and various families use it as a title before their name to indicate that they are descendants of men who were formerly famous and great in their country, e.g., **Mac-Donald**, **Mac-Pherson**, etc. And so this expression could very well have been used by **Prince Charles II** <note>Charles II (1630–1685). <meta>This page continues the etymological investigation into the Masonic password "Mac-Benac." The previous page established that "Mac" is derived from the Hebrew word for decay or "rot" (maq), connecting it to the state of Hiram Abiff’s body upon its discovery in Masonic legend.</meta>

rotten, he is dead and (already) decayed. The *verb* from which the *substantive* <note>In 18th-century grammar, a "substantive" is a noun.</note> is derived appears several times and means to be consumed, to wither, or to rot. This expression could not only be used regarding the deceased King **Charles I** <note>Charles I of England (1600–1649). High-degree Masonic myths in the 1700s often allegorized his execution as the death of Hiram Abiff.</note>, but it also aligns well with the legend according to which the flesh of the slain **Hiram** <note>Hiram Abiff, the central character of the Masonic Master Mason ritual, an architect of Solomon's Temple who is murdered by three ruffians.</note> was already rotten, so that it fell away from the bones during the attempted grip.

As for what the word **Benac** is supposed to mean, however, I cannot find it, whether I look at it as one word or split it into two so that the syllable **Be** represents a preposition. But perhaps it was originally not **Benac**, but **Benah**, from which the former was likely created over time for the sake of euphony and rhyme. That it was originally called **Benah** becomes probable from *Masonry Dissected* <note>Samuel Prichard's famous 1730 exposé of Masonic rituals.</note>, where it is explicitly written as such (p. 216 of the German translation of *The Jesuits Chased Out* <note>original: "Jesuites chassés," referring to Nicolas de Bonneville's 1788 work *Les Jésuites chassés de la maçonnerie*.</note>). In the earlier edition of this pamphlet, it must have already appeared this way, as it is written the same way in the previously mentioned *The Ruins* 1744 <note>original: "Ruenen," likely referring to an early Masonic publication.</note> (p. 35), in *The Burning Candlestick* 1746 <note>original: "der brennende Leuchter," a common title for early German Masonic exposés.</note> (pp. 153, 157), and in the appendix of the *Book of Constitutions* (1783, p. 577), even though in *The Traited Freemason* <note>original catchword: "trahi," referring to the 1745 French exposé *L'Ordre des Francs-Maçons Trahi*.</note>

<meta>This page continues the etymological analysis of Masonic passwords, specifically the transition from "Mac-Benac" to "Mac-Benah." *Trahi* <note>Referring to the 1744 book *L'Ordre des Francs-Maçons trahi* (The Order of Freemasons Betrayed), an early exposé of Masonic secrets.</note> also from 1744 (pp. 85, 89) already uses the name **Benac**. The explanation given by the author of *Masonry Dissected* <note>Samuel Prichard's 1730 pamphlet which revealed Masonic rituals to the public.</note> also proves that the word was originally written with an **h** at the end: For he says it means: the master builder is slain, *the builder is smitten*. However, in Hebrew, **Banah** <note>original: בָּנָה (banah)</note> means "to build." The participle of this word, which takes the place of the noun, is **Boneh** <note>original: בּוֹנֶה (boneh)</note>, and thus the word should have been called that here as well to express the meaning of a "master builder." But perhaps the inventor [of the word] was not so precise and merely remembered the root **Banah**, and through a quicker pronunciation, it became **Benah**. That the word **Mac** fits well here is self-evident. According to this derivation, **Mac-Benah** thus means: The master builder is dead, already decayed.

The inventor could easily have come upon the Hebrew word **Mac**. The generally known Scottish word **Mac**, which means a son, led him to the idea of looking up what the same word might mean in Hebrew; and this all the more so because the "Son of the Widow," **Charles II** <note>Charles II of England (1630–1685). In certain 18th-century "Stuart Masonry" theories, the "Son of the Widow" was a code for the exiled or restored Stuart kings.</note>, was commemorated in this institution. Now he found a meaning that suited the legend of the slain master; for he was dead and be-

<meta>This page continues the etymological investigation into the Masonic password "Mac-Benac." The previous page established that "Mac" likely refers to decay or rot, and that "Benah" (with an 'h') relates to the Hebrew word for "to build." This page examines the alternative spelling "Benac" and whether it might be derived from a Hebrew word for a grave or cave.</meta>

...already passed into decay when he was found.

---

But how did he come upon the other word: **Benac**; assuming that it was originally pronounced not as *Benah*, but as **Benac**? This is more difficult to explain. The Master <note>Refers to Hiram Abiff, the legendary architect of Solomon's Temple, whose death and burial are central to Masonic ritual.</note> lay under a **heap of rubble**, and according to the old legend, in a proper grave. The word **Nac** (or **Nak**) can mean a cave and consequently also a grave, as it is well known how often the grave is compared to a cave; one even finds *grave-cave* among the poets. Accordingly, it meant: the dead man, who is already decayed, lies in the grave. This fits the legend well. But I must not conceal the fact that the word **Nac** (**Nak**) does not occur in the Hebrew Bible, although a word derived from the same root, which actually means a cave, a cleft, and the like, is found therein. Secondly, that **Benac** is not written with a **k** at the end, like that Hebrew word, even though the English do have a **k** in their language. Thirdly, that it is not apparent why the inventor did not rather choose one or another more common word by which grave or cave is expressed, which naturally would have occurred to him more readily. If one were to say, it

<summary>The author analyzes the Masonic word "Benac," suggesting it might stem from a Hebrew root for "cave" or "grave" to match the legend of the Master’s burial, though he acknowledges linguistic inconsistencies.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the etymology of Masonic passwords, specifically the word "Benac." The previous page discussed the possibility of "Nac" referring to a cave or grave. This page evaluates the likelihood of these words being chosen for their rhyme or being remnants of ancient mystery traditions.</meta>

...happened for the sake of the rhyme, then one cannot see why he would have wanted a word that specifically rhymed. For in none of the sacred and passwords of the two preceding degrees <note>In Freemasonry, the first two degrees are Entered Apprentice and Fellowcraft.</note> do words occur that rhyme. Finally, it also stands in opposition that this explanation does not agree with the generally accepted one, according to which both words are supposed to mean: **the flesh leaves the bones**.

I do not wish to search in other Middle Eastern dialects <note>original: "orientalischen Dialecten"</note> in which a word might perhaps be found that could be applied here. For it is not probable that the inventor of this word understood any other dialects besides Hebrew.

I therefore do not know how to reliably explain the word: <term>Benac</term> <note>A word used in the Master Mason degree, often paired with "Mac."</note>. Perhaps it was also merely adopted arbitrarily, or arose by chance. Even in the higher degrees, words are found that seem to have arisen in this manner and cannot be explained so easily. Already in the <term>Eleusinian Mysteries</term> <note>Ancient Greek religious ceremonies and initiations held annually for the cult of Demeter and Persephone; many of their specific rituals and "secret" words remain a mystery to modern scholars.</note> one had such words, which can no longer be explained today. It is enough that even without this word, the proposition stands firm: that after the death of King Charles I <note>King of England, Scotland, and Ireland, executed in 1649. ->“In the year 1666, on the 2nd of September, the Great Fire of London occurred, and the Freemasons became very necessary for the rebuilding of the city.”<-

Where would one address the Freemasons for such a purpose nowadays? And why not? Today they are a society completely separate from the masons' guild <term>Masons’ guild: From the German "Maurerzunft," the professional organization of actual stone-workers.</term>; at that time, however, they were still a part of the large guild, whose members had already called themselves Freemasons <note>original: "Freymaurer"</note> for a long time.

During the reign of Cromwell <note>Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658), who led the Commonwealth of England as Lord Protector after the execution of King Charles I.</note>, when people within this masons' guild were also considering the restoration of the royal family, the first foundation was laid for what are now specifically called Freemasons. Naturally, one could not rely on all the actual masons and other members of the guild. For how quickly would Cromwell have discovered the intention? <meta>This page continues the argument from page 194 regarding why the secret structures of Freemasonry persisted even after the political necessity for them (likely the English Civil War and the Interregnum) had passed. The occasion for this soon arose: for since **Charles II** <note>King Charles II (r. 1660–1685). His return from exile is known as the Restoration. The text suggests that the initial "whirl of joy" at his return blinded people to the need for constitutional safeguards.</note> was initially accepted as King in a whirl of joy, almost without any conditions, wise people who were so intent on their freedoms could easily imagine that new measures would soon have to be taken; and indeed, it did not take long before discontent and unrest arose once more. Is it any wonder, then, if people sought out their old friends, who were linked by such a tight bond, to consult with one another? 

Even if no such foresight had been taken in advance, it was only natural that people who knew each other so intimately, who had been such good friends during dangerous times, and—as the common proverb says—had shared joy and sorrow with one another, would enjoy being together. They delighted in intimate conversations, remembering those old, troubled times they had now happily survived, and in memory—*in pious remembrance* <note>original Latin: "pia recordatione"</note>, as is now frequently said—they maintained their old customs. Furthermore, the behavior of the newly initiated members during certain arduous and frightening trials they were required to undergo—the purpose of which they did not yet understand—provided the older members with much amusement and much plea-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the early development of Freemasonry following the Restoration of King Charles II. -sure <note>completing the word "pleasure" from the previous page</note>, as is the case with most people, according to what I have said previously. Finally, they wished to continue the **society** <term>Gesellschaft: here referring to the burgeoning fraternity of Freemasons</term> in which they had found so many friends and had so often enjoyed themselves, and to preserve it through the admission of new members. To that end, they had to pretend there was a **secret** <note>The author suggests that the "secret" of the Masons was, by this point, more of a social construct than a functional political or trade secret.</note> that, in essence, was no longer there. For in this way, they could hope that there would never be a lack of people who wished to join. They therefore retained the old ceremonies; and thus every member in the society learned a "secret"—that is, a matter which, because it was kept silent, people outside the society did not know. 

Admittedly, they did not explain the meaning of these ceremonies and the **images** associated with them, partly because they had not done this before—at least not immediately—but instead left the meaning of them to be guessed by the newly admitted member. They also did not explain them because there was no longer a **real-world counterpart** <note>German: "Gegenbild." This refers to the actual historical or political event that the symbol originally represented.</note> to the primary image. Had this been told to some members, they might have left the society in indignation and given it a poor reputation among others. Because of this, however, the opposite happened: since people still spoke of a continuing secret, some began to brood over them and seek things within the **symbols** <term>Sinnbilder: allegorical or symbolic representations used in ritual</term> that until now—

The author of the *Clarification on Important Subjects in Freemasonry* <note>Original German title: *Aufklärung über wichtige Gegenstände in der Freymaurerey*</note> is of the opinion (page 20 and following) that the first lodges in London were recruitment grounds for Charles II’s party against Cromwell. To achieve this purpose, the meetings had to maintain an innocent appearance. The members themselves were initially not permitted to fully know their ultimate purpose, but only to arrive at it gradually; for this reason, those in the lower classes were not permitted to know their true superiors, nor the actual opponents of Cromwell. Furthermore, all members had to be able to identify one another whenever they met, without betraying themselves to strangers. Because every citizen in London was required to belong to a guild, they assembled under the guise of a guild, and for this purpose, they chose the most mysterious one of that time: the guild of the **Word-Masons** <term>Wortmaurer: A term for operative masons who possessed the "Mason Word," a secret sign or password used for professional recognition</term> (concerning which I have already provided the passage from the author dealing with them). They invented **hieroglyphs** <note>In the 18th century, "hieroglyphs" referred to any symbolic image, emblem, or cryptic sign used to convey a hidden meaning.</note> to make the new recruits aware of the true intention without exposing themselves. To Masonry nothing was...

In essence, this conception amounts to the same as the one I have presented. However, one must object that it has not been made clear how those of Royalist sentiment could mingle among the Masons. If they had allowed themselves to be initiated by the Masons, the sudden arrival of such a large number of them would have inevitably caused a stir; this concern is removed, however, if—as I have proven—it had already long been common practice for men of status and from all walks of life to be among the Masons. Care was also taken to attract skilled master builders <term>Baumeister: the architects and lead managers of the project</term>, masons, and stonecutters by establishing a remarkable, exempt masonry <note>original: "befreyte Maurerey"; a guild granted special legal privileges and exemptions from local city taxes</note> which has its large lodge <gloss>Hütte</gloss> or "Masonry Court" next to the cathedral. It possesses statutes that are kept very secret and are guarded under the lock and key of the cathedral's master builder and several masters of the trade. This guild of privileged sculptors and master builders formed the main pillar or the highest lodge of many subordinate lodges distributed throughout Germany and Hungary. It began in 1452 (thus after the completion of the construction, as **Grandidier** explicitly adds in the letter) through the initiative of the cathedral architect at the time, **Jobst Dotzinger** of Worms. On April 25, 1459, all the masters of the subordinate lodges met in Regensburg, drafted their first statutes, recognized the current cathedral architect as the permanent head of the entire brotherhood, and resolved to hold annual provincial meetings and occasional general assemblies, which occurred in 1464 and 1469.
