<meta>This page continues the sentence from the previous page, identifying Emperor Maximilian I as the protector of the stonemasons' guild. It outlines the expansion of the guild's lodge system across Europe and its eventual legal dissolution in the early 18th century.</meta>

Maximilian <note>Emperor Maximilian I, reigned 1493–1519</note> took their society under his protection and granted them exemptions, or rather issued them a privilege, on October 3, 1494. The most recent statutes were issued on September 29, 1563, by 72 subordinate lodge masters in Basel. At that time, three "Great Lodges" <gloss>Hütten</gloss> existed—at St. Stephen’s in Vienna, in Cologne, and in Zurich—upon which many smaller ones depended, just as twenty-two lodges were dependent on the supreme lodge in Strasbourg alone. The last instance of jurisdiction exercised by the Strasbourg Supreme Lodge occurred in the year 1700, during a dispute between the lodges of Dresden and Nuremberg. However, on March 16, 1707, a decree from the Imperial Diet <term>Reichstagsschluß: a formal legal resolution passed by the assembly of the Holy Roman Empire</term> in Regensburg prohibited all lodges in Germany from maintaining connections with the one in Strasbourg <note>This was a political consequence of Strasbourg having been annexed by France in 1681; the German Empire did not want its guilds governed by a city now under French rule</note>. These free masons were not permitted to reveal anything of their laws and constitution to other masons (over whom, according to **Grandidier’s** letter, they claimed a great superiority); and to recognize one another, they had their "word-signs" <note>passwords</note>, their greetings, and their <term>Handschenken: a ritualized handshake or manual sign used to identify fellow guild members</term>. (This is likely the craftsmen's expression for when one reaches out his hand to another. This word is omitted from the *German Encyclopedia*, and therefore was presumably also missing from the various dictionaries used by the authors.) An apprentice had to a—

<meta>This page continues the critique of Philippe-André Grandidier’s theory that Freemasonry originated from the medieval Strasbourg building guilds (Bauhütten). The author compares Masonic allegories with historical records to demonstrate that the symbols and legends of the Craft likely predate or differ significantly from the specific history of the Strasbourg Cathedral.</meta>

Since the "hieroglyphs" <note>in this context, symbols or emblems used in ritual</note> in the **Apprentice** and **Fellow Craft** degrees <note>the first and second levels of Masonic initiation</note> are taken from architecture anyway, it requires no great ingenuity to apply them to the construction of the **Strasbourg tower** <note>the famous spire of the Strasbourg Cathedral</note>. Furthermore, since a master died during the laying of the foundation stone in Strasbourg, the death of the slain **Master Hiram** <note>Hiram Abiff, the central figure of the Master Mason degree, who according to legend was the architect of Solomon's Temple</note>—which constitutes the "hieroglyph" of the **Master’s degree**—will also have been interpreted in that way, or rather, forced into that interpretation. For in the ritual legend, it is three journeymen who slew Hiram to obtain a higher wage; but here, the master was slain by other masters in a quarrel arising by chance.

Yet that might pass at most, were it not for other circumstances—for example, concerning the "**Son of the Widow**" <note>a traditional title for Hiram and for Freemasons in general</note> or the "**Lost Word**" <note>a central mystery in Masonic symbolism representing a lost sacred truth</note>—which do not fit; but above all, the fact stands in opposition that a society of **Free Masons** already existed in **England** prior to the construction of the Strasbourg tower. Even if one wished to reject all other evidence, one must still accept the **Act of Parliament** from 1425 under **Henry VI**, in which—even if the word "Freemason" does not appear—mention is nonetheless made of the chapters, congregations, and general assemblies that were held annually. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of the Strasbourg Cathedral builders and their relationship to the origins of Freemasonry. ...but only received its formal organization in 1452, and in 1459 the first general assembly of the lodges was held at **Regensburg**.

The author of the frequently mentioned letters <note>likely referring to a contemporary published correspondence regarding Masonic history</note> cannot reconcile the fact that in **Grandidier’s** *Essays* <note>Philippe-André Grandidier (1752–1787), a historian who proposed that Freemasonry evolved from the Strasbourg cathedral's building guilds</note> it is said that around the time the tower's construction began in 1275, a "freed lodge" <note>a lodge granted special legal exemptions or "freedoms"</note> was established to attract skilled people, even though it later says that the supreme lodge only began in 1452 after the tower’s completion. However, I see no contradiction here. Initially, all sorts of liberties were granted to lure useful people, just as had been done in England and is still sometimes done elsewhere. The body of workers was permitted a kind of internal jurisdiction, something people in the Middle Ages were so keen on: a **privileged forum** <note>original Latin: "forum privilegiatum"; a legal right for a specific group to be tried by their own court rather than ordinary civil courts</note>, which is still highly valued everywhere today; a so-called **judgment by peers** <note>original Latin: "Judicium Parium"; the right to be tried by one’s equals</note>, which is still practiced today in England and elsewhere among certain estates, the military, and so on. And thus the "freed lodge" of the builders originally came into being. The construction lasted for nearly two centuries. In the meantime, many workers departed and returned to their homelands. --- Page 227 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical analysis of the Strasbourg builders' guilds. The previous page established that while a "freed lodge" existed from 1275 to attract labor for the cathedral tower, the formal "Supreme Lodge" was not organized until after the construction was completed in the 15th century. This page explains how that transition of power occurred through legal precedent and voluntary recognition.</meta>

...found favor; others imitated them, and in matters of importance they turned to this lodge for a decision, as they were familiar with it and knew it contained men well-versed in such matters. Through the legal opinions <note>original: "Responsa"</note> sought from it and the appeals <note>original: "Appellationen"</note> lodged with it, the lodge gradually acquired a form of jurisdiction, even over those from elsewhere. This was well-received, and so a Supreme Lodge <note>German: "Oberloge"</note> was eventually established to exercise this jurisdiction. During the construction <note>referring to the centuries-long building of the Strasbourg Cathedral</note>, there was not enough time to formally settle all of these arrangements. This was only done after its completion. Thus, this newly organized Supreme Lodge is a completely different entity than the "freed lodge" of two hundred years earlier, even though the former evolved from the latter. 

Yet this entire jurisdiction was merely precarious <note>meaning it existed only by favor or consent and had no permanent legal standing</note>; much like many magistrate courts <term>Schöppenstühle: traditional German courts where lay judges (Schöppen) provided legal rulings and advice that other towns or courts could choose to follow</term> that were once famous in Germany. Everything depended on the individual lodges voluntarily recognizing the Supreme Lodge for that purpose, and on the local rulers not interfering with the exercise of their jurisdiction. It is therefore not surprising that such an institution—especially as rulers began to take more interest in the guilds and trade associations <note>German: "Zünfte und Gilden"</note> within their territories and brought their supervision under their own territorial sovereignty <note>German: "Landeshoheit"</note>—no longer wished to take orders from outsiders, and...

**Vocabulary:**
Lodge, Supreme Lodge, Jurisdiction, Legal Opinions, Appeals, Magistrate Courts, Guilds, Trade Associations, Territorial Sovereignty

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the legal decline of the Strasbourg Supreme Lodge. The previous page explained that its jurisdiction was "precarious," relying on the voluntary cooperation of other lodges and local rulers rather than firm law.</meta>

...did not always heed even general imperial privileges if they were perceived as detrimental to their own rights, gradually declined and lost all its prestige. At most, it was only occasionally consulted for a legal opinion, much like one requests formal opinions <note>original: "Responsa"</note> from universities. These opinions, however, possess no legal force <term>Rechtskraft: the binding power of a law or judicial decision</term> on their own, but only acquire it through the formal ruling of a regular judge.

The honest old Greeting Mason <term>Grußmaurer: a term for traveling journeymen masons who identified themselves and their training through specific ritual greetings and handshakes</term>, whom the author of the *Letters Concerning Freemasonry* mentioned earlier, stated that the Greeting Masons were governed by three great Main Lodges <note>original: "Haupthütten"</note> or Supreme Lodges in Germany. However, he did not identify them by name. Perhaps they were the Main Lodges in Vienna, Cologne, and Zurich, which Grandidier <note>Philippe-André Grandidier (1752–1787), a French historian whose work linked the medieval stonemasons' guilds to the origins of modern Freemasonry</note> mentioned previously. The entire connection between the Greeting Masons and the Supreme Lodge at Strasbourg was, as Grandidier reports, dissolved by an Imperial Decree <term>Reichsschluß: a formal resolution or law passed by the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire</term> dated March 16, 1707.

Admittedly, the introduction of this decree speaks only of stonecutters, but its scope was later extended to other craftsmen, among whom the masons could also be included, even if they were not mentioned by name. Through this same decree, all other Main Lodges within the German Empire were likewise abolished, and the disputes of these crafts—

For everyone knows how difficult it is to get craftsmen to abandon their old customs.

In the year 1731, on June 28, a further Imperial Decree <term>Reichsschluß: a formal resolution passed by the Imperial Diet of the Holy Roman Empire</term> was issued to abolish abuses among the craftsmen, and yet several of these abuses still persist. In this Imperial Decree, mention is also made of "Greeters" <note>original: "Grüßern," a synonym for Greeting Masons</note> and Letter Masons <term>Briefmaurer: masons who proved their credentials through written certificates rather than oral ritual secrets</term>, as they are called—a distinction which was to be abolished—and of "Main Chests" <note>original: "Hauptladen," referring to the physical chests containing guild charters and funds, serving as the administrative headquarters</note> or so-called Main Lodges, not only of the masons but of several types of craftsmen, which were to be destroyed, abolished, and done away with by virtue of this decree.

And so the matter would have continued, at least in secret, even after the aforementioned Imperial Decree of 1731. If the perspective I have presented here—according to which **Freemasonry** <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> arose from the liberated stonemasons' guild—is correct, then other opinions fall away of their own accord. <meta>This page continues the critique of Abbé Larudan’s "Cromwell theory" of Masonic origins. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing the claim that Cromwell used the name "Freemason" as a metaphor for a new political structure.</meta>

...prove <note>completing the word "improve" from the previous page</note> [mankind], and to eradicate kings and potentates; and this he supposedly presented under the image of the rebuilding of Solomon’s Temple. However, this image does not fit the alleged purpose; Cromwell desired to improve the human race just as little as he desired to eradicate kings and potentates in general. The whole world knows what his intentions gradually became, and the name "Freemason" was known long before him.

The author goes on to relate (p. 102) that the society changed its names very often in its early years; first its members were called Freemasons, then "Nivelleurs" <note>original: "Nivelleurs"; a French term for the Levellers</note>, then people who were not subject to public order <note>original: "Policey"; in the 18th century, this term referred to the general internal administration and civil order of a state, rather than just a modern police force</note>, afterwards members of the Fifth Monarchy, and then, still under Cromwell’s reign, they were called Freemasons once more. All of this is incorrect! The "Nivelleurs," who are called Levellers or "Equalizers" in English, arose from the so-called Independents in 1647 and asserted that all men were equal, that all social ranks should be made equal, and that all property should be distributed according to strict equality; they were anything but the creatures of Cromwell. Consequently, when they began to spread throughout the army, he suppressed them with the greatest speed, [treating] some with

<meta>This page continues the refutation of the idea that Freemasonry grew out of Cromwell’s political maneuvers. It specifically addresses the confusion between secret societies and the various religious and political factions of the English Civil War era, such as the Levellers and the Fifth Monarchists.</meta>

The people who did not wish to stand under the civil order <term>Policey: in the 18th century, this referred to the general administration, social discipline, and internal order of a state, rather than just a modern police force</term> are the so-called Independents, who initially only demanded complete independence in ecclesiastical matters, but later also refused to tolerate any king. Cromwell was, of course, their leader for a time, and from 1645 onward, they formed the majority. They were the most zealous and strict Republicans, but they were not a secret society. In time, Cromwell sought to suppress them because they began to use his own principles—which he himself had previously proposed—against him.

Alongside them was the party of the Chiliasts <note>original: "Chiliasten"; from the Greek "chilioi" (thousand), referring to the belief in a literal thousand-year reign of Christ</note>, or Millenarians, who asserted that a fifth, imminent monarchy of Christ was coming to the world. Cromwell certainly supported these people because, as fanatics <note>original: "Schwärmer"; a term often used in the Enlightenment to describe religious enthusiasts or those perceived as having delusional zeal</note>, he could use them for any purpose and could use them to weaken those of Republican sentiment; from among them, he filled the Parliament as much as he could once he held the highest power. But these, too, were no secret society.

Algernon Sidney, Henry Nevill <note>the original text lists these as "Algernon, Sidney Nevill," likely a typographical error separating Sidney's first and last names</note>, John Wildman <note>original: "Martin Wildmann"; likely referring to Sir John Wildman, a prominent Leveller and conspirator</note>, and James Harrington also did not belong to this party, as the author claims (p. 103), but were instead Deists, who entirely rejected Christian revelation and strove for the highest political liberty. <meta>This page continues the author's critique of theories linking the origins of Freemasonry to Oliver Cromwell. [Crom]well was no friend to them because he could not guide them through religious fanaticism <note>original: "Schwärmerey"; a derogatory term used during the Enlightenment to describe irrational religious zeal or mystical enthusiasm</note>; he treated them harshly and called them heathens <note>original: "Heyden"</note>. (See: *General World History* by Guthrie and Gray, Vol. 13, Part 2, p. 414, and the original authors cited there <note>William Guthrie and John Gray were editors of a popular 18th-century "Universal History." This reference points to the documented history of the English Civil War</note>.) It is absurd to throw all these diverse parties into one pot—to present them all as followers of Cromwell, all as members of one and the same secret society, or as Freemasons.

These few samples of the author’s method of proof will likely be sufficient; from them, one can infer the rest. I would abuse the patience of my readers if I were to cite further examples here, especially the unfair accusations he levels against Freemasonry without any proof whatsoever. Even assuming it were correct that the Freemasons held certain maxims <term>Maximen: fundamental principles or rules of conduct</term> that Cromwell also possessed, it by no means follows that Cromwell founded the society. Many individuals before and after him have held individual maxims that Cromwell had; but his entire character, which is well enough known from history, does not suit a founder of a society such as Freemasonry is now, or was then.

<meta>This page continues the author's historical timeline, moving from the era of Cromwell to the Restoration of the English monarchy under Charles II. It focuses on the distinction between the professional stonemasons' guild and the emerging "speculative" Freemasonry.</meta>

Under King Charles II <note>Charles II (1630–1685) was restored to the English throne in 1660 following the Interregnum</note>, who came to power in 1660 and was made a Freemason during his travels—as the *Book of Constitutions* <note>original: *Constitutionsbuch*; this refers to James Anderson's 1723 "The Constitutions of the Free-Masons," which provided a semi-legendary history of the organization</note> asserts (p. 328)—the Freemason society was still a division within the stonemasons' guild <term>Maurerzunft: a professional association of "operative" masons who physically worked with stone, as opposed to "speculative" masons who joined for social or philosophical reasons</term>. Although they may have occasionally met separately, as occurred under Cromwell, they did not yet use the name "Freemason" in an exclusive sense; for this name was still applied to all members of the guild. I have already cited a passage from the *Book of Constitutions* (p. 331) as proof of this. Now I wish to strengthen this point further.

Under this King, several Lords served as Grand Masters <term>Grand Master: the presiding senior officer of a Masonic Grand Lodge or jurisdiction</term>, and Christopher Wren <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723) was one of the most highly acclaimed English architects in history</note> was Deputy Grand Master, if not from 1660, then certainly from 1663 onwards. As a famous architect following the Great Fire of London, Wren erected many buildings. In 1673, alongside the King, the Grand Master, and many other noble and distinguished men, he laid the foundation stone of the new St. Paul’s Cathedral in London, for which he had also designed the floor plan (p. 333), and whose construction he completed in 1708 (p. 345). During that time, specifically in 1679, the brotherhood was quite prestigious, and many gentlemen <note>original: "Herrn"; this marks the increasing trend of non-masons (aristocrats and intellectuals) seeking membership in the society</note> desired to be admitted into it (p. 337).

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Grand Masters of England, transitioning from the reign of Charles II to his successors, James II and William III, while focusing on the role of Sir Christopher Wren.</meta>

In the year 1685, **James II** <note>James II (1633–1701) was the last Roman Catholic monarch of England, Scotland, and Ireland; his reign was marked by religious conflict</note> ascended the throne; he was not a Freemason, and therefore the **Craft** <term>the Craft: a traditional term for Freemasonry, referring to its origins as a trade or "mystery" of stoneworking</term> was greatly neglected, and people of all ranks became occupied with other affairs during his reign. However, the Lodges elected **Christopher Wren** as Grand Master, who continued with the construction of **St. Paul’s Cathedral**, and he summoned those brothers who were able to attend to meet with him annually in order to preserve the good old customs (p. 338, 339).

Thus, the Freemasons remained primarily builders, and this continued under **William III** <note>William III (1650–1702), also known as William of Orange, took the throne following the Glorious Revolution of 1688</note>, who came to the throne in 1688 during the so-called Revolution; he was secretly made a Freemason, as **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister best known for his "Constitutions of the Free-Masons" (1723), which serves as the primary source for this historical narrative</note> asserts. He approved the election of Grand Master Wren and made use of his services for St. Paul’s and other buildings, for example at **Hampton Court**, where a substantial Lodge was held during the construction; the King also brought **Kensington Palace** to completion (Anderson does not mention the year, but it is known from elsewhere that construction was still ongoing in 1691). At that time, there were mostly **occasional lodges** <term>occasional lodges: temporary gatherings of Freemasons convened for a specific purpose, such as an initiation, rather than a permanent, chartered group</term>, some of which later became permanent Lodges. Anderson mentions seven of them by name and says that besides these, there were several others that likewise or...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Sir Christopher Wren’s role in English Freemasonry, transitioning from the construction activities under William III to the historiographical debates concerning the origins of the order.</meta>

had properly assembled. In the year 1695, a Duke <note>Charles Lennox, 1st Duke of Richmond (1672–1723), who was Master of a Lodge in Chichester</note>, who was the Master of a lodge in Chichester, was elected Grand Master when he came to London for the annual assembly and feast, and was confirmed by the King. **Wren** was Deputy Grand Master under him until he became Grand Master himself again in 1698.

Here we must pause once more. **Lessing** <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), the famous Enlightenment philosopher and author of "Ernst and Falk: Conversations for Freemasons"</note> opined that Freemasonry originated while Wren was Grand Master, and that Wren had actually organized it; he argued that Wren had modified a long-existing **Masonry** <note>original: "Masonry"</note> (a secret society) of **Knights Templar**. However, Lessing proved the existence of this Templar connection as little as he proved his claim that the name "Freemason" appeared in no printed book before this time—a claim which **Mr. Nicolai** <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a Berlin-based writer and bookseller who wrote "An Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of Knights Templar and Their Secret Interior"</note> (Essay, Vol. 1, pp. 152-161) has already refuted. On the other hand, the latter [Nicolai] believes that **Freemasonry** arose from a type of **Rosicrucian society** <term>Rosicrucianism: a spiritual and cultural movement that arose in early 17th-century Europe, claiming to possess secret wisdom passed down from ancient times</term>, which, however, as I have demonstrated, is equally unprovable. But that the Freemasonic society took on a secret political purpose after the death of **Charles I** <note>Charles I (1600–1649) was executed during the English Civil War; many theories suggest the "Craft" was used by Royalists to plot the restoration of the monarchy</note> is something Mr. Nicolai has not merely stated, but also made probable, which I have then further detailed and confirmed.

<meta>This page continues the critique of Friedrich Nicolai’s theories regarding the origins of Freemasonry. Now he continues with the history as he imagines it. <note>original: "l. c. S. 210. u. f." — referring to Friedrich Nicolai’s "Essay on the Accusations Made Against the Order of Knights Templar," page 210 and following.</note>

After the restoration of the royal dignity <note>Referring to the Stuart Restoration in 1660, when Charles II returned to the throne.</note> in 1660, the society remained together and, in a general assembly in 1663, took various measures for its preservation. However, their political business now seemed concluded, and their first primary purpose—to pursue the knowledge of nature <term>esoterically: intended for or likely to be understood by only a small number of people with specialized knowledge</term> (which Mr. Nicolai has not proven)—could no longer take place around 1680 as it had before. 

The **Royal Society of Sciences**, which followed the opposite purpose <note>The Royal Society, founded in 1660, promoted "exoteric" or public, empirical science, as opposed to the "esoteric" or secret study of nature mentioned by Nicolai.</note>, had made significant strides since its establishment in 1660. Various members of the **Freemasons** had abandoned the previous purpose or had passed away. Furthermore, **Christopher Wren** <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the famed architect of St. Paul's Cathedral.</note> was not inclined toward the esoteric method of teaching. 

If the society were to continue to exist, a change had to be made and a (new) specific purpose adopted. This was done, and it was deemed appropriate to replace **"Solomon's House"** <note>A reference to the fictional research institution described in Francis Bacon's "New Atlantis" (1627).</note> in their external symbols with the **Temple of Solomon**, by which the various parts could be more conveniently linked and interpreted more consistently. It is possible that the choice of this particular symbolic interpretation was influenced by the construction of **St. Paul's Cathedral** in London at that time; that the

If history were to allow it, one might rather claim that the Society first adopted this intention after the expulsion of **James II** <note>original: "Jacobs II."; James II (r. 1685–1688) was the last Catholic monarch of England, Scotland, and Ireland.</note>, specifically at the time when **King William III** in 1689, with the approval of Parliament, issued the famous <term>Act of Toleration</term> <note>original: "Toleranzacte"; a landmark 1689 law granting freedom of worship to Nonconformist Protestants, though not to Catholics.</note>. In this act, freedom of conscience was granted to all the various parties, with the exception of the <term>Socinians</term> <note>A Christian sect that rejected the Trinity and was often persecuted as heretical.</note> and <term>Catholics</term>. For its part, Parliament was compliant with the King in this matter because it well understood the utility and the fairness of such a policy of tolerance. It was easy to arrive at the thought that if so many parties and even <term>Quakers</term> <note>A radical Protestant movement known for its refusal to swear oaths or participate in war; they were frequently persecuted before the Act of Toleration.</note> were tolerated, it would also be fair to grant this tolerance, to a certain degree, to the Catholics as well.

And because the common populace was far too prejudiced against them, it is not impossible that fair-minded people first sought, within a secret society, to incline others toward this very principle and to prepare the ground little by little before coming forward with it publicly. The **Freemasons**—or the **Masons' Guild**—was certainly convenient for such a purpose, as it had long included people of all social ranks who, despite the great differences between its members, recognized one another as brothers, valued each other, and lived together in brotherly harmony. However, in the end, this is again nothing more than a mere possibility,

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Freemasonry, specifically addressing the claim that its ceremonies have Catholic roots. It moves from discussing the influence of the Jesuits during the reign of James II to debunking a specific legend concerning the construction of Kensington Palace.</meta>

Note: For the fact that Catholic ceremonies entered into Freemasonry <note>original: "Freymaurerey"</note> to some extent—upon which that author relies most heavily—proves nothing regarding that time period. For it is possible that such things happened afterward; and I shall show further below how that came to pass.

Regarding the construction of the palace at Kensington <note>Kensington Palace was significantly expanded starting in 1689 for William III and Mary II; this project would have required a massive workforce of specialized builders.</note>, the opportunity was also taken to invent the following legend in order to explain the origin of the Freemasons. During the construction, the builders became rebellious against their superiors, especially against the government in London. Every guild <term>Zunft: a medieval and early modern trade association that controlled training and professional standards</term> had banded together and chosen a certain word as a password <term>Losung: a secret sign or password used for identification among members during a conflict or for entry into meetings</term> for the attack. 

Those who were tasked with investigating the matter and quelling the riot had discovered the passwords of all the guilds, with the exception of the masons. <meta>This page continues the historical account of the development of the Grand Lodge of London. On March 25, 1722, the officers of twenty-four lodges gathered at the Grand Lodge. The learned brothers submitted their report, stating that they had read through *Anderson’s* manuscript—specifically the history, charges <note>original: "Pflichten"; these refer to the "Old Charges," the traditional moral and professional duties of a mason.</note>, regulations <note>original: "Einrichtungen"</note>, and the Master’s song—and had approved it after certain improvements. Whereupon the Lodge requested the Grand Master to issue an order to commit this work to the press. (p. 357.) Meanwhile, as *Anderson* continues, sensible people of all ranks and means became convinced that the purpose of the lodges was nothing other than love and friendship; they thus zealously sought to become Freemasons, preferring this peaceful brotherhood to other societies, which at that time were sometimes disturbed by heated verbal disputes. (p. 358.)

However, some disagreements arose. Most wanted to keep the Duke of *Montagu* as Grand Master for another year; yet others proposed the Duke of *Wharton* <note>Philip, Duke of Wharton (1698–1731), was a powerful but controversial nobleman; his irregular election caused a brief schism in the early Grand Lodge.</note>, who had only recently been made a brother and was not yet the Master of a lodge. <meta>This page continues the historical narrative regarding the growth of the Grand Lodge in London and shifts into a broader analysis of the transition from operative stonemasonry to speculative Freemasonry. ...ful and pleasant distraction from deep reflection or from restless business, in which not the slightest trace of intrigue or partisanship was to be found. The Grand Master <term>Großmeister: the presiding officer of a Grand Lodge</term> saw himself compelled by this to establish more new lodges. (p. 360.)

If one takes everything [discussed] so far together, the following results: However old the Freemason Society <note>original: "Freymaurergesellschaft"</note> may be, until the death of King Charles I <note>Charles I of England, executed in 1649</note>, it was nothing more than a guild <term>Zunft: a professional association or trade union</term> of actual masons who held a privilege over the remaining masons who might be in the realm and who had not bought their way into this guild; for this reason, the former were then called Freemasons. Within this guild, there were also people from other social classes, who were called accepted masons <note>original: "angenommene Maurer"; these were non-operative members who joined the guild for social, philosophical, or political reasons</note>.

Under Cromwell <note>Oliver Cromwell, who led the Commonwealth of England after the Civil War</note>, this society took on a political purpose; and it is highly probable that at that time a portion of it—a sort of inner committee—met in secret and without the knowledge of the others, and that it was precisely then that the famous hieroglyph <note>The author uses "hieroglyph" here to mean a symbolic allegory or sacred emblem</note> of the slain master <term>this refers to the Legend of Hiram Abiff, a central allegorical myth in Masonic ritual involving the architect of Solomon's Temple</term> was introduced by this inner committee, which nevertheless still adhered to the guild, using it as a shell and intending to act through it.

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical analysis of the transition of Freemasonry in England. The previous page established that under Cromwell, an "inner committee" formed within the masons' guild for secret political purposes related to the restoration of the monarchy. This page explores what happened to that committee after the monarchy was restored.</meta>

Although the political purpose vanished after Charles II <note>King Charles II, whose restoration in 1660 ended the Commonwealth period</note> ascended the throne, this inner committee <term>Ausschuß: a select group or "committee" within the larger Masonic guild</term>—whose existence no longer needed to be hidden—nonetheless remained in place. Anyone was admitted who was willing to submit to the established ceremonies, which were retained as a memorial to former times and for amusement.

This continued until the era of King George I <note>King George I of Great Britain, who reigned from 1714 to 1727, marking the beginning of the Hanoverian period</note>. It appears, however, that this inner committee did not receive much growth; at the very least, it did not separate itself from the rest of the guild. It is unprovable that during the period from Charles II to George I, after the political purpose had lapsed, another purpose—specifically the promotion of tolerance <note>The author is likely addressing later claims that Freemasonry was founded specifically to promote Enlightenment ideals like religious tolerance; he argues it was still just a social club at this point</note>—was put in its place. Aside from the guild business of the masons, the purpose was nothing more than to forge friendships and to entertain oneself in a pleasant manner when one was tired of other vexatious business, as is the purpose of all clubs so common in England.

Meanwhile, this society fell into a kind of decline because, in the absence of a Grand Master <term>Großmeister: the highest-ranking officer of a Masonic Grand Lodge</term> of noble rank
<meta>catchword: not</meta>

<summary>This page argues that after the English Restoration, the secret "inner committee" of Masons lost its political character and became a social club for friendship and amusement. The author disputes claims that the society had a philosophical mission of "tolerance" at this time, noting a period of decline due to the lack of aristocratic leadership.</summary>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Masonic society's decline following the Restoration period, specifically focusing on the leadership of Sir Christopher Wren and the organizational shift that occurred between 1716 and 1717.</meta>

...did not attract enough attention. Christopher Wren, who had mostly served as Grand Master <term>Großmeister: the presiding officer of a Masonic Grand Lodge</term> under James II, William III, and Anne, took no further interest in the society after he completed St. Paul’s Cathedral in 1708. And so, from then until the year 1717, there was essentially no Grand Master (Page 412: Wren died in February 1723 and thus witnessed the new arrangements; however, no trace is found of him having taken any part in them). Consequently, many old brothers—likely those from other social classes <note>This refers to the "accepted" or speculative masons—gentlemen and scholars—who were not professional stonemasons</note>—had withdrawn and no longer attended the meetings; thus, these for the most part sank back down into being mere guild meetings of actual masons <term>Maurer: here meaning "operative" masons who worked in the building trade</term>.

Yet, there were still people from former times among them who were pained that the guild of Free Masons—which, due to the number of members from other social classes, had previously held a certain distinction over other guilds—had fallen into decline. They united in 1716, under George I <note>King George I, the first Hanoverian monarch</note>, to remedy the situation and to elect a Grand Master from among their own, which they indeed did in 1717. Their further intention was, in time, to once again obtain a nobleman <note>The "vornehm" or noble patron was essential for the social prestige of the society</note> as Grand Master, an aim they only achieved in 1721.

<summary>This page describes the decline of the Masonic society following Christopher Wren's withdrawal in 1708 and its subsequent revitalization in 1717, which laid the foundation for modern speculative Freemasonry.</summary>
<keywords>Christopher Wren, Grand Master, St. Paul's Cathedral, 1717 reorganization, operative masons, George I, Masonic history</keywords>

--- Page 266 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the reorganization of the Masonic Grand Lodge in London, specifically focusing on the transition from a trade guild to a social society under noble leadership and the codification of its rules between 1719 and 1723.</meta>

Meanwhile, they had made some arrangements for improvement, and in particular had sought out the old <term>Constitutionen: the ancient "charges" or traditional rules that governed medieval stonemasons</term> in order to give the society an orderly and permanent constitution by comparing them and applying them to the circumstances of those times. In the year 1719, various "old brothers" <note>Former members who had drifted away during the decline mentioned on the previous page</note> appeared again at the meetings; some distinguished gentlemen also allowed themselves to be admitted <note>initiated as "Accepted" Masons rather than working tradesmen</note>. They also established several new <term>Logen: lodges; the basic organizational unit of the society</term>. In 1716, there were four of them; but when the Duke of Montagu <note>John Montagu, 2nd Duke of Montagu (1690–1749), whose election marked a turning point in the society's social prestige</note> was elected <term>Grosmeister: Grand Master</term> in 1721, there were already twelve; and the number rose so quickly that by January 17, 1723, there were already twenty-six of them.

In the years 1721 and 1722, during the Grand Mastership of the Duke of Montagu, the new laws and regulations—though partly drawn from the old constitutions—were finalized and also printed. Under his successor, on January 17, 1723, the printed material was approved once again.

Up to this point in time, from which <term>Anderson: James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), the Scottish minister and author of the first published Masonic Constitutions</term> himself dates the <term>Flor: flowering or golden age</term> of <term>Maurerey: Masonry</term>, the society was still a <term>Maurerzunft: a professional trade guild of stonemasons</term>, and the inner committee, which

<summary>This page details the rapid expansion of Freemasonry in London between 1719 and 1723, highlighting the election of the Duke of Montagu and the formal printing of the society's new Constitutions. It marks the historical pivot point where the organization transitioned from a tradesmen's guild to its modern speculative form.</summary>

By the year 1723, this distinct society was established and became increasingly visible as such from then on. ...[consider]ed, so that it must now be viewed as a distinct society existing for itself; this can be explained quite understandably. Those newcomers began various discussions that were beyond the comprehension <note>The German "Fassung" here implies the intellectual grasp or mental capacity of the working-class craftsmen.</note> of the masons, who consequently attended the meetings less frequently; for this reason, fewer and fewer masons joined this new society.

This [new society], however, insisted on these ceremonies all the more because they could thereby exclude certain people in whom they found insufficient conversation <note>The author suggests the rituals acted as a social filter to ensure the company remained intellectually or socially "refined."</note>.

emerged; and perhaps at that time people were not yet decided on how far they wanted to take the matter; they were still uncertain whether turbulent times might come, during which they would have to give an account of this society as a newly emerged one, and thus be forced to reveal what they wanted to keep secret in order to attract more and more people to themselves. Therefore, it seemed most convenient and safest to continue passing themselves off as members of the actual Masons, who had long borne the name of **Freemasons**, and thus be able to avoid further investigation and accountability regarding the **secret** <note>original: "Geheimniß"</note> of which they boasted. For the Masons had a secret of their own kind <note>In the medieval and early modern trade guilds, "secrets" usually referred to technical knowledge of geometry and architecture, as well as signs and passwords used to identify qualified craftsmen.</note>; and the Freemasons had to have a secret as well, if the desire to be admitted into this society was not to quickly grow cold.

This secret was nothing other than the customs observed during initiation and at the other meetings; which could indeed be called a secret because they were known to no one who did not belong to the society. Presumably, at the time the **separation** <note>original: "Absonderung"; the split between the trade guild and the symbolic fraternity.</note> occurred, because they wanted to retain as much as possible of **Masonry** <note>original: "Maurerey"</note>, they kept the three degrees of an **Appren-** <note>original: "Lehr-"; the text cuts off mid-word, likely meaning "Lehrling" or Apprentice, the first of the three symbolic degrees.</note>

...prentice, Fellow, and Master were introduced; whereas previously, anyone who wished to be admitted into the inner committee was likely only acquainted with the allegory of the slain master—a symbol <note>original: "Hieroglyphe"; in the 18th century, this term was often used to refer to any mystical or symbolic image, not just Egyptian writing.</note> that now belongs to the Master’s degree of current **Freemasons**. 

Now, they also incorporated various questions into this Master’s degree that were already customary among actual masons when they became masters, adding a few new ones as well. This then provided the opportunity for the Fellow’s and Apprentice’s degrees, which were also adopted, and where once again much was borrowed from the actual masons in order to maintain a resemblance to them while keeping the name. 

And so the whole thing became a mixture of Masonic ceremonies and new additions, which over time became incomprehensible because the manner of their origin had been forgotten; consequently, people sought who-knows-what kind of manifold secrets within the **compass**, **square**, **plumb-line**, and similar things, which were, after all, nothing more than mere Masonic and entirely natural symbols. <meta>This page continues the evaluation of 'Christian Rose's' history of Freemasonry, specifically responding to the third question regarding the antiquity of the society and the validity of its historical records.</meta>

3. When [did it originate? Answer:] in the most ancient of times; likely already among the Egyptian priests. However, that the Masonic secrets <note>original: *maurerischen Geheimniße*</note> stem directly from them cannot be asserted with certainty, because sufficient traces are lacking in history, and the oldest record <note>The author uses *Urkunde*, meaning a charter or foundational legal document.</note> of the Masons is not yet seven centuries old. I would be eager to see this record. Where is it located? Or where is it kept? Anderson <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister and author of *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), which serves as the basis for modern Masonic history and law.</note>, who nevertheless mentions so many old records, provides the year for none of them as to when they were composed; he also regards them merely as legends. If it is not yet seven [centuries], then it must be over six hundred years old—unless one deliberately intends to use ambiguities, according to which one could certainly say of a much younger record that it is not yet over seven hundred years old. Consequently, it would have to fall into the eleventh or twelfth century. But from this period, Anderson—or rather the entire Grand Lodge <term>Grand Lodge (Große Loge): The central governing body of Masonic lodges, specifically referring here to the Grand Lodge of England formed in 1717.</term>, in whose name he wrote, had no record. Moreover, if the Royal Art <term>Royal Art (Königliche Kunst): A symbolic term for Freemasonry, referring to the "art" of building one's character as if it were a grand structure.</term> has become a secret, it follows that it was not so originally. And thus the author should have shown for what reasons people began to make a secret of it.

<meta>This page continues the critique of a history of Freemasonry, moving from the second question regarding records to the third question concerning the official establishment of the society as an organized order.</meta>

3.) When did the Freemasons establish their own society under the name of a Club, Assembly, or Order? According to the author, the answer to this question is undisputedly a secret. However, he declares it false when the author of the *Newest Discoveries of the High Degrees of Freemasonry, Jerusalem, 1768* <note>original title: *Entdeckungen der hohen Stufen der Freymaurerey, Jerusalem, 1768*</note> cites the year 1330 as the founding year of the Order: for the Order is undoubtedly far older, and one finds it already several centuries earlier in France. At the same time, he is himself eager to know from which secret sources its originators might have drawn their knowledge, as they were likely rough, uneducated warriors. One must wonder how such men could design such an excellent plan—having the construction of our Temple <note>Refers to the Temple of Solomon, the central allegorical symbol in Masonic ritual.</note> as its foundation—and could have hidden it in hieroglyphs <term>Hieroglyphs (Hieroglyphen): In 18th-century Masonic texts, this refers to symbolic drawings, emblems, and architectural signs used to encode philosophical or moral secrets.</term> even back then. From this, the conclusion is drawn that either the sources from which they sought their secrets must be entirely lost to us, or the founders must have directly enjoyed the instruction of the Deity <note>original: *Unterrichts der Gottheit*</note>.

This is, like what was answered for the two preceding questions, mere dust <note>A reference to the German idiom "Sand in die Augen streuen," meaning to deceive or "throw dust in one's eyes."</note> thrown at good-natured people

tries to throw into their eyes. The greater antiquity of the Order in France is just as impossible to prove as the claim that it was founded in the year 1330. It is also strange to wonder where the uneducated warriors, who are said to have founded the Order, obtained their knowledge. One would first have to prove the age of the Order; one would also have to have proven that its plan and its hieroglyphs <term>Hieroglyphs (Hieroglyphen): In 18th-century Masonic texts, this refers to symbolic drawings, emblems, and architectural signs used to encode philosophical or moral secrets.</term> are so excellent. 4.) When did the society of Freemasons transform into a guild? The author answers: This has not been for as long as most writers are accustomed to claiming. Lessing's <note>Gotthold Ephraim Lessing (1729–1781), a famous German philosopher and writer whose work *Ernst und Falk* examined the nature of Freemasonry.</note> opinion—that the society of Freemasons arose from a Templar-Masonry (a secret society) which had continuously maintained itself in London and was formed by Christopher Wren <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a key figure of the Berlin Enlightenment who sought to debunk mystical Masonic myths in favor of historical facts.</note> has demonstrated that the society was already present before then and in the time of Ashmole <note>Elias Ashmole (1617–1692), an English antiquarian and one of the earliest recorded "speculative" (non-working) masons.</note>.

--- Page 279 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Masonic history, specifically addressing the transition from a trade guild of stonemasons to a speculative fraternal order. It completes the sentence from the previous page which likely ended with the word "proven" (bewiesen).</meta>

...proven. If one were to trust the talkative historians of the Order, the **Royal Art** <term>Royal Art (königliche Kunst): A traditional name for Freemasonry, suggesting it is a noble and sovereign pursuit involving self-improvement and symbolic architecture.</term> was practiced in a most disadvantageous way until the year 1663 and was held back by the civil wars. Even after that, it must have fallen into great decay again, because it only received a regular and lasting **constitution** <note>This refers to the formal organization of the Grand Lodge in London and the subsequent publication of the "Constitutions of the Free-Masons" in 1723 (often associated with the years 1717–1721).</note> in the years 1717 and 1721. It is possible that the Society of Freemasons already existed under this name in the 16th century. However, it is historically uncertain in which year the society transformed into the **Guild** of Freemasons. It is only from the year 1721 onward that the history of the Freemasons begins to become certain.

Here, the truth has forced the author into a confession that agrees with what I have proven earlier. It is correct that the society had existed long before and only then received its regular form. But it is false that its resemblance to a guild is only to be derived from this period. For that resemblance had already existed long before. The society was originally nothing other than the **guild** of actual, liberated masons <note>Refers to "operative" masons—actual stone-cutters—as opposed to "speculative" masons who joined for philosophical reasons.</note>, and since Cromwell’s times <note>Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658). Some historical theories of the 18th century claimed Freemasonry was used as a political tool during the English Civil War.</note> at least a part of it. At the aforementioned point in time, however,

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the historical transition of Freemasonry from a trade guild to a philosophical society. It completes the sentence from the previous page describing the formalization of the Order in the early 18th century.</meta>

...the beginning was made of a separate society, which however retained many of the old customs from the **guild** <note>The "Zunft" refers to the medieval trade guilds of operative stonemasons who actually built cathedrals.</note>. The author is right that only from then on does the history begin to become certain. For until then, it was the history of the actual guild, of the real and so-called free masons; but henceforth it becomes the history of the particular society which bore the name of **Freemasons** in the sense customary today <note>This marks the distinction between "operative" masonry (manual labor) and "speculative" masonry (philosophical fraternalism).</note>.

But if the same author believes (p. 12) that **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister best known for authoring the "Constitutions of the Free-Masons" in 1723, which synthesized the history and regulations of the craft.</note> could probably have had few authentic masonic documents in his hands because **Masonry** (some time before the drafting of the **Book of Constitutions**) had fallen into an inactive state: then he should have indicated where more authentic and better documents were to be found. But he does as all writers after Anderson have done who have written in favor of Masonry. He says what he considers advisable and beneficial; and that is enough! Anderson at least still gave proofs, as well as he could give them. But one waits in vain for these afterwards. If all our writers agreed with one another, it might still pass. But now one says this, the other that; they contradict each other and even refute one another. But no one proves anything!

<meta>This page continues the chronological account of the early development of the Grand Lodge of England, specifically following the publication of the Book of Constitutions in 1723. **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (1679–1739), the primary historian of early Freemasonry whose "Constitutions" established the governing rules and legendary history of the Order.</note> makes no explicit mention of the gradual separation <note>The "separation" refers to the transition of Masonry from a trade guild of manual laborers (operative) to a philosophical society of gentlemen (speculative).</note> that took place, but contents himself with describing the new arrangements that were made from time to time. On April 25, 1723, a Grand Lodge was held, in which the officers of thirty lodges appeared. On June 24 of the same year, a new **Grand Master** was appointed as usual, who was the **Earl of Dalkeith**, later the **Duke of Buccleuch**. This man appointed the frequently mentioned **Doctor Desaguliers** <note>John Theophilus Desaguliers (1683–1744), a renowned scientist and assistant to Isaac Newton, who was instrumental in the early organization of the Grand Lodge.</note> as Deputy Grand Master, and two Grand Wardens, one of whom was an **Esquire**, the other a bookseller. Also, a secretary and commissioners were appointed to keep away scoffers <note>Early Masonic meetings often attracted public curiosity and ridicule; these officials were likely early versions of the "Tyler," who guards the door of the lodge.</note>. Approximately 400 **Freemasons** appeared at the feast. On November 25, there was again a Grand Lodge; they reached an agreement on various matters for the benefit of Masonry, made some new arrangements, established a **Charity Commission** <note>The Committee of Charity, established here, became one of the most important administrative bodies of the Grand Lodge, managing funds for members in distress.</note> (p. 502), and so forth (pp. 361-364).

In the year 1724, on June 24, the **Duke of Richmond** became Grand Master. During the first procession around the table, performed after the meal, three candlesticks were carried around, as well as the **Book of Constitutions** on a cushion, with a sword carried before it. (p. 217.) The first lodge in France is said to have been established in 1725 in Paris at the house of an English innkeeper <note>original: "Speisewirth"; a tavern-keeper or caterer. Lord Derwentwater, who was beheaded in 1716 because he supported the party of the Pretender <note>The "Pretenders" were the exiled Stuart claimants to the British throne; supporting their return was a central goal of the Jacobite movement.</note>, cannot be the one meant here; nor can his son, who was only 14 years old in 1725. It was likely the Lord’s brother <note>Charles Radclyffe (1693–1746), who assumed the title of 5th Earl of Derwentwater.</note>, who was also taken prisoner in 1716 but escaped and went to France, where he became a Colonel. His brother's son died in 1732, and from that time on, he used the title of a Lord, though he was not recognized as such in England. He arrived there in 1746 with the Young Pretender <note>Charles Edward Stuart, also known as "Bonnie Prince Charlie."</note>, but was captured and likewise beheaded. Even if a man of the Derwentwater family was living in France in 1725 who called himself a Lord some time later, this report is still suspicious for another reason. If there is any truth to it, then those three Englishmen may have once held a gather- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "kunft," likely forming "Zusammenkunft" (gathering or meeting).</note>

--- Page 290 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the historical legitimacy of the "York Constitution" and the 18th-century schisms within English Freemasonry. It specifically addresses James Anderson's historical accounts and the tensions between the Grand Lodge in London and independent lodges in York.</meta>

<page-num>272</page-num>

...but it is also possible that, even if that ancient assembly were mythical <note>original: *fabelhaft*; referring to the legendary 926 AD assembly at York mentioned on the previous page</note>, the assembly at York and the so-called **York Constitution** nonetheless belonged to earlier times. **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister and key figure in the Grand Lodge of England, whose *Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723) provided the first printed history of the craft.</note> at least mentions an earlier assembly at York under **Queen Elizabeth** in 1561 and speaks of the Grand Lodge at York as a well-known and customary thing (p. 289). On the other hand, he mentions no assembly at York throughout the entire 17th century.

The Grand Lodge in London claimed that the York Constitution had been nullified by the **Restoration** 1717—referring to the restoration of <term>Freemasonry</term><note>The reorganization of the four London lodges into the first Grand Lodge in 1717.</note>, not of the royal dignity which occurred in 1660 and is simply called "the Restoration" in English history. However, the dissatisfied members raised many objections, among others, that the Grand Lodge was not entitled to arbitrarily alter the old Masonic constitution. Consequently, a separation occurred in 1739 during the <term>Grand Mastership</term> of **Lord Raymond** <note>Robert Raymond, 2nd Baron Raymond (1717–1756), who served as Grand Master of the Grand Lodge of England from 1739 to 1740.</note> (p. 101).

Anderson does not explicitly recount this because his history does not extend to the year 1739. However, in the passage shared previously, he mentions the matter in general and reports that the old lodge in the city of York, as well as other lodges outside of England, presumed independence from the Grand Mastership in London.

--- Page 291 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Restoration" of 1717 and the subsequent schisms within English masonry, specifically focusing on the transition from operative stone-cutting guilds to the speculative society we recognize today.</meta>

Meanwhile, through these disturbances <note>original: *Irrungen*; referring to the disputes between the Grand Lodge and independent masons mentioned on the previous page</note> of that time, nothing was essentially changed; rather, everything remained as it had been established in *1717* and the years immediately following, with the exception of the single point regarding dependency.

But since, according to the account by *Rose* <note>Likely referring to an author or contemporary source cited by the writer</note>, the Grand Lodge referred explicitly to the "Restoration" of *1717*, and the dissatisfied members accused it of altering the old constitution; it is clear from this (as has already been proven by other reasons) that the transformation of <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>The author uses *Freymaurerey* to distinguish the modern philosophical society from the medieval building trade.</note> began around *1717*. At that time, the first step was taken in the transition from the old <term>guild</term> <note>original: *Zunft*; a traditional trade organization of skilled craftsmen.</note> of free masons into what is now strictly called Freemasonry. 

For what occurred in the time of *Cromwell* <note>Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658); the author suggests that while secret groups or committees may have formed during the English Civil War era, they did not yet constitute a separate organization.</note>, where a narrower committee had formed, did not yet result in a formal separation. But in *1717*—or to speak more precisely, *1721*, as is evident from what preceded—the separate society of those now called Freemasons in the modern sense of the word had its beginning, and in the following years this process was continued and brought to completion. Even though no essential change occurred through the aforementioned disturbances in the years *1730* and *1739*, nonetheless, from another side and through other persons, all sorts of changes—

<summary>This page identifies the years 1717 to 1721 as the definitive period when Freemasonry transitioned from a medieval craft guild into a modern speculative society. It argues that while earlier political committees existed under Cromwell, the formal institutional break occurred in the early 18th century.</summary>
<keywords>1717 Restoration, Grand Lodge, operative vs speculative masonry, guilds, Oliver Cromwell, Masonic schisms, constitutional history</keywords>

--- Page 292 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the evolution of Freemasonry after 1717, specifically looking at the introduction of higher degrees and the scholarly investigation into the individuals behind these changes, such as Ramsay and Nicolai. It follows from the previous page's mention that the process of organizational change was "brought" about.</meta>

...[vari]ations <note>The text begins mid-word: *[Veränder]ungen*.</note> were attempted little by little. Partly, the history of <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: *Freymaurerey*</note> was told differently; partly, other purposes were sought to be achieved through it, and new, previously unknown <term>higher degrees</term> <note>In masonry, "degrees" refer to levels of membership and ritual; "higher degrees" refers to systems beyond the standard three steps of Apprentice, Companion, and Master.</note> were introduced.

It is now only a matter of finding the people who provided the first occasion for this, and the time when it occurred. The first step is the most important here: for once this had been taken, once the boundaries had been crossed, it was easy thereafter to stray further and further to the right and left of the straight line, and to devise opinions and systems that had never occurred to any Freemason before.

And here <term>Mr. Nicolai</term> <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a prominent Berlin writer and bookseller of the Enlightenment.</note> has again done some preliminary work, though only to a certain extent; for he has only given a few hints and mixed in much that does not stand up to scrutiny and can be doubted without prejudice to the main point. In the *Anecdotes for the Biography of Famous Scholars* <note>original: *Anecdoten zur Lebensgeschichte berühmter Gelehrten*, Volume 7, Part 8, Leipzig 1764.</note>, there is a short report on <term>Ramsay</term> <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), a Scottish-born author whose 1737 speech is often cited as the catalyst for the Crusader-themed "higher degrees" in Freemasonry.</note>, whose name is written as Ramsay by others, and mostly even by Mr. Nicolai himself. This report was taken by the famous <term>Mr. Büsching</term> <note>Anton Friedrich Büsching (1724–1793), a noted geographer and educator.</note> from the unpublished travel journal of the late

...visited. ...this brotherhood <note>Continuing the summary of Ramsay’s "Oration," which claimed Freemasonry originated during the Crusades</note> had joined together in the **Holy Land** during the time of the **Crusades** to rebuild the **Christian churches** ruined by the **Saracens** <note>A medieval term for Muslims, particularly those in the Levant and North Africa who fought against the Crusaders</note>. Because they were hindered in this by the barbarians in every way, and because these barbarians had even mixed among them disguised as Christians to place obstacles in their path all the more unnoticed, the brotherhood invented certain **secret signs** to distinguish the true brothers from the false ones.

And since the Christians who came to the **Holy Land** on such Crusades were for the most part very ignorant in religion, the brotherhood saw fit to ordain all kinds of **symbolic ceremonies** to instruct such a newcomer in both the doctrines of faith and the duties of life in a pleasant way and without long preaching.

With the increasing power of the **Saracens**, and since the good intentions of the brotherhood could no longer be achieved in those regions, a **King in England** <note>Ramsay’s narrative links the Crusaders to British royalty (often cited as Athelstan or Edward I in Masonic legend) to explain how the "secrets" moved from the East to the West</note> invited them to his realm. They followed this invitation and devoted themselves—alongside the planting of good morals and the promotion of the general welfare of the human race—to particularly bringing **architecture** <term>Baukunst: Literally the "art of building." joined this society and, for the stated purpose, held their usual meetings from time to time. To **Queen Elizabeth**, however, these meetings appeared to be **papist** <note>original: "papistische" — a derogatory term used in Protestant England to describe Roman Catholic practices, implying they were loyal to the Pope rather than the Crown</note> gatherings, because the ceremonies of initiation shared much in common with the rituals of the **Roman Church**. At that time, in order to remove all suspicion from the Queen, they believed themselves obligated to change the old ceremonies; however, through this alteration, a **confused mishmash** was created, and now many things occur in the ceremonies that have no rational meaning at all.

This story is a **mere fiction**. During the **Crusades**, the **Christians** could only build churches in those lands of which they were the owners; and for this, such arrangements, secret signs, and so forth were not necessary. It is improbable that **Saracens** <note>A medieval term for Muslims</note> would have mixed among them in a disguised manner to hinder them. How could they have done this in lands where the Christians were the masters? And how easily would they have been recognized by their language? ...certainly knew little, but they were familiar with the so-called **Apostles' Creed** <note>original: "apostolische Symbolum" — a concise statement of Christian faith used in the early church and baptismal rites</note>; alongside this, they had Baptism and the Lord’s Supper <note>The Eucharist or Communion</note>, and certain feast days, such as **Easter** and so forth, through which they could be instructed in the small amount of knowledge required at that time. One can easily see, however, what **Ramsay** <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), a Scottish writer who proposed that Freemasonry originated with the Crusading knights</note> is driving at; and I shall say more about this hereafter.

What sort of King of England it might have been who supposedly invited the brotherhood, Ramsay does not say; and this single circumstance already makes the entire story suspicious. It would have to have been a king who lived after or toward the end of the **Crusades**. At most, one might guess **Henry III**, who died in 1272, since under his reign Italian builders—among whom, according to **Christopher Wren** <note>The famous English architect (1632–1723); the author is referencing Wren's theory that Gothic architecture was brought to Europe by "traveling masons"</note>, there were also Greek refugees, as recounted above—are said to have traveled about and come to England. However, it has already been shown that it is not yet proven that these people ever actually came to England; and even if they had, it is certain, or at least highly probable, that **Freemasons** already existed in England before them. If the note concerning **Freemasonry** shared above from the letters regarding the life of **Christopher Wren** <note>Sir Christopher Wren (1632–1723), the architect of St. Paul's Cathedral. Masonic tradition often claimed him as a Grand Master, though historical evidence is debated.</note>, in the tenth volume of the *British Biography* <note>original: "Brittische Biographie"; likely referring to the *Biographia Britannica*, a multi-volume biographical dictionary of notable British figures.</note>, was known to **Ramsay**; then it is possible that **Ramsay** took the opportunity from it to place the origin of the **Freemasons** in the **Crusades**. For it says there: **Wren** was of the opinion that the so-called **Gothic style** was actually the **Saracenic** <note>Refers to Arab or Muslim architecture. Wren’s theory suggested that "Gothic" was a misnomer for a style brought back from the Middle East.</note>; and that the Christians had become acquainted with this in the **Holy War**, brought it back home, and refined it: Italians, Greek refugees, Frenchmen, Germans, and Flemings had supposedly established a brotherhood among themselves and called themselves **Freemasons**; they had traveled from one nation to another to build churches, and so on, as I have reported above. This idea is, of course, different from that of **Ramsay**: For according to this version, the brotherhood did not arise in the Holy Land itself, as it did with **Ramsay**, but only once the people had returned from there; and their purpose also did not concern the construction of churches in this

Masons <note>Completing the word "Freemasons" from the previous page's catchword.</note> borrowed [from the society], a claim that cannot rightly be doubted, is a question that is irrelevant here, as the cited passage was intended to prove nothing more than that **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister and a central figure in the early Grand Lodge of England.</note> alludes to the opinion concerning the origin of the **Freemasons** from the era of the **Crusades**, and their connection with the **Knights of St. John** <note>The Order of Hospitallers; a medieval military order often linked to Freemasonry in various historical myths.</note>. Once the Crusades were being discussed, the transition to the knights who had so greatly distinguished themselves therein was not so difficult. Presumably, this [connection] was first made in **France**. Everyone who wished to become a **Freemason** had to be a **gentleman**. In **English**, this means nothing more than that he must not be from the lowest class of the rabble, but rather belong to the civilized classes. Anyone in **England** who is not of the nobility, but is nonetheless an officer, a scholar, a merchant, a manufacturer, or even just a respected citizen or artisan, is a **gentleman**. The **French** translated this as *gentilhomme* <note>original: "Gentil-homme"</note>, meaning a nobleman; and thus it was easy to arrive at the thought that the **Freemasons** descended from noblemen, and, by a further leap, from such knights,

<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 290 regarding the false historical connection between Freemasonry and the medieval Crusading orders. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding a thought about gentlemen who had joined together in the Holy Land.</meta>

...of the **Holy Land** <note>original: "heiligen Landes"</note> united, who had chosen Saint John as their patron.

The matter itself requires no refutation. However, as for the circumstance regarding Saint John, the expression "Holy Lodge of Saint John" is already found in *Masonry Dissected* <note>original: "Masonry dissected"; a famous 1730 exposure of masonic rituals by Samuel Prichard.</note> from 1730 (*Freemasonry Dissected* in the **Book of Constitutions** <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch"; likely referring to a German translation or edition of James Anderson's seminal 1723 rulebook for Freemasonry.</note>, p. 551), where the first question to the **apprentice** <note>original: "Lehrling"; the first of the three degrees of craft masonry.</note> is:

> where did you come from?

to which the answer follows:

> from the Holy Lodge of Saint John.

However, this expression does not entitle us to jump to the **Knights of Saint John** <note>original: "Johanniter"; also known as the Hospitallers, a medieval military order.</note> and claim a connection between them and these knights at the time of the **Crusades** <note>original: "Kreuzzüge"</note>. The reason why Saint John was brought into play here lies elsewhere and is easy to find. In Catholic times and countries, all **guilds** <note>original: "Zünfte"</note> had a certain patron saint, and many still do. The Masons quite naturally settled on Saint John—specifically and originally the Apostle and Evangelist of that name, whose feast day was celebrated on December 27th. This day was convenient for the Masons for their assembly because it fell in the winter, when they were not held back by any work <note>Operative masons, who worked with stone, generally could not build during the freezing winter months.</note>. Their main assembly was also originally on this day. ***Anderson*** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister and a key figure in the early development of organized Freemasonry.</note> says of the Ma-

<summary>This page refutes the claim that Freemasonry originated from the Crusading Knights of St. John, arguing instead that the name stems from the practical tradition of medieval guilds choosing patron saints.</summary>
