--- Page 310 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of why Freemasons chose Saint John as their patron. It provides historical evidence from Scotland and early Grand Lodge records in London regarding the timing of their assemblies and feast days, explaining the shift from the winter feast of St. John the Evangelist to the summer feast of St. John the Baptist.</meta>

...ers in **Scotland** <note>original: "Schottland"</note>, (p. 307.) 

> "The brotherhood formerly met in monasteries during bad weather; but when the weather was fine, they gathered early in the morning on the tops of hills, especially on **St. John the Evangelist's Day** <note>December 27th.</note>."

And further on (p. 329) 

> "The **Grand Master** <note>original: "Grosmeister"</note> held a general assembly and feast on St. John's Day, December 27, 1663."

But because quarterly meetings were also held, and feast days were likewise chosen for them, they aptly settled on the **Annunciation of Mary** <note>March 25th; often called Lady Day</note>, on **Michaelmas** <note>September 29th; the feast of St. Michael the Archangel</note>, and on the day of **St. John the Baptist** <note>June 24th.</note>. On the latter, the great feast was therefore also sometimes held, because this day was more convenient due to the better weather than the day of the Evangelist. This occurred, for example, in 1666 (p. 331), 1717 (p. 347), and usually thereafter; on this day, the Grand Master was also chosen according to the rule, as is clear from the history from 1717-1738 provided above. In the general old and new regulations found in the **Book of Constitutions** <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch"; referring to James Anderson's 1723 foundational rulebook for Freemasonry</note>, which were approved in 1721 and 1722 (p. 442), it states (p. 474): 

> "The brothers of all lodges in and around **London and Westminster** <note>original: "London und Westmister"</note> shall gather for an annual meeting and feast at a convenient place or public hall."

<summary>This page explains the historical transition of Masonic feast days from the winter feast of St. John the Evangelist to the summer feast of St. John the Baptist, primarily for the sake of better weather for outdoor gatherings.</summary>
<keywords>St. John the Evangelist, St. John the Baptist, Grand Master, Book of Constitutions, London, Westminster, Scotland, feast days, Annunciation, Michaelmas</keywords>

--- Page 311 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical explanation of Masonic feast days, specifically detailing the shift in the 1720s from fixed religious holidays to more flexible dates based on the convenience of the Grand Lodge members in London.</meta>

...at a convenient place or...

This takes place on **St. John the Baptist's** or **St. John the Evangelist's Day**, as the **Grand Lodge** <note>original: "große Loge"</note> shall determine through a new regulation. For several years now, the Grand Lodge has appointed **St. John the Baptist's Day** <note>June 24th</note> for this purpose. In the new regulations, it states (p. 475): "The annual feast has been held on both **St. John's days**, as the **Grand Master** <note>original: "Großmeister"</note> has seen fit." And on November 25, 1723, it was ordained that one of the quarterly communications <term>Communicationen: formal administrative and business meetings of the Grand Lodge</term> should be held on **St. John the Evangelist's** day, and another on **St. John the Baptist's** day, in each year, whether there be a feast or banquet <term>Gasterey: an archaic term for a formal feast or hospitable gathering</term> or not; unless the Grand Master finds it detrimental to the best interests of the guild <term>Zunft: traditionally a trade guild; here used to refer to the Masonic fraternity</term>, which is of greater concern than the specific dates. Yet in recent years, because most of the distinguished brothers find themselves out of town on both St. John’s days, the Grand Master has moved the feast to such a day as seemed best to the brotherhood. It is therefore quite understandable why one or both Johns have been involved here; and for this, there is no need for any mystical explanation concerning either the Evangelist or the Baptist as the **forerunner of Christ**, of which more will be said below.

**Vocabulary:** St. John, Baptist, Evangelist, Grand Lodge, Regulation, Grand Master, Communications, Banquet, Guild, Brotherhood, Christ

<meta>This page transitions from discussing the specific feast days of St. John to exploring the historical origins of the Masonic Order, specifically the theory—popular in France—connecting the fraternity to the Crusades and the building of the Second Temple.</meta>

The idea of the origin of the Order from the times of the Crusades particularly pleased the French. It is found as early as an address by the Grand Master in France to the Lodge assembled in *Paris* in 1740, which is located in the appendix to *Anderson’s Book of Constitutions* <note>James Anderson’s 1723 work which established the modern foundations of Masonic law and history</note> in the third edition of 1762 (but not in the fourth of 1783) (pp. 127, 140). There it says:

“At the time of the Crusades, many lords and citizens united to restore the Christian temples in the Holy Land and to bring their architecture back to its first establishment. They agreed upon various old signs and figurative words, taken from the innermost parts of religion, in order to recognize one another in the presence of the infidels <note>original: "Unglaubigen"; a historical term used here to refer to non-Christians during the Crusades</note>. These were shared only with those who solemnly and frequently vowed before the altars never to reveal them. This promise was no abominable oath, but rather a bond to unite Christians of all nations into a single brotherhood. Some time later, our Order allied itself most closely with the Knights of *St. John* of *Jerusalem* <note>also known as the Knights Hospitaller, a medieval Catholic military order</note>. From that point on, our lodges all bore the name of the Lodges of *St. John*. This union occurred following the example of the Israelites when they erected the second temple, and in a

Clearly, this way of thinking—even though it contains some additions—was borrowed from Ramsay. <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), a Scottish writer whose 1737 oration is credited with introducing the "Crusader" myth into Freemasonry, shifting its perceived origins from medieval stonemasons to noble knights.</note> This is further confirmed by another passage from the same address (p. 135):

Apparently, he had the intention of giving the Freemasonry of his time a different form. But what likely motivated him to do so? Ramsay was a man of the sciences. He was displeased that Freemasonry had become a mere game. The symbols of the two lower degrees, which related solely to masonry and architecture, had now in a sense lost their meaning and their value, because the Freemasons were no longer [actual] masons, as before. For the barren moralizing, which people gradually began to derive from the <term>square<gloss>Winkelmaaß</gloss></term>, the <term>plumb level<gloss>Bleywaage</gloss></term>, and so forth—and which some people still pride themselves on today—could not satisfy a man like Ramsay. Even though it was retained—because it was already there and could serve not only for amusement but also for the pretense of a secret—it was explained even less from then on. For the first purpose had completely vanished, and for the achievement of the other two purposes it was not necessary; rather, it was in a sense disadvantageous: for if the true meaning of this *allegory* had been made known to the new Masters afterwards, it would have resulted in a contempt for the Order among most of them. It would have displeased them that something which was over and now pointless had been presented to them as an important secret. And therefore one remained silent, and let everyone guess as they saw fit.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Andrew Michael Ramsay’s vision for Freemasonry. Authors, no matter what kind of work they may be engaged in, can find refuge there. Whatever a person desires to know, they will certainly find in the collections of a Lodge, even if it cannot be found anywhere else. He need only make his request known in writing; this inquiry is then sent from one Lodge to another, and once the requested information is found, it is prepared in the form of an *excerpt*, along with the evidence and *experiments*, at no cost to the inquirer. Artists and all other types of people can see their wishes fulfilled just as easily. The Masonic archives are inexhaustible sources from which all sciences and arts can draw something for their improvement. It was highly doubtful whether the English—who regarded themselves as the founders of the Freemasons and those of other nations merely as their successors—would have even appeared. In the year 1743, France began to support the Pretender <note>The "Pretender" refers to the Jacobite claimant to the British throne, Charles Edward Stuart (Bonnie Prince Charlie), whose Catholic cause was supported by France against the Protestant House of Hanover.</note>. Ramsay died in May of this very same year and did not live to see this. However, as early as 1739, when Spain went to war with England—or at least in 1740, when Emperor Charles VI died—it was foreseeable that France would become entangled in war with England; and then there was at least a possibility that the Pretender might be used, as indeed happened later. But little can be built upon this circumstance, because it is not known in which year Ramsay composed and published his *Discourse* <note>Ramsay's "Oration" or *Discourse* (c. 1737) is one of the most influential texts in Masonic history, linking the order to the Crusades.</note>, or made the proposal for the reintroduction of Catholic ceremonies through a general convention. It is possible, however, that Ramsay had been occupied with this for many years beforehand. His 1737 "Oration" is often credited with introducing high-degree masonry and the legend of Crusader origins.</note> traveled from France to England. "

What Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a prominent Berlin bookseller and Enlightenment thinker who wrote a history of Freemasonry.</note> accuses Prichard of here, albeit falsely, he does himself. He casts insinuations out into the world which were later taken up by him and others and developed further. He does not state clearly what Prichard had done; rather, he contents himself with presenting certain expressions of his as remarkable, without explaining why they are so. He gives only hints and lets what he intends to say merely shimmer through. It is a pity that a man who has shed so much light on one part of the history of the Society, should...

<meta>This page continues the critique of Friedrich Nicolai’s interpretation of Samuel Prichard’s 1730 book "Masonry Dissected." The previous page ended by suggesting that Nicolai provided only vague hints about Prichard’s true motives.</meta>

...obscured the other part of it. From the conclusion of these words, it can finally be seen that he <note>Friedrich Nicolai.</note> wants to say that Prichard intended to use **Freemasonry** for the benefit of **Catholicism**. For he connects Prichard’s "insinuations," as he calls them, with the activities of **Ramsey** <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay, who promoted the idea that Masonry descended from Crusader knights.</note>, who was in England at roughly the same time that Prichard was writing. Ramsey, however, wanted to restore the old ceremonies as they were said to have existed in Catholic times, and thereby indirectly serve the Catholic religion and the **Pretender** <note>James Francis Edward Stuart, the "Old Pretender," who sought to reclaim the British throne for the Catholic Stuart line.</note>.

However, Mr. Nicolai is certainly mistaken about Prichard’s purpose. Prichard’s aim was to make the history of the high antiquity of the Masonic society—as contained in the first edition of the *Book of Constitutions* <note>James Anderson’s 1723 "Constitutions of the Free-Masons," which claimed a lineage for the craft stretching back to Adam and the biblical patriarchs.</note> from 1723, which could not possibly have been unknown to him—appear ridiculous. He intended to do the very same with its customs. If this can be proven, then every other reason for the publication of his work that Mr. Nicolai merely hints at falls away of its own accord; and of **Catholics** and **Jesuits**—which is what Mr. Nicolai’s argument ultimately aims at, as confirmed by the more recent disputes to be discussed in more detail below—not a trace remains.

<meta>This page continues the author’s critique of Samuel Prichard’s "Masonry Dissected" (1730). I prove this from the history that Prichard provides, and in which he intentionally makes quite large leaps. According to him, the art and the mystery (as it is called in the German translation, but more accurately the art and the guild <term>the art and mystery</term> <note>This phrase was a standard legal term for a trade or craft guild’s specialized knowledge.</note>, as Mr. Nicolai also remarks) of **Freemasonry** was first introduced at the Tower of Babel, and from there propagated by Euclid, a worthy and excellent mathematician of the Egyptians. The leap from the construction of the Tower to Euclid is large, spanning at least 1,800 years. Of Euclid, Prichard says that he communicated the art to **Hiram**, who was Grand Master in **Solomon’s** time and was active during the building of the Temple. Euclid lived after Alexander under the Egyptian king **Ptolemy Lagi** <note>Ptolemy I Soter, the son of Lagus, who ruled c. 323–282 BCE.</note> in Alexandria, about 700 years *later* than when the Solomonic Temple was built. Under the Grand Master Hiram, as Prichard continues, the most distinguished man was **Mannon Gräcus** <note>A legendary figure in Masonic "Old Charges," sometimes called Naymus Graecus, said to have brought Masonry to France.</note>, an excellent and skilled Freemason, who gave instruction in the art of Freemasonry to a certain **Charles Martel** in France. (This is how it appears in *Masonry Dissected* <note>original: "zergliederten Freymaurerey"</note>, in the *Book of Constitutions*, and in the other books that contain this history. <meta>This page continues the author’s critique of the chronological errors found in Samuel Prichard’s "Masonry Dissected" (1730). The previous page discussed the legendary figure "Mannon Graecus" and the impossible timeline linking Euclid to the building of Solomon's Temple. This page focuses on the equally absurd leap from the biblical era to the medieval Frankish ruler Charles Martel.</meta>

In Bonneville’s *The Jesuits Expelled* <note>original: "Jésuites chassés," referring to Nicolas de Bonneville’s 1788 work *Les Jésuites chassés de la maçonnerie* (The Jesuits Expelled from Masonry)</note>, as well as in the work of his translator—who really should have noticed the error—the name is written as **Charles Marcell**. In Anderson’s work (p. 250) <note>Referring to James Anderson's *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), the foundational text of modern Freemasonry.</note>, under the entry for **Charles Martel**, there appears a certain **Greek Mime** <note>original: "Mimus Graecus." This is likely a corruption of the legendary Masonic figure "Naymus Graecus," who supposedly brought the craft from the East to France.</note> who is said to have educated Charles Martel. Prichard is referring to this person, and he likely wrote the name **Mannon** simply from memory without looking it up. Neither name, however, can be found anywhere in actual history. It is likely intended to be an old tradition, of the sort that Anderson also cites here.

From Hiram in Solomon’s time to Charles Martel is once again a leap of 1,800 years. Prichard acts as if the Freemasons told their history with such massive gaps, seeking to make them appear ridiculous. For these errors would necessarily strike any reader at first glance. He intentionally says "a certain Charles Martel," as if the Freemasons had adopted a completely unknown man as their protector. He further states: this **Charles Marcell** was later elected King of France, which is false <note>Charles Martel (c. 688–741) was Duke and Prince of the Franks and Mayor of the Palace, but he never held the title of King, though he was the de facto ruler.</note>; Anderson (p. 250) had not said this either, and Prichard himself perhaps did not know better. In the time of **King Athelstan** <note>Athelstan (r. 924–939), often cited in Masonic legend as the king who granted the first charter to English Masons at York.</note>, the art reached England; and this was its first introduction into England.

...around London and Westminster a Lodge of this name appears in another street, which is called the **Bishop’s Mitre** (p. 520). And even if the first Lodge had truly been **constituted** <note>A lodge is "constituted" when it is officially recognized and granted a charter by a central authority like a Grand Lodge.</note> in the year 1691, it still could not be proven from this that a change in the society’s purpose had occurred. For this establishment might have merely concerned the external organization and governance of this society—which was scattered throughout the country—which they intended to subject, by this means, to the **Grand Lodge in London**. Perhaps an attempt was made at that time; although it remains to be seen whether **Prichard** <note>Samuel Prichard, author of the 1730 "exposure" *Masonry Dissected*.</note> was correctly informed, since **Anderson** <note>James Anderson, who compiled the *Constitutions of the Free-Masons* in 1723.</note> reports nothing remarkable from the year 1691. He does this, however, with the intention of disputing the great antiquity of the society: for if before 1721 only three lodges existed (according to the *Constitution Book* only two, because the older ones had expired or been moved to new locations), of which the oldest was only from 1691, whereas all others were from later times, then the alleged great antiquity was suspicious on both counts.

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of historical theories regarding the origins of Freemasonry. Specifically, it contrasts the views of the German Enlightenment scholar Friedrich Nicolai with those of Samuel Prichard, whose 1730 book "Masonry Dissected" is a primary source for this debate. The author is arguing that Nicolai misinterpreted Prichard’s terminology to fit his own timeline of the society's evolution.</meta>

These words seem to point Mr. Nicolai <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811) was a prominent German writer and book dealer who published a famous history of the Freemasons, theorizing that they grew out of the scientific and philosophical movements of the 17th century.</note> toward the period he has assumed; at the same time, he asserts that these words were not included there without reason. I believe the latter as well. But **Prichard** <note>Samuel Prichard, whose "exposure" of Masonic rituals was published in 1730, claimed that the "modern" form of the organization was a recent invention.</note> did not intend to allude to that period; he tells the story quite differently and knows nothing of any change in Mr. Nicolai’s sense back then—at which he presumably would have poked fun, had he known of it. Instead, he speaks of a change of an entirely different kind, according to which lodges were **constituted** <note>A lodge is "constituted" when it is formally chartered and recognized by a central authority, rather than just meeting informally.</note>, and he places this change—which he hints at more than he explicitly names—at another point in time, namely in the year 1691. 

If Mr. Nicolai, by remarking that these words are not there for nothing, intends to suggest (as it seems) that something suspicious lies hidden behind them, then he is mistaken. Prichard told the truth and could not represent the origin of the **Freemasons** in any other way, even though he disputes the great antiquity of the society. <meta>This page continues the author’s analysis of Samuel Prichard’s 1730 work and his disagreement with Friedrich Nicolai. The previous page established that Nicolai believed Prichard’s use of the term "real Masons" hinted at a specific historical shift in 1691, whereas the author argues Prichard was simply describing the evolution from manual labor to social clubs.</meta>

...people from other social classes joined them, who were then called **accepted** <note>original: "accepted"</note> Masons. From these latter individuals, who gradually outnumbered the working masons, there arose at a certain time—which Prichard does not further define—the **Freemasons** in the new sense of the word, according to which their society secretly separated itself from the guild of the former free and operative masons. Prichard calls the latter group **real Masons** <note>original: "real Maſons"</note>, meaning actual Freemasons as they are now called, who are entirely distinct from the working masons. The aforementioned catechism <note>Referring to Prichard's "Masonry Dissected," which was written in a question-and-answer format.</note> labels them, in contrast to the **operative Mason**, with the name: **Gentleman-Masons**, for which I have given the reason above. Thus, Prichard’s entire sentence is clear, correct, and harmless.

However, the fact that Prichard intended to mock becomes even more evident from what follows immediately after. After he had quite rightly said: "From the accepted Masons have sprung the real (modern) Freemasons," he then adds: "But from both come the **Gormogons** <note>The Gormogons were a satirical secret society established in 1724, likely as a parody of the Freemasons; they claimed to be older than the Masons and to have originated in China.</note>, whose Grand Master **Volgi** <note>Often referred to as "Chin-Quaw-Kee-Po-Zee," the mythical or pseudonymous head of the order.</note> derives his origin from the **Chinese**, whose writings, if they are to be believed, maintain the opinion of the **Pre-Adamites** <note>The "Pre-Adamite" theory suggested that humans existed before the biblical Adam, a controversial idea used by the Gormogons to mock the Freemasons' claims of ancient lineage.</note>...

<meta>This page continues the author's critique of Samuel Prichard’s 1730 exposé and the satirical groups of the era. It follows the discussion of the "Gormogons," a group that claimed a Chinese origin to mock the Freemasons' claims of ancient lineage.</meta>

...claimed <note>The OCR "pten" completes the German word "behaupteten" (claimed) from the previous page.</note> and consequently (this likely means: which, although it reads this way in the original,) must be even older than Freemasonry. The mockery here is unmistakable!

Against the **Dissected Freemasonry** <note>original: "zergliederte Freymaurerey", referring to Samuel Prichard's pamphlet "Masonry Dissected."</note>, a defense appeared by a non-Mason, it seems; and regarding this defense, a Brother under the assumed name **Euclid** issued a letter to **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (1679–1739), the author of the foundational "Constitutions of the Free-Masons."</note> on November 9, 1738. Both are found in the translated **Book of Constitutions** <note>original: "Constitutionsbuch"</note> (from 1762 and 1783). In the aforementioned letter, it now says (p. 618): The brotherhood had been envied for its antiquity and prestige by some who had only recently joined together in societies to imitate the Freemasons, or who had—though in vain—opposed them, such as the **Gormogons**, who soon disappeared again, and others that still persisted. 

That is all I have been able to find regarding these people. Perhaps their founder really was named **Volgi**, and he put forward that fanciful opinion to give his society a precedence over the Freemasons. Perhaps **Prichard** himself added it. For it was certainly enough to be used to make the Freemasons look ridiculous; especially if one [viewed] these people, as Pri-

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Samuel Prichard’s "Masonry Dissected" (1730) and the satirical responses to Masonic claims of ancient lineage. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, completing the name "Prichard."</meta>

—chard did, derived from them. Indeed, Locke had already made a note regarding the examination that King Henry VI is said to have transcribed: it appeared that the Masons assumed there were men in the East before Adam, who was called the first man in the West. However, the text says: **Freemasonry began with the first men in the East, who were before the first man in the West.** Not a word is found here saying that Adam was the first man in the West; rather, the sense is: before even a (single) man was in the West, there were already (many) men in the East who were already practicing **Freemasonry**; it came from the East to the West; it was not invented here, but there (See also **Preston's Explanation** <note>original: "Prestons Erklåuterung," referring to William Preston's "Illustrations of Masonry," a key masonic text.</note>, page 103).

**Prichard**, or perhaps that **Volgi**, if he is a real person, apparently knew nothing of **Locke's** letter to which that examination is attached: for I have made it probable above that it was only printed in very recent times. But that there had been scholars who asserted the existence of **Pre-Adamites** <note>The Pre-Adamite hypothesis suggests that humans existed before the biblical Adam, a theory used here to challenge the Masons' claim that their craft began with Adam.</note> was well known; and so this hypothesis could be used against the Freemasons, who boasted of their origin from Adam—who was said to have taught his sons the <term>art of building</term> <note>original: "Baukunst." In Masonic contexts, this refers to both literal architecture and the symbolic "craft" of the society.</note> so that they could establish a **lodge** (**Book of Constitutions**, p. 7).

But if **Prichard’s** work is a **satire**: how can it be credible? The

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Samuel Prichard’s "Masonry Dissected" and the theories regarding the "other system" he mentions. The previous page ended mid-sentence, suggesting Prichard wrote during a year when certain lodges had elected their own masters.</meta>

The efforts of the <term>Grand Lodge</term> <note>original: "große Loge." The central governing body of Freemasonry established in London in 1717.</note> to keep all others under its authority as much as possible, and thus to constitute a single body of which the Grand Lodge would be and remain the head—something which **Anderson** <note>James Anderson (c. 1679–1739), a Scottish minister and author of "The Constitutions of the Free-Masons" (1723).</note> by no means conceals (see the general regulations, p. 451 and elsewhere)—are what **Prichard** <note>Samuel Prichard, whose 1730 pamphlet "Masonry Dissected" claimed to reveal the secret rituals of the craft.</note> may have meant by the "other system." That he does not express himself more clearly, and does not state this quite specifically, was not necessary for his purpose. It was even useful for the suspicion he wanted to arouse that he expressed himself somewhat obscurely. For in this way, readers were led to believe that something highly criminal must be hidden beneath the matter. Elsewhere, too, **Prichard** does not always express himself clearly enough. Indeed, **Mr. Nicolai** <note>Friedrich Nicolai (1733–1811), a German Enlightenment writer and bookseller who wrote extensively on the history of secret societies.</note> himself does not!

**Prichard** mocks and calls <term>Freemasonry</term> <note>original: "Freymaurerey."</note> an old building, an old <term>fabric</term> <note>original: "Fabrick." In this context, it refers to the construction or structural integrity of the institution.</note>, and prophesies its imminent downfall, unless it be supported and repaired by a secret yet hidden at this time, by certain still unknown arts, means, and ways. Perhaps he thought here...

<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding Friedrich Nicolai’s research into Masonic history and transitions into a summary of the order’s origins. The previous page ended by suggesting that the German translator of Bonneville’s work should have noticed discrepancies between different editions of Masonic texts.</meta>

must have been; whereupon he could have easily inquired further with Mr. Nicolai.

The Order of Freemasons therefore—to summarize everything briefly—emerged from actual masons; specifically from those who possessed certain freedoms and privileges over others in England, and who were connected to one another throughout the entire kingdom to a greater or lesser degree. How old these "freed" masons <note>The author is referring to "operative" masons who were "free" of the local guild restrictions or held royal charters.</note> are, and when they began to constitute a guild <term>Zunft: a traditional association of craftsmen which controlled the practice of their craft in a particular town</term>, cannot be determined with reliability; and little depends on this fact. It is certain, however, that this guild existed long before the times of Queen Elizabeth—to whom their assembly was initially offensive <note>Elizabeth I reportedly sent an armed force to break up a Masonic meeting in York in 1561, though she later became a protector of the craft.</note>—as well as before the times of King Henry VI and the Act of Parliament of 1425. For in this Act, their annual congregations and general assemblies are mentioned as a well-known and customary matter.

Within this guild, there were also people from other social ranks, and over time, this provided the opportunity for the formation of the separate society of Freemasons <note>This refers to the transition from "operative" masonry (stone-cutting) to "speculative" masonry (philosophical and social membership).</note>. During Cromwell’s time <note>Oliver Cromwell (1599–1658), Lord Protector of the Commonwealth of England.</note>, the first foundation for this was laid, as at that time, to all appearances, a narrow—

<summary>This page outlines the historical transition of Freemasonry from a medieval English craft guild of "operative" stonemasons into a social and philosophical society. It cites the Act of Parliament of 1425 as evidence of the guild's long-standing existence before it began admitting members from other social classes during the era of Oliver Cromwell.</summary>

<keywords>Freemasonry origins, operative masonry, English guilds, Queen Elizabeth I, King Henry VI, Act of Parliament 1425, Oliver Cromwell, Friedrich Nicolai, speculative masonry</keywords>

--- Page 368 ---
<meta>This page continues the historical overview of the transition of Freemasonry from a guild-based organization to a formal society. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page's discussion of a specific committee or group formation.</meta>

...committee formed. The separation itself, however, did not take place until the years 1721 to 1723, occurring gradually. Through the Book of Constitutions <note>A reference to James Anderson's *The Constitutions of the Free-Masons* (1723), which established the rules and legendary history for the first Grand Lodge.</note> and the arrangements made at that time, this new society gained its consistency.

Afterwards, people outside of England began wanting to transform this society. Ramsey <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), a Scottish expatriate in France known as the Chevalier Ramsay. He is famous in Masonic history for his "Oration" which shifted the guild's origins from stonemasons to crusading knights.</note> was, as far as is known, the first to undertake this. He altered the history and traced the origins of the Freemasons back to the times of the Crusades. He also sought to attribute other purposes to the society: a literary one, promoting certain arts and the compilation of a universal dictionary <note>This is likely a reference to early Enlightenment efforts to organize all human knowledge, a precursor to the famous *Encyclopédie*.</note>; a religious one, by restoring the old Catholic customs as they had existed before the time of Queen Elizabeth; and a political one, by intending to act in favor of the House of Stuart <note>The deposed royal house of Scotland and England. Ramsay was a Jacobite supporter who wanted to see the Stuarts restored to the throne.</note>, particularly among the Scots, the nation to which he belonged.

I have proven all of this in the preceding sections. Ramsey died in 1743. Even during his lifetime, his ideas were adopted and expanded upon in France. Because the brotherhood, according to his claim, had [located/found] itself in the...

<meta>catchword: Cru- <note>original: "Kreuz-"</note></meta>

<summary>This page details how Freemasonry gained organizational stability in the 1720s before being reimagined by Chevalier Ramsay. Ramsay famously shifted Masonic history away from English stoneworkers toward a romanticized heritage involving the Crusades and the political interests of the House of Stuart.</summary>

--- Page 369 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Andrew Michael Ramsay’s attempts to rewrite Masonic history, moving from the previous page's description of how he linked the fraternity to the Crusades. The catchword "ge-" from the previous page likely completed a word like "gehörten" (belonged) or "gesellten" (joined), leading into the description of the Crusades.</meta>

...Crusades for the reconstruction of the Christian churches destroyed by the Saracens <note>original: "Saracenen"; a historical term used by Europeans in the Middle Ages to refer to Muslims.</note> had joined together, and because the masons in England had Saint John as their patron saint: thus, a connection between the brotherhood and the Order of Saint John <note>The Knights Hospitaller, a medieval and early modern Catholic military order. This connection is the origin of "St. John’s Masonry."</note> was fabricated.

Now people arrived at the idea that those men, who had united for the aforementioned purpose, might have been knights themselves, or at least had knights and other noble people among them. This latter possibility, if one assumed the proposed origin, was not at all unbelievable, since the restoration of churches was considered just as meritorious a work as the protection of pilgrims—the activity with which other knightly orders occupied themselves during the Crusades. The incorrect translation of the word *Gentleman* also contributed to this; and this hypothesis was accepted all the more readily because it was so flattering that all members were now supposed to be knights. 

To please the Scots—upon whom Ramsay, with regard to Catholic customs and the House of Stuart <note>The deposed royal line of Scotland and England; Ramsay hoped to use Masonry to support their restoration.</note>, likely relied the most—a new and higher degree was invented: the "Scottish" degree <note>The "Ecossais" degrees, which marked the beginning of the "High Degree" systems in Freemasonry that went beyond the original three levels of Apprentice, Fellowcraft, and Master.</note>. Nothing had been known of this degree until then, and it also found no approval in England 

<summary>This page explains how the "Knights Templar" myth of Freemasonry was invented by linking stonemasons to the Crusades and the Order of St. John. It highlights how linguistic misunderstandings of the word "Gentleman" and political motives regarding the House of Stuart led to the creation of "Scottish" high degrees.</summary>
<keywords>Crusades, Order of Saint John, Saracens, Gentleman, Andrew Michael Ramsay, House of Stuart, Scottish Degree, High Degrees, Knights Templar myth</keywords>

--- Page 370 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Andrew Michael Ramsay’s attempts to rewrite Masonic history. It tracks the shift from linking Freemasons to the Knights of St. John toward the more popular and enduring myth of descent from the Knights Templar.</meta>

...had found. He found this even more so in France, and from there it gradually spread to other countries.

Meanwhile, it was soon noticed that the alleged connection with the Knights of St. John <note>The Knights Hospitaller; the previous page explained how Ramsay tried to link Masonry to this order due to their shared patron saint, John the Baptist.</note> was unprovable and subject to many contradictions. Consequently, they looked around for another chivalric order from which the Freemasons—who were determined to have a knightly origin—could be derived. Quite naturally, they hit upon the Knights Templar <note>The Order of the Temple; a powerful medieval military order that was suppressed by the Pope and the King of France in 1312.</note>. Even their name was convenient for attributing to them the restoration of Christian churches or temples as an objective. They had been exterminated; and thus, without fear of contradiction—which might have been expected from other still-existing orders <note>Since the Knights of St. John still existed as the Knights of Malta, they could have easily refuted any false historical claims about their organization.</note>—one could derive the Knight-Masons from them and merge both into a single society. It was therefore claimed that the Knights Templar had secretly [gone] into Scotland, which one...

<meta>catchword: here</meta>

<summary>This page describes the shift in Masonic mythology from a claimed descent from the Knights Hospitaller to the Knights Templar. The author explains that the Templars were a more convenient choice for myth-making because the order was extinct and could not dispute the fabricated history.</summary>

<keywords>Freemasonry, Knights Templar, Knights of St. John, origin myths, Scotland, chivalric orders, Andrew Michael Ramsay, Knight-Masons</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of the Templar myth within Freemasonry, focusing on the role of Chevalier Ramsay’s 1737 Oration as the foundational "seed" for these legends. [here] named not without cause, continued; and that their descendants were the Freemasons, albeit in a somewhat altered form made necessary by the circumstances.

All of this seems to have followed in quite rapid succession; one step led to the next. Although it cannot be asserted with certainty that Ramsay <note>Andrew Michael Ramsay (1686–1743), also known as Chevalier Ramsay, a Scottish writer living in France who was a prominent figure in early French Freemasonry.</note> invented all of this himself and alone, there are nevertheless traces and even clear evidence that these ideas were devised during his lifetime. Ramsay, at the very least, provided the catalyst for it. For within the idea he first expressed—and which was spread through his *Oration* <note>original: "Discurs"; referring to Ramsay's famous 1737 speech to the Grand Lodge of France, which shifted the history of the craft from a guild of stonemasons to an order of chivalry.</note>—that the Freemasons originated during the Crusades in the Holy Land, the seed of all subsequent additions was already contained.

My readers may rightly demand that I [provide more detail on] that which I [have presented] here regarding the further development of Ramsay’s hypothesis...

The author suggests that while Ramsay may not have invented every detail, his ideas provided the essential foundation for later Templar-Masonic legends.</summary>
